# The rules audit's answered questions, word for word

Written 2026-10-20 (pass MN), at Mikey's word (his answer to Q52, "Q52 - B": "move each answered question word for word to docs/RULES-AUDIT-HISTORY.md, held unchanged by a hash test as CLAUDE.md's history is, and leave in its section 11 one line each with its number, your answer word for word and where its rule now lives"). docs/RULES-AUDIT.md is read by every new chat right after NEW-CHAT, and by pass MM its section 11 printed all 48 answered questions in full, about 65 KB of the file's 283 KB. Each answered question is kept below exactly as section 11 held it, with Mikey's answer and what was built, followed by the question as it stood; the answers of passes MC and MB, which carried no number, come first, as section 11 printed them. Section 11 keeps one line for each answered question, its number, Mikey's answer word for word and where its rule now lives, and the questions still open in full. tests/docs-sync.test.mjs hashes the text between the two markers below, so nothing here can be lost or edited; a question answered in a later pass moves here in that pass, in a block of its own after this one, with its own markers and digest.

<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions through pass MN, word for word: begin -->
Answered in pass MC (Mikey, opening the chat after MB, each in his words), kept here so the answers travel with the questions:

We can leave whatever is most accurate with the final product. I don't want to change anything merely to reflect what we have now. I'm thinking of the final product." So his line stays as he wrote it, and program item L1 (a long story for every course) makes it true. (The line is the Elective stories line in a student's report summary.)
- How an elective read is counted (Practice again counts, twenty seconds for any length, a refresh does not count, a game opened and closed counts as a break): "Yes keep those".
- Ages in long stories (33 of 105 stated one): "I think we should have half of that number for the 105 stories and keep a similar ratio throughout all of the grades/long stories". Now 16 of 105, held by the stories test to one in six, never two in a row, every chapter of six or more with at least one and at most one in five, one in six module stories with the core cast, and no about line (section 3).
- Leonardo's model: "Yeah, let's fold into the prompt" (Nano Banana 2). tools/prompts-csv.mjs folds the bans into the prompt as positive sentences (section 5).
- Backup in the claude.ai preview: "Keep as is". Backups are tested on the standalone page.
- Games on day one: "I think that's a good setup as long as the rest of the courses unlock a new game." The Listen and Point exception stays, and every other course keeps a game to unlock (section 7).

- Q16. Answered in pass MD (Mikey: "For your question about stories for early learners (answer to question 4).   They can play after the lesson as a reward like you mentioned."): the story is the reward of the first passed round of a lesson, read aloud by itself, and then waits in an early learner's Let's Read (section 3). "After the lesson" was read as after the passed round, so a child who misses the round goes back through the spoken lesson without the story (Q20 asks about that). The question as it stood: Pre-readers' lessons (pass MB, part one; Mikey in pass MC: "What are your thoughts on this?"): a pre-reader's lesson never offers its module story, while a reader's lesson offers View story. Pass MC found it is worse than the question said: Let's Read holds only a course's long story, and My Stories lists only stories already read, so the 196 module stories of the 25 read-aloud courses (pre-K 3 to grade 2) reach a child only when an educator opens them in the Story Log or prints the book. Claude's recommendation, not built: on a lesson's first visit, the story follows the spoken lesson and is read aloud at the course's pace (slow for pre-K), then practice begins, since a story told right after the lesson is one more meeting with its ideas before the questions; on later visits practice comes straight after the lesson, with a book button beside the star to hear the story again; and an early learner's Let's Read lists the module stories already heard. The cost (the alignment read): on a first visit a three-year-old would hear the whole lesson (A and B is 11 lines), then the story read slowly (about two minutes), before the first question; the story screen would need a pre-reader form that reads itself, with one big arrow and nothing to read; and readers meet the story on every visit, pre-readers once. It could come after the round instead, as a reward. Before practice, after the round, or only the book button?

The question as it stood: One story at a time (pass MD, the screen check): when the passed round of a course's last lesson earns that lesson's story, the result shows only the book, and the course's long story waits, haloed, in Let's Read, so a child is never offered two stories at once. Claude's recommendation (the alignment read): chain the long story after the reward story's arrow, read aloud the same way, or at least give its Let's Read row a gold book that reads it. Keep it as built, chain it, or the gold book?

- Q23. Answered in pass MG (Mikey: "Q23 - change"): the drawings on the gold and green taps take the dark ink in the dark theme only (C.onAccent: 7.93 to 1 on the gold and 8.67 on the green), and the light theme keeps its white (section 8). - Q27. Answered in pass MH (Mikey: "Q27 - whatever you think is most visually appealing from a modern and unique designer perspective"): the light theme matches the dark theme in all five: with motion off the strips show the whole rainbow at once, with a lighter purple (#9A7BC4) under their words; the breathing ring stays on, still; the sparkles hide; and the Let's Wonder! title runs from its deep green to the deeper gold #A67A26, moving or still, and shows its letters from 700 pixels wide; the still strips' arrow takes #24291F in both themes (section 8). The question as it stood: The light theme on a device that asks for less motion, and on wide screens (pass MG, from Mikey's note on the gold dropdowns, widened by the screen check and its second read). Where: a child's courses page and the Wonder question screen, in the light theme, the one chosen with the sun on the welcome page or at the top of the Classroom page; the app opens in the dark theme, where all five things below are fixed, and Mikey's own two screenshots are in the dark theme, so the strips he saw should now show their whole rainbow. Background: a phone or tablet can ask apps for less motion (on Android, Settings, Accessibility, Remove animations; newer Samsung phones call it Reduce animations). Mikey's phone most likely has it on: his screenshot of the course-finished page shows the green ring around the big gold book standing still, the way the app draws that ring when motion is off (with motion on it ripples outward and fades). With it on, anything that normally moves stops where it starts, and in the light theme four things stop in a poor place. First, the three colorful strips, Let's Color, Let's Read and Let's Play, whose rainbow drifts from color to color, stop on their first color, the gold end, so all three look gold; in the dark theme each now shows its whole rainbow at once, still, from gold through orange, coral, purple, blue and green, and back to gold. Second, the soft green ring that breathes around what a young learner can tap (each course group with something ready, the shelf's gold books, the coloring and game tiles; a different ring from the one around the big gold book on the course-finished page) disappears, because its resting moment has no ring; in the dark theme it now stays on, still. Third, the white sparkles that cross each strip sit still as three white dots, one at the strip's left end and two beside its little arrow; in the dark theme they are hidden. Fourth, the shimmering "Let's Wonder!" title at the top of every Wonder question freezes with the end of "Wonder!" in its gold, about 2.4 to 1 on a pre-reader's question and 2.0 at the exclamation mark on a reader's, where words that large need 3; in the dark theme the title now runs from mint to gold, 7.3 to 1 or better, moving or still. Fifth, on a screen 700 pixels wide or more (a tablet or a laptop), the light theme's "Let's Wonder!" title has shown nothing since September 2026, with motion on or off: the plain backing a wide screen puts behind loose words also wipes out the title's colors, so an empty strip sits at the top of every Wonder question; pass MG fixed it in the dark theme. The light theme is frozen at Mikey's word since September 2026, so none of these changed there. What matching would mean: for the strips, the light rainbow's middle is a deep purple, and the strip's dark words would sit on it at 2.78 to 1, the dip Mikey chose to keep in Q12, so the change would also put a lighter purple under the words; for the title, the dark theme's mint reads only 1.90 to 1 on the light page, so the light title would keep its deep green and take a deeper gold, for example #A67A26, 3.58 to 1 or better along the whole word. Should the light theme match the dark theme for all five, or stay as it is? Claude recommends matching all five, with the lighter purple and the deeper gold, so a child on any device sees the same courses page and the same Wonder title in both themes.
- Q28. Answered in pass MH (Mikey: "Q28 - keep"): Savanah's and Georgette's paths stand as pass MG wrote them (docs/CHARACTERS.md). The question as it stood: Savanah's and Georgette's working lives, as pass MG wrote them from Mikey's notes ("Savanah isn't always CTO.  She gets there through hard work and starts in customer service."; "Georgette works up to history teacher through her fascination with history"), for Mikey to correct. Each story named here is the long story a student opens on finishing that course, from grade 3 to college. Savanah, in the order of her life: she grows up selling eggs on the farm and saving a third of every Saturday's profit in a jar marked college (econ-3, grade 3), works two summers as a junior camp counselor, at seventeen and eighteen (health-6 and philosophy-6, grade 6), and leaves the farm for the city with a print shop job and pays for a first year of community college from the jar marked college (econ-6, grade 6); her first job at a company that builds ultrasound machines is in customer service, answering calls from clinics, and on Thursdays she goes along with the company's trainer to a clinic with a new machine, where the cardiologist asks the mother whether both visitors may stay and Savanah watches a baby's heart on the screen (sexual-health-6, grade 6, where until this pass she was the company's chief technology officer); in her mid-twenties she manages a clinic's front office, in a story that now opens "Long before she became a chief technology officer" (psychology-9, grade 9); at twenty-seven she starts an engineering program at a community college, remembering the two signs she once tested in the clinic's waiting room (psychology-college); she transfers to a university to finish her engineering degree and comes back to the company as an engineer; and she works her way up until, in the adult chapter, she is its chief technology officer. One story still breaks the path: speech-3 (grade 3) shows her grown at the company while that chapter has her a teenager on the farm, and EARMARKS A14 moves it when the review reaches that course. Georgette: history has fascinated her since her grandmother's stories; as a young woman she writes a piece of the town history, and history catches her for good (reading-12, grade 12); she studies for a degree at a university (reading-college); she works her way up from teacher's aide, earning her teaching license along the way, to a history classroom of her own (philosophy-3, grade 3), where she teaches for forty years, mostly sixth-graders; and in retirement she runs library clubs and leads museum tours as a docent. Is that the path you meant? For example, should Savanah's customer service job be somewhere else, should Georgette's years as an aide come before her degree rather than after it, or should a later story show Savanah as the chief technology officer? Claude recommends keeping these paths, since each step is one a real person can take and every story but speech-3 already agrees with it, and letting the grade 9 to 12 and college stories show her as the company's chief when the review reaches them.
- Q29. Answered in pass MH (Mikey: "Q29 - your discretion"): the light theme's speaker drawing and the stop square on the same buttons take #A67A26, 3.25 to 1 on the cream; the dark theme is unchanged (section 8). The question as it stood: The speaker button's drawing in the light theme (pass MG, the screen check). Where: the round speaker button a child taps to hear the words again, on a pre-reader's lesson, the result, the course-finished page, the Wonder screens and the story pages that read themselves aloud (where the same gold draws its stop square), in the light theme. What it looks like: a cream circle with a gold border and a gold loudspeaker drawn inside. The gold drawing on the cream reads 2.72 to 1, under the 3 to 1 that a drawing a child must recognize needs, the same rule as Q23; in the dark theme, where the app opens, it reads 5.36 to 1, so nothing there needs changing. The light theme is frozen, so nothing changed. The fix would draw the loudspeaker a shade deeper (#A67A26, the gold the dark theme already uses for lit letters), 3.25 to 1, with the circle and its border as they are, and the stop square with it; a child would see the same button with its drawing a little darker. Change it in the light theme, or keep it? Claude recommends the change, since a child who cannot read finds this button by its drawing alone.
- Q30. Answered in pass MI (Mikey: "Q30 - I agree with you"): the setup line stays above its question, as pass MH put it (the order the voice reads and Quick fire shows), and a setup and its question are merged where one sentence reads better as each grade is reviewed (EARMARKS L11). The question as it stood: The setup line above the question (pass MH, the screen check). Where: the practice screen, on every question that comes with a setup line, a short scene, sentence or list shown with the question, in every grade from pre-K 3 up: a reader reads it there, and in the read-aloud courses (pre-K to grade 1 and two grade 2 courses) the voice says it first while the grown-up beside the child sees it (pre-K 3's 37 situation questions, in seven kinds, are now one sentence). What a child sees today: since pass MH the setup line sits above the question, the order the voice already reads it and Quick fire already shows it; before, the question came first and the setup line under it, the order pass IA chose with Mikey on October 2 ("a prompt must make sense read first", so a prompt points at its setup with this). An example from grade 3's Fact or Opinion: today "Rainy days are the worst." and under it "Is this a fact or an opinion?"; before, "Is this a fact or an opinion?" and under it "Rainy days are the worst." Keeping it above reads the way a person tells it, the scene and then the question about it; going back puts the question first, as before. Where one sentence reads better ("What is a kind thing to do when your friend looks sad?"), the two are merged as each grade is reviewed (EARMARKS L11) either way. Keep the setup line above, or put it back under? Claude recommends keeping it above, since a child reads the scene first and the voice already says it that way.
- Q31. Answered in pass MI (Mikey: "Q31 - I agree with you"): the twelve four-color reasons stay held back after a wrong tap, and each waits in More Info after the right tap. The question as it stood: The twelve reasons a pre-reader does not hear (pass MH, the lesson check's second read). Where: pre-K 3's "What color is this?" with four color choices (p3-which-colour-four), for a child who cannot read yet, after a wrong tap. Since pass MH a pre-reader hears why a wrong choice does not fit, then "Have another try.", for every reason but twelve. In those twelve the reason would name the answer while two or more choices are still on the screen to try: shown a yellow square, a child who taps green would hear "Green is the color of the grass, and this one is yellow like a banana.", which answers the retry for the child; so the child hears "Not that one. Have another try.", and the reason waits in More Info after the right tap. Saying them would give the child the answer by ear; holding them back keeps the retry the child's own. Hold the twelve back, or say them too? Claude recommends holding them back, since the retry is where the child shows what they know, and More Info says it all after the right tap.
- Q32. Answered in pass MI (Mikey: "Q32 - I agree with you"): the lit sentence takes the light green (#AAD8C5, words #16201B) in the light theme too, one light in both themes (section 8). The question as it stood: The light theme's lit sentence (pass MH, the screen check). Where: a story, a pre-reader's lesson line or a Wonder choice being read aloud, in the light theme (the one chosen with the sun on the welcome page or at the top of the Classroom page). Since pass MH the sentence being read is lit; in the dark theme it sits on the light green you asked for (#AAD8C5, the words on it 10.6 to 1), while the light theme, frozen at your word, keeps its pale gold tint, which barely shows: 1.12 to 1 against the story card and 1.19 on a lesson's white, so a child can lose the light. This pass caused it: before your note at 2:03 ("I meant just the words (how they were before)") a story lit its whole paragraph, where the same pale gold showed plainly on a block that size, and at the size of a sentence it barely shows. Taking the same light green in the light theme would read 1.57 to 1 against white, the words on it 9.45 to 1, and look the same in both themes; a deeper gold is another way; and the light theme could light the whole paragraph again, as a story did before this pass, while the dark theme keeps the sentence. Use the light green in the light theme too, a deeper gold, the paragraph again in the light theme, or keep it as it is? Claude recommends the light green, so the light is easy to follow and the same in both themes.
- Q33. Answered in pass MI (Mikey: "Q33 - If you are talking about the Wonder Question portraits, flat is okay.  We can make a prompt for the flat version of the 3D character so it still matches in a way."): U2 to U5 ask for a flat, outlined portrait of the same person as the 3D character sheet attached to it, each prompt opening by naming what its sheet is for (section 5). A 3D portrait matches the story pictures of the same people; a flat one reads more clearly at 64 pixels and matches the pondering pictures and the stand-ins on the same card. Paint them in 3D as written, or rewrite the four prompts in a flat, outlined style? Claude leans flat (the picture check too), since the portraits are small and sit beside line art.
- Q34. Answered in pass MI (Mikey: "Q34 - I agree with you"): Frederick's portrait shows him in his late thirties, his short hair and large handlebar mustache gone gray early, his face smooth and his eyebrows still dark (U2, docs/CHARACTERS.md). U2 asks for "an older man, a professor ... short gray hair", following his character sheet C6 and docs/CHARACTERS.md ("As an old man"), but the story clock makes Frederick the same age as Mike and Chloe, whose portraits ask for people in their thirties, and Georgette, about fifty years older than all three, would sit beside him looking younger, with her auburn hair. Keep him older (your reference scientist is an older man), or ask for "a man in his late thirties, a professor, whose short hair and large handlebar mustache have gone gray early"? Claude recommends the late thirties with the gray coming early, so the four look like the friends the stories make them.
- Q35. Answered in pass MI (Mikey: "Q35 - I agree with you"): the drawn stand-ins follow the character sheets, and Georgette's face carries her years so she reads as the elder (section 5). The question as it stood: The stand-ins against the cast's looks (pass MH, the picture check). Where: the four drawn portraits a Wonder card shows until the paintings arrive. They borrow from your reference pictures where docs/CHARACTERS.md and the character sheets differ: Georgette in round glasses (her sheet has none), Mike in an orange tee (his sheet wears dark rolled-sleeve shirts), Frederick with wild white hair (his sheet, short gray hair and a handlebar mustache) and Chloe in a beret. The stand-ins showed one effect of that mix on every pre-reader's card: Frederick white-haired beside Georgette with auburn hair and no lines, so the Grandparent of Faith looked younger than the Scientist, though your reference grandparent has gray hair in a bun (the alignment read); until you answer, Georgette's stand-in has a silver streak at her temple and two smile lines, her auburn hair kept from docs/CHARACTERS.md. Should the stand-ins follow the character sheets, so they match the Leonardo portraits and every story picture of the same people, or do your references change the cast's looks, in which case the portrait prompts and the character notes change with them? Claude recommends the character sheets, since the stand-ins are temporary and the paintings must match the stories.
- Q36. Answered in pass MI (Mikey: "Q36 - words when the recordings arrive."): the sentence stays lit for now, and the light moves word by word once the recorded clips arrive, from ElevenLabs' timing data (EARMARKS L12). The question as it stood: A light that moves word by word (pass MH, from Mikey's note at 2:03: "I meant just the words (how they were before)"). Where: a story, a pre-reader's lesson line or a Wonder choice being read aloud. Today the light marks the sentence the voice is saying, as a lesson line did before this pass (a story used to light its whole paragraph). A light that moves word by word needs the time each word is spoken: the voice built into the browser reports it on some devices and not others, and the recorded clips would need ElevenLabs' timing data, which its timestamps option gives for every character. Keep the sentence everywhere; move word by word once the recorded clips arrive, with the sentence wherever no timing exists; or move word by word now on devices whose voice reports each word (some do and some do not, so Claude would test the phone and the laptop you use first), with the sentence elsewhere and the clips' timings when they arrive? Claude recommends the sentence now and word by word with the recorded clips, which carry the timing.
- Q37. Answered in pass MI (Mikey: "Q37 - celebrate"): a quick check that places a course's last lesson not yet passed celebrates as a round does, with Celebrate! on its result and the course's long story (section 7). The question as it stood: A course finished by a quick check (pass MH, the alignment read). Where: a reader's "I already know this" quick check, the five questions that place a lesson without its lesson, offered in 86 of the 122 courses: grade 2's math and reading courses and every course from grade 3 up but Writing, since the read-aloud courses have none. What a reader sees today: when the quick check places the last lesson of a course the reader had not yet passed, no Celebrate! comes for the course, and its long story opens quietly on Let's Read. For example, a grade 3 reader who has passed four of the five Fractions lessons and places the fifth with the quick check goes back to the courses page with no party, and finds the long story on the shelf later. Your words were "Even for early readers, they should be launched into the long story at the end of a completed course (both 1st and 2nd time on different days)"; this pass held that placing is not doing every lesson, so the round that celebrates is a round of practice. Celebrate from the quick check's result too, with Celebrate! beside its buttons as after a round, or keep it quiet? Claude recommends celebrating, since the reader has shown every lesson (the check asks five questions and passes at four, as a round does through grade 5), and the long story is the reward either way.
- Q38. Answered in pass MI (Mikey: "Q38 - Inside each review"): each course's next long story is written inside that course's review, a grade at a time as the sweep reaches it (EARMARKS L9). The question as it stood: When the next long stories are written (pass MH, the alignment read). Where: the second long story a course opens when its last star is earned on another day (EARMARKS L9). Today pre-K 3's two courses have theirs (The rainy-day picnic and The ball over the wall); 103 courses open their first long story again at mastery, and 17 younger courses have no long story yet at all, so they need their first as well (EARMARKS L1). The next stories alone are 120 stories of 400 to 460 words, some 50,000 words and 720 paintings; with those 17 first stories, 137 stories, nearly 60,000 words and 822 paintings. They could be written inside each course's review, a grade at a time as the sweep reaches it (pre-K 4 next), so each story retells its course's lessons as they were just rewritten and the sweep carries L9, L10 and L11 together; or as a program of their own, a batch of courses each pass beside the sweep, so children meet them sooner, though before their courses' lessons are read again, and a later rewrite of a lesson may mean a fix to its story. Inside each review, or a program of their own? Claude recommends inside each review, since a long story retells its course's lessons and should follow their final words, and the sweep's passes stay whole.
- Q39. Answered in pass MK (Mikey: "Q39 - both"): an older student starts the space shooter further up its speed ramp, five chips a grade above grade 2 and twenty from grade 6, by the oldest grade among the student's courses (shipStartFor, topGrade), so a grade 6 student's first chips fall in about six seconds where grade 2's take about ten; and a lost ship comes back on every tenth fitting chip hit, never past three (SHIP_BACK_EVERY), a note on the board saying "Ten more hits, and a ship came back!" (section 7). The question as it stood: The space shooter's difficulty for older students (pass MI, the game check). Where: Let's Play's Space Shooter games, Even Numbers from grade 2, Gases from grade 3 and Elements from grade 6, for a student who has unlocked one. What a student sees today: since pass MI the game runs until three ships are lost, every falling word or number a little faster than the one before; every grade starts at the same slow speed, about ten seconds for the first ones to fall, so a grade 6 student waits through the slow part, and a ship lost early is gone for the rest of the game. The game check suggested two changes: older grades start further up the speed ramp (grade 6 starting where grade 2 is after about twenty words or numbers have fallen, about six seconds a fall instead of ten, for example), and a ship comes back every second wave (every ten hits), so a game is decided more by speed than by one early slip. Keep it as it is, start older grades faster, give a ship back every second wave, or both? Claude recommends both, since the faster start spares an older student the slow part, and a ship won back rewards the long run the best score measures.
- Q40. Answered in pass MK (Mikey: "Q40 - your recommendation"): the slower voice stays in More Info alone, at 0.85 for the read-aloud courses, as pass MI built it, and is looked at again when the recorded clips arrive, which set their own pace. The question as it stood: A slower voice for early learners everywhere? (pass MI, from Mikey's More Info note). Where: everything read aloud to a child who cannot read yet, in the read-aloud courses (pre-K to grade 1 and two grade 2 courses): lessons, questions, stories, Wonder questions and More Info. Your note asked that More Info's reading "should be a little slower too for early learners", and since pass MI More Info reads at 0.85 of its usual pace for them, with a pause between explanations. Everything else keeps its own pace: a pre-K story starts on the snail, already slower, with the hare to speed it up, and a question is said at its usual pace (until pass MI a story's snail stayed on after the story and slowed the questions after it; it no longer does). Example: "What color is this?" is said as before, while More Info's "Red is the color of a strawberry, and this one is blue like the sky." is now a little slower. Keep the slower voice to More Info, or slow every early-learner reading by the same amount? Claude recommends More Info only for now, since the stories already have the snail and the hare and a slower question drags for a child waiting to tap, and to look again when the recorded clips arrive, which set their own pace.
- Q41. Answered in pass MK (Mikey: "Q41 - we can remove the restriction and you can choose what makes sense as you write considering the general chronological order.  This gives you more freedom and flexibility with the stories."): docs/CHARACTERS.md's rule 4 is lifted; a story brings in as many of the core cast as it needs, in keeping with the general chronological order of the cast's clock, so pre-K 3's four long stories stand as written (section 3). The question as it stood: Core characters who meet in one story (pass MJ, the story check and the alignment read). Where: pre-K 3's four long stories, which a child hears once every lesson of a course is passed and again at its second celebration: Very First Steps' The mixed-up picnic and The rainy-day picnic, where Chloe, Mike and Frederick picnic with their teacher, Miss Hazel, and Listen and Point's The ball that rolled away and The ball over the wall, where Mike goes to the park with Georgette, a grown-up in her fifties. What the rule says today: docs/CHARACTERS.md's rule 4 reads "Introduce at most one new core character per story, and never all of them at once.", so a child meets the cast one at a time, "the way you meet neighbors". Who is new depends on which course a child finishes first. Finishing Listen and Point first (six lessons, against Very First Steps' fifteen), a child meets Mike and Georgette together ("Mike was three, and his favorite thing was his red ball ... he took it to the park with Georgette"), then Chloe and Frederick together at the picnic. Finishing Very First Steps first, a child meets three at once ("Chloe, Mike and Frederick were going on a picnic in the park with their teacher, Miss Hazel."), then Georgette alone. Keep: nothing changes on screen, and rule 4 gains one written exception for pre-K 3's first stories, the classmates' picnic and Mike's outing with Georgette; every later story still brings in one new character at most. Rewrite: four long stories and their 24 pictures change, since the second stories call back to the first (the rain socks, Frederick's bug, "the one that once rolled down the hill"), for example Chloe alone at the first picnic, with Mike and Frederick arriving in later stories. Claude recommends Keep: they are three-year-old classmates and a grown-up friend, a class picnic is how pre-K friends meet, and the stories and pictures are written and checked.
- Q42. Answered in pass ML (Mikey: "Q42 - relax it for scene words only, from grade 3 up."): a picture for grade 3 or older may carry one or two short words that belong to its scene (a street sign, a shop's name, a book's cover, a jersey), quoted in its prompt and spelled out letter by letter in its check note, its bans saying "text other than the scene words", and the spreadsheet folds them into "The only writing in the picture is ..."; a word that teaches or that a question asks for (a letter in a reading lesson, a numeral, a count, a clock, a label on a diagram, a map's names) stays drawn by the app; pre-K to grade 2 keep the rule whole, and their EARMARKS A6 pictures are planned around things; S2844, grade 9's storefront lender, reads "CASH TODAY", the first (section 5; the ledger test holds it). The question as it stood: Words inside the paintings, now that the models write them well (pass MK, from your note: "Now that we know AI is decent at creating accurate text, do you think we should edit our rules to be more lax?"). Where: every Leonardo picture, the story scenes, lesson paintings and coloring pages, about 4,800 rows in the spreadsheet. What the rule says today: no text of any kind in a picture (docs/LEONARDO-BRIEF.md, rule 1); anything that needs writing is drawn by the app, and 169 early-years pictures that lean on drawn letters, words or numerals wait to be re-planned (EARMARKS A6), with several hundred more from grade 3 up. What the model can do now: Nano Banana 2.1 writes a short, large word well, while its own model card says small text and long paragraphs are still weak, and Leonardo's model list now has models made for text too (Ideogram 4.5, Seedream 5.0 Pro, Recraft V4, FLUX 3 Image). Example: grade 9's story Fifteen Dollars per Hundred (the True Cost of Credit lesson in Money for a Life) has a picture of "a young woman outside a storefront lender with a cash today sign" (S2844); under today's rule the sign would be painted with no words, and with the rule relaxed it could read CASH TODAY, checked letter by letter before it is saved. Keep the rule as it is; relax it for a few large words that belong to a scene (a street sign, a shop's name, a book's cover, a jersey), each spelled out in the row's check note; or relax it everywhere? Claude recommends relaxing it for scene words only, from grade 3 up: a word that teaches, or that a question asks for (a letter in a reading lesson, a numeral, a count, a clock, a label on a diagram, a map's names), stays drawn by the app, where it is always right, can be read aloud and changes without repainting, while a scene word painted wrong costs only a retry. Pre-K to grade 2 keep the rule, since their lessons teach letters by their shapes to children learning to read, and a painted letter a little wrong would show the wrong shape; their A6 pictures are re-planned around things, as A6 says, and the rows from grade 3 up around their scene words.
- Q43. Answered in pass ML (Mikey: "Q43 - after the review, paint the picture-like ones (the object pictures, the animals and the faces) as their own set."): after the standards review the hundred object pictures IconPic draws, the twelve sign-in animals and the four feeling faces are painted as a set of their own with I serials, a dozen tried first at the size a question card shows them; whatever carries an exact count, size, length, time, letter or label a question depends on stays drawn; each painting shows every sign a lesson line, a question or a More Info reason names for it, written into its check note; and each drawing stays until its painting arrives (EARMARKS L15, section 5). The question as it stood: Paintings for the pictures the app draws (pass MK, from your note: "Should we consider also generating images for your drawn-in images for greater consistency?  If so, you can leave the drawn in images for now until I can submit the new ones."). Where: the pictures the app draws in code beside the paintings: about a hundred object pictures a lesson line or a question shows (a sun, a cup, a fish, a ball; IconPic in src/ui.jsx), the twelve sign-in animals, the four feeling faces, and the game boards' pieces. What a child will see once the paintings are made (today a story shows a placeholder until its painting arrives): a flat drawing of a cup on a question card, and a 3D painting of a cup in the story the same lesson opens, two looks for one thing. Paint them all; paint only the picture-like ones; or keep them drawn? Claude recommends painting the picture-like ones (the object pictures, the animals and the faces) as a set of their own after the review, starting with a dozen tried at the size a question card shows them (about 48 to 96 pixels), where a busy painting can stop reading, and keeping drawn whatever carries an exact count, size, length, time, letter or label a question depends on (dots, ten frames, number lines, clocks, rows of shapes, tracing letters, the maps' tap areas, the game boards), since a painted count can disagree with its words (pass KZ). A painted object, animal or face also shows every sign a lesson line, a question or a More Info reason names for it, written into its check note (the sad face's tear, a bird's wings, a cat's pointy ears), since a reason that names a sign the painting lacks is a picture that contradicts its words. Each drawing stays until its painting arrives, as you said.
- Q44. - Q45. Answered in pass MM (Mikey: "Q45 - yes C"): from pre-K 3 to grade 2 a course's own game opens at the course's first celebration, when every lesson is passed once and its first long story opens, and from grade 3 up it still opens when every lesson has its star (gameOpensAtFirstPass and courseGameOpen in logic.mjs, read by unlockedGameIds; the rules test holds both stages and was proven failing on the old rule). The line a reader sees over a locked tile now says which (playLockedNote): "Finishing every module within a course unlocks a new game." for an early-years course, "Mastering every module within a course unlocks a new game." from grade 3 up, both sentences when both kinds are locked; round two's screen check found the early-years sentence reaches no screen today, since a student with an early-years lesson unmastered sees the young learner's screens, with no strip, and one who has mastered them all has every early game open. The question as it stood: When a course's game opens, for the youngest (pass ML, the game check). Where: Let's Play, for a child from pre-K 3 to grade 2, though every course works the same way today. What the child sees today: a course's own game opens only when every lesson in the course has its star, which means passing each lesson once and then again on another day, while the course's first long story opens sooner, as soon as every lesson is passed once. That is how pass CO built Mikey's request of September 24 for a game with every course (CLAUDE.md: "finishing the course unlocks them"), set before a course had two celebrations (pass MH). The exceptions are the games Let's Play may open on the first day, by his pass MC rule: Listen and Point's Step on the Circles, and one of the two games of a course that owns two, such as Very First Steps. Example: a four-year-old who has passed all eighteen First Steps lessons hears The Treasure in the Garden at the first celebration, but Look Closely, the course's new matching game, stays locked until all eighteen stars are earned, at least one more day and often many. Choices: (a) keep it as it is, the game at the last star, for every course; (b) open a course's game at the first celebration, beside its first long story, for every course; (c) open it at the first celebration from pre-K 3 to grade 2 only, and keep the last star from grade 3 up. What changes: with (b) or (c) the game's tile opens as soon as the course is completed once, and the second celebration still brings the next long story. Claude recommends (c): a four-year-old feels a wait of days far more than an older student does, and these games practice the course's own idea, so playing them between the first pass and the stars helps earn the stars, while older students keep the game as a reward for mastery.
- Q46. Answered in pass MM (Mikey: "Q46 - your recommendation"): a Wonder answer that names a color the color lessons teach as a square (red, blue, yellow or green) shows that square beside its word, on the pre-reader's big buttons, the reader's buttons and the educator's review chips, in both themes, with a thin darker edge so the yellow reads on white (optionSwatch in logic.mjs, OptionSwatch in ui.jsx); the word and the lit reading stay.
