Where: the Wonder question "Which color makes you happiest?", which belongs to First Steps (pre-K 4) and, once an educator has approved it, can come up for any child from pre-K 3 to grade 2 after a lesson; it is the only Wonder question whose answers are colors. What the child sees today: four chips with the words Red, Blue, Yellow and Green, read aloud one at a time and each lit as it is read, so a four-year-old who cannot read must wait for the color's name or remember where it was. Example: a child who loves blue waits for "Blue" to be read and lit before tapping. Choices: keep the words alone, or add a small square of the color (the same square the color lessons show) beside each word, keeping the words and the lit reading for children with red-green color vision deficiency. What changes: the four chips gain a colored square in both themes, a change to the light theme's look of those chips. Claude recommends adding the squares, since a pre-reader can then find blue by sight, the way the color lessons teach it.
- Q47. Answered in pass MM (Mikey: "Q47 - B"): in the light theme every red-brown word takes clayInk, #9A4F39 (4.80 to 1 on the pale pink strip, 5.48 on the page, 5.91 on white), while clay keeps the edges, fills, bars and drawings (#B9634A); the dark theme's clayInk is its clay, so nothing changes there; and the game and coloring titles' shimmer runs through #C46A1F and #3F8F3A in the light theme only, its lowest moment on the page now the red it always had, 3.41 to 1. The rules test reads the tokens, every word color in ui.jsx, and the shimmer's whole cycle. The question as it stood: Two light-theme colors below the usual contrast guideline (pass ML, the code and screen check). Where: (1) every red-brown word of the light theme: the line on a pale pink strip after a wrong answer, in every course (a reader sees "Not quite. The answer is ..." there, and the grown-up beside a pre-reader reads the reason for the wrong tap), and every warning line written in the same color (a wrong PIN, "Missed in the last checkpoint" on a student's lesson card, the touch-screen notes, the setup and roster notes); (2) the title above every game and every coloring page, whose letters shimmer through a rainbow when the device allows motion. Who meets it: everyone using the light theme. What they see today: (1) the red-brown (#B9634A) reads 3.44 to 1 on the pink strip (#F6E4DB), 3.93 on the page and 4.25 on white, where 4.5 to 1 is the guideline for words this size (the dark theme's strip reads 5.37 to 1); (2) the shimmering title dips to 1.9 to 1 at its light orange moment and 2.7 at its green one, under the 3 to 1 guideline for big words (its red, blue and purple pass). Example: after a wrong tap on the circle in Match the Shapes ("Tap the one that is the same as this triangle."), "The circle is round, with no corners, and a triangle has three corners. Have another try." shows in a pale red-brown that is hard to read on a bright phone in the sun. Choices: (a) keep both, since the light theme is frozen; (b) in the light theme only, darken every red-brown word to #9A4F39 (4.80 to 1 on the strip, 5.48 on the page, 5.91 on white) with a color for words alone, so the clay edges and fills of the game boards and cards stay as they are, and run the shimmer through a deeper orange (#C46A1F, 3.58 to 1) and a deeper green (#3F8F3A, 3.74 to 1); (c) darken the red-brown words only, keeping the shimmer. What changes: with (b) those words look a shade darker and the titles shimmer through deeper colors; nothing else in the light theme moves. Claude recommends (b): the grown-up beside a pre-reader reads that reason to understand the mistake, and the guideline exists for exactly that, while a slightly deeper shimmer loses none of its fun.
- Q48. Answered in pass MM (Mikey: "Q48 - B"): pre-K is read as one grade for its earmarks: First Sounds and Letters' review comes next, and then pre-K 3's remaining earmark (E12) and pre-K 4's (E13) are built together, side by side by domain, before kindergarten (section 10 and EARMARKS E12 and E13 say so). The question as it stood: When pre-K 3's remaining earmarks are built (pass ML, the alignment read). Where: docs/EARMARKS.md, the list of standards no lesson teaches yet (E12 for pre-K 3, E13 for pre-K 4); nothing on a child's screen changes until a lesson is built. What stands today: Mikey's plan (RULES-AUDIT section 10, step 3) builds a grade's earmarks after its full review and before the next grade; passes MD and ME built two parts of E12 (Happy, Sad, Mad and Scared; Helping a Friend; One More and One Fewer; Words That Rhyme), passes ME to MH planned the rest a lesson or two a pass alongside, and since pass MJ restarted the review at pre-K 3 no plan line has named a pass for them, so some forty-four pre-K 3 entries still wait, several of them Head Start twins of Texas outcomes. Example: II.A.2, "shows understanding by following two-step verbal directions.", has no lesson in pre-K 3 or pre-K 4. Choices: (a) build E12's remaining outcomes next, before First Sounds and Letters, as step 3 reads for pre-K 3 alone; (b) finish pre-K 4's review first with First Sounds and Letters, then build E12 and E13 together before kindergarten, reading pre-K as one grade; (c) a lesson or two a pass alongside the reviews, as passes ME to MH planned. What changes: (a) puts new pre-K 3 lessons first and First Sounds and Letters a pass or two later; (b) keeps First Sounds and Letters next and builds the new pre-K 3 and pre-K 4 lessons together after it; (c) spreads them through the passes ahead. Claude recommends (b): the two lists cover the same domains at two ages, so reading them side by side keeps each new lesson at the right age and never teaches one outcome twice, and pre-K 4's review is half done.
- Q49. Answered in pass MN (Mikey: "Q49 - Your recommendation"): (d), built in: every tracing pad shows one line's green start dot and arrow at a time, the next once a line is traced, as the demo draws them (traceLineState in logic.mjs, TracePad in ui.jsx), and pre-K keeps a line traced in any direction; from kindergarten up a line traced from its end back to its start clears as the finger lifts, a status line under the pad shows "Start at the green dot." and the voice says it until the next line starts, and a trace with a backward line earns no point (traceDirections, traceChecksDirection by the question's own module, read by checkAnswer); the connect-the-dots pictures keep their numbered order; the rules test traces 75 shapes forward in seven ways with five wobbles, never cleared, and every judged line backward, always cleared, and the browser test draws T both ways in pre-K and in kindergarten (section 6). The question as it stood: Whether a trace counts the order and direction of each line (pass MM, round one's and round two's lesson check B). Where: every tracing question of a mark, a shape, a letter or a number, the pad where a child draws with a finger, from pre-K 3 to grade 1 (Very First Steps' First marks, First Steps' First strokes and Draw the shapes, First Sounds and Letters' three tracing lessons, kindergarten's tracing lessons, grade 1's small letters, numbers and shapes); the connect-the-dots pictures (First Steps' Connect the dots, Very First Steps' Three dots) already make a child join their numbered dots in order, and stay as they are (round three's lesson check). What the child sees today: each line shows a green starting dot and an arrow, the lesson's demo draws the lines in order, and the lessons say "Start at the dot and follow the arrow"; but the check that decides whether a trace earns its point (traceMatches) looks only at whether the ink covers each line, so a letter drawn backward (bottom first, right to left), its lines in another order, or an O drawn clockwise or three times around passes the same as one traced as taught. The tracing lessons cite TEA's IV.C.4, "uses appropriate directionality when writing (e.g., top to bottom, left to right)", which the lines teach and nothing checks. Example: a four-year-old traces T by drawing the bar from right to left first, then the stem from the bottom up; today that earns the point, and nothing tells the child the dot and the arrow were the point. Choices: (a) keep it as it is, any order and direction; (b) guide the order without failing anyone: only the first line's dot and arrow show, and the next line's appear once a line is traced, as the demo draws them, and direction is not checked; (c) as (b), and a line traced from its end back to its start does not count: it clears, and the voice says "Start at the green dot." for another try; (d) (b) in pre-K, and (c) from kindergarten up. What changes: (b) changes what the pad shows, one line at a time, in every tracing question but the connect-the-dots pictures; (c) also changes when a trace counts, so a few traces children make today would need another try; pre-K, kindergarten and grade 1 share the pad and the check. Claude recommends (d): a four-year-old is still learning to hold a crayon, and showing one line at a time teaches the order with no failure, while from kindergarten a gentle redo of a backward line teaches the directionality the lessons cite, and every star stays within reach.
- Q50. Answered in pass MN (Mikey: "Q50 - Your recommendation"): (b), built in: each round of First Sounds and Letters' three tracing lessons is three traces, one of each of the lesson's letters, and two choices, "Tap the letter you hear." among the three (printed with no letter while the voice says it) and a letter found by its lines, nine distinct questions a lesson (pl-hear-* and pl-lines-*); no tracing round in any tracing lesson traces a letter twice while another is still to trace (buildAttempt); the citations count thirteen letters toward III.C.1 and P-LIT 3, with seven more waiting (EARMARKS E13; section 6). The question as it stood: Letter names inside the tracing lessons (pass MM, round one's lesson check B). Where: First Sounds and Letters' three tracing lessons (Trace a big letter: L, T and O; Trace more big letters: I, H and E; Trace L, T and F), for four-year-olds. What the child sees today: each round is five tracing questions, each picking one of the lesson's three letters at random, so a letter can come up three times or more, and in about two rounds in five one of the three is never traced (O in about one round in eight; round three's lesson check); nothing asks for a traced letter's name; the lines say the names ("That is L.") and Find the Letter asks them, but no lesson question does, so the course's citations count seven letters by name (Big letters') toward III.C.1 ("recognizes and names at least 20 letters") and Head Start's P-LIT 3. Example: a child traces L, T and O well and earns the star, but is never asked which of the three is T. Choices: (a) keep the tracing lessons tracing only, the letters picked at random; (b) mix two choice questions into each tracing round, "Tap the letter you hear." among the lesson's three letters (printed without the letter while the voice can say it) and a letter found by its lines ("Which letter goes down, then across the bottom?"), so a round is three traces, one of each letter, and two choices, nine distinct questions a lesson in place of three; (c) add only the name question, with each letter traced once before any comes again; (d) keep them tracing only, each of the three letters traced once before any comes again. What changes: with (b) or (c) a tracing round mixes drawing and tapping, which no touch lesson does yet; the traced letters are asked by name, the citations count thirteen letters toward III.C.1's twenty and P-LIT 3's half of the alphabet, and "thirteen more letters wait" becomes "seven more"; with (b), (c) or (d) every round traces each letter the lesson teaches, and kindergarten's tracing lessons, which pick their letters the same way, would do the same. Claude recommends (b): practicing the same three letters both ways helps a four-year-old tie each letter's shape to its name, a round of only three distinct tracings repeats itself, and every round then traces every letter the lesson teaches.
- Q52. Answered in pass MN (Mikey: "Q52 - B"): every answered question moved word for word to docs/RULES-AUDIT-HISTORY.md, with the answers of passes MB and MC that carried no number, held unchanged by a hash test as CLAUDE.md's history is, and this section keeps one line each, its number, the answer word for word and where its rule now lives (section 9). The question as it stood: The answered questions moved to a history file (pass MM, round three's alignment read). Where: docs/RULES-AUDIT.md, which every new chat reads right after NEW-CHAT. What it holds today: section 11 prints all 48 answered questions in full with your answers, about 65 KB of the file's 283 KB, and this pass added 41 KB in all; since pass LE nothing here is deleted or changed without your approval. Example: Q42's long question and your answer ("relax it for scene words only, from grade 3 up.") sit in full beside the open questions a chat must read. Choices: (a) keep them here as they are; (b) move each answered question word for word to docs/RULES-AUDIT-HISTORY.md, held unchanged by a hash test as CLAUDE.md's history is, and leave in its section 11 one line each with its number, your answer word for word and where its rule now lives. What changes: (b) cuts a new chat's reading by about a quarter of this file and loses nothing; the rows each answer set stay where they are. Claude recommends (b).
- Q53. Answered in pass MN (Mikey: "Q53 - B"): a kept CLAUDE.md line that has gone stale is corrected in place once this file records the change, since docs/CLAUDE-HISTORY.md keeps every old line word for word, and the list of stale lines that stood in for corrections is folded into them (section 9). The question as it stood: A kept CLAUDE.md line corrected in place (pass MM, round three's alignment read). Where: CLAUDE.md, the short manual every session and every checking agent loads. What it does today: its own rule says existing lines are not changed without your approval, so a line that has gone stale stays word for word and a list at the end says what stands instead; the list has ten items now, and the file sits at its 40,000-byte limit (39,995). Example: the Commands line still says the click-through is check.sh's step 5 and takes about a minute; it is step 10 and takes six to nine. Choices: (a) keep the kept lines word for word, with the list; (b) correct a kept line in place once RULES-AUDIT records the change, since docs/CLAUDE-HISTORY.md keeps every old line word for word, and fold the list into those corrections. What changes: (b) keeps the manual true where a coder reads it, with nothing lost. Claude recommends (b).
<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions through pass MN, word for word: end -->

<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass MO, word for word: begin -->
- Q54. Answered in pass MO (Mikey: "Q54 - your call as long as it looks visually appealing and sleek."): (b), built in: the rabbit wears the dark theme's soft brown outline (#8C7A5B) in the light theme too, with soft pink inner ears drawn behind its head, and the candle's body a slate outline (#76828B, 3.9 to 1 on white and 3.1 to 1 on the dark theme's paper), wherever they are drawn; the question-screens test measures both outlines at 3 to 1 or more in both themes (section 8). The question as it stood: Whether the light theme's rabbit and candle drawings get an outline (pass MN, round one's screen check). Where: the rabbit drawing, which since pass MN stands for a friend, mostly beside the bear, in ten lesson lines of pre-K 3's Can I play too? and pre-K 4's How do friends feel? and Being a good friend (19 lesson lines in all), and is also one of the pictures a young child picks for their name, and the candle in the breathing lines of Happy, sad, mad and scared and Feelings and what we do and their questions. Who meets them: three- and four-year-olds first, and anyone using the light theme. What they see today: in the light theme the rabbit is a pale cream (#E8DFD3) with no outline, 1.3 to 1 against the white page, and the candle's body outline is 1.8 to 1, where a drawing that carries meaning needs 3 to 1 (the rule the gold and green taps follow); both still read by their eyes and their flame. In the dark theme the rabbit has worn a soft brown outline since pass MA (#8C7A5B), and the light theme was left as it was, since it is frozen. Example: on a phone in the light theme, open Can I play too? and go to the line "You can find another friend to play with, too.": the bear stands out, and the rabbit beside it is a faint shape with eyes. Choices: (a) keep both as they are; (b) give the rabbit the dark theme's soft brown outline in the light theme too, and the candle's body a darker outline, wherever they are drawn. What changes: (b) changes how two drawings look in the light theme, in lessons, questions and the name pictures, and nothing else. Claude recommends (b): a three-year-old meets the rabbit as a friend in ten lines, and a shape at 1.3 to 1 is hard to see on a dim phone or in sunlight.
- Q59. Answered in pass MO (Mikey: "Q59 your recommendation is good"): (a), built in: each half of a lesson pair draws at 85 percent on a wrapper its drift never reaches, in the light theme as in the dark theme since round six, so a pair is the same size with motion on or off; the question-screens test measures three pairs in the light theme with motion off and on (section 8). The question as it stood: Whether the light theme draws a lesson's pairs at one size with motion on and off (pass MO, round six's code and screen read). Where: every lesson picture that shows two things side by side, such as a big circle beside a little one, two faces or a flower and a candle, in every grade, most of all pre-K's Big and small, Two steps and Two ideas. What a child sees today: each half of a pair is drawn at 85 percent of its size, so the two fit a phone with room between them, but with motion on (most devices' setting) the half's gentle drift animation takes the place of that size, so the pair draws at full size; with motion off it draws at 85 percent. In the dark theme at 320 pixels wide, full size made nine pairs touch or overlap, so since this pass the dark theme keeps the 85 percent size with motion on too. The light theme has room for full size and nothing overlaps there, so it draws as it always has, since its look is yours to change. Example: on a light-theme phone with motion on, a lesson line that shows a big circle beside a little one draws both about 18 percent larger than the same phone draws them with motion off. Choices: (a) the light theme does as the dark theme now does, each pair the same 85 percent size with motion on or off; (b) keep the light theme as it is. What changes: (a) makes a light-theme phone's pairs, with motion on, about 15 percent smaller, as they already are with motion off, and nothing else; (b) changes nothing. Claude recommends (a), since 85 percent is the size the pairs were drawn for, a motion setting should never change how big a drawing is, and the two themes would match; (b) is safe too, since nothing overlaps in the light theme today.
<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass MO, word for word: end -->

<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass MP, word for word: begin -->
- Q55. Answered in pass MP (Mikey: "Q55 - A"): (a), kept: a heard rhyme question keeps its two words unprinted, "Listen. Do these two words rhyme?" on the screen while the voice says them, so the skill stays a listening one (section 6). The question as it stood: Whether a heard rhyme question prints its two words (pass MO, from Mikey's note in this pass's opening message). Where: First Sounds and Letters' Rhyme time, for four-year-olds, the 740 questions that ask by ear. What a child sees today: the voice says "Listen. Do top and hop rhyme?" while the screen prints "Listen. Do these two words rhyme?" over Yes and No, so the two words are heard and never printed. Mikey asked: "Should we list the words top and hop within the question too? Same with hat and cat.  Maybe you did it so the answer isn't given away by reading?" Why they are not printed: in pass MM round one's lesson check found that every rhyme question with its words on the screen could be answered by matching letters, without listening, on every one of the 6,000 seeds it tried (hat and cat end in the same two letters), while the skill the standard names, telling whether two words rhyme (TEA's III.B.6), is a listening one, so round three printed "these two words" and left the words to the voice. Example: a four-year-old who knows a few letters sees "hat" and "cat", spots the same two letters at the end and taps Yes without hearing anything; with "these two words" on the screen she has to listen, and the speaker says them again as often as she likes. Choices: (a) keep the words unprinted, as today; (b) print them for every child; (c) print them only in an educator's walk-through, never to a child. What changes: (b) lets a child answer by sight; (c) changes only what an educator sees while walking through a lesson. Claude recommends (a), since the skill is listening, with (c) as a fine addition if you want educators to see the words.
- Q56. Answered in pass MP (Mikey: "Q56 - A"): (a), built in: every yellow shape and color square wears a thin darker edge, #9C7A14 (4.0 to 1 on white, 3.1 on the dark theme's paper), in both themes, and the lessons' green wears #3D6B41 (4.8 to 1 on that paper) in the dark theme, the fill itself unchanged (swatchEdge in ui.jsx, read by Swatch and the shapes; section 8). The question as it stood: Whether yellow shapes get a thin darker edge (pass MO, round one's screen check). Where: every yellow shape and color square the app draws on white or on a lesson's paper, for three- and four-year-olds: pre-K 3's Yellow and green, Two steps and Two ideas, pre-K 4's Colors, and the color questions of both courses. What a child sees today: the lessons' yellow (#F0C93A) reads 1.6 to 1 against a white answer tile in the light theme and 1.2 to 1 against a lesson's paper in the dark theme (the dark theme's own tiles are fine, 8.7 to 1), and on that dark paper a green shape reads 2.6 to 1 too, in More Info's boxes for Two ideas, so a yellow shape's edge is faint, and for a child or a grown-up who sees less well it can nearly vanish; the bar this app keeps for a picture a child must see is 3 to 1 (the web's guideline for shapes, and the bar Q54's outlines met). Example: "Tap the yellow one, and then tap the blue one." shows a red, a yellow and a blue square on white tiles, and the yellow one is the palest thing on the screen. Choices: (a) give every yellow shape and color square the thin darker edge the Wonder color squares took in Q46 (a soft dark line around the shape, the yellow itself unchanged), in both themes, and the green ones on the dark theme's paper the same; (b) a deeper yellow for every drawing (it would no longer match the yellow the color lessons name, like a banana); (c) keep it as it is. What changes: (a) and (b) change how the light theme looks, which is why this is yours to answer; (a) touches only the shapes' edges. Claude recommends (a), since Q46 already chose it for the yellow square on a white button, and a color a lesson names must be easy to see.
- Q57. Answered in pass MP (Mikey: "Q57 - A"): (a), built in: the app makes the five short vowels itself, with buh, duh, guh and juh and the endings ig, og and at, on the voice check page's rows 33 to 50 alone (TRIAL_SOUNDS), and no lesson uses one until Mikey says which (Q60; section 4). The question as it stood: When grade 1's sounding-out lines get a true vowel sound (pass MO, round three's alignment read). Where: grade 1 reading, in ten lines that sound out a word, in Read the word, Sh, ch and th, The silent e and the story The word on the box, for six-year-olds (EARMARKS A42). What a child hears today: in Read the word's "C says cuh, a says the sound at the start of apple, and n says nnn. Cuh, a, nnn. Can.", the app plays its own c and n sounds, but the a between them goes to the phone's voice alone, and a voice says a letter alone as its name, so the run can be heard as cuh, ay, nnn, nearer cane than can. Row 32 of the voice check page plays that line just as a lesson does. Example: a six-year-old who hears ay in the middle learns to blend can as cane, the very slip the lesson is there to prevent. Choices: (a) next pass, before Domain II's vocabulary, the app makes the five short vowels itself, the way it makes sss, with new rows on the voice check page for you to hear before any lesson uses them; (b) next pass, each run names its vowel by the word it starts ("Cuh, the sound at the start of apple, nnn."), which needs no new sounds but makes a run longer to blend; (c) leave the ten lines for grade 1's review, as EARMARKS A42 plans now. What changes: (a) and (b) change only what grade 1 hears, never what the screen shows; (c) changes nothing until grade 1's turn. Claude recommends (a), since a wrong vowel teaches a wrong blend, and a made vowel can be heard and tuned on the voice check page, as the other sounds were for Q51.
- Q58. Answered in pass MP (Mikey: "Q58 - A"): (a), built in: after two wrong orders only the ringed picture takes a number, and a tap on another picture takes nothing and wobbles a moment with a clay edge, the ring staying where it is (stepNudge in ui.jsx; section 6). The question as it stood: What a tap on another picture does while a direction's green ring shows (pass MO, round four's code and screen read). Where: pre-K's Follow the directions questions, in pre-K 3's Two steps and pre-K 4's Three steps, for three- and four-year-olds. What a child sees today: after two wrong orders, the next picture to tap wears the app's green "go here" ring, one step at a time. If the child then taps a different picture, that picture takes the next gold number and the ring goes away; it comes back only when the child has tapped every step and checked a wrong order again, or has tapped the wrong picture again to take its number back, which a three-year-old may not know to do. Example: "Tap the cup, and then tap the spoon." The cup wears the ring, the child taps the spoon, the spoon shows a gold 1, and no picture wears the ring until the child taps the cup, taps Check answer, hears "Let's listen again." and tries once more. Choices: (a) after two wrong orders only the ringed picture takes a number, and a tap on another picture gives it a little wobble and takes nothing, so the ring stays where it is and the child always finishes the direction; (b) keep today's way; (c) a wrong tap takes its number as today, and the ring moves to that numbered picture, to show it can be tapped to take the number back. What changes: (a) and (c) change only what happens after two wrong orders, never the first two tries, and in every choice mastery still counts only the first try. Claude recommends (a), since it is how the app already helps after two misses (a wrong choice greys out on its retry, and a trace draws itself), and a child who cannot hold the steps meets success instead of a loop.
<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass MP, word for word: end -->

<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass MQ, word for word: begin -->
- Q51. Answered in pass MQ (Mikey: "We can close question 51 for now since it won't be relevant in our refinements until later."): closed for now, with its earmark, in his words "let's just earmark this for later when we upload the eleven audio.": the lessons' sounds are heard again when the recorded voice is made (EARMARKS A41), and until then every page with spoken audio, games included, shows a small note at its foot in his words, "All spoken audio on this page will be updated during the final phases of development. What you hear now is merely a placeholder spoken by your device." (VoiceNote in ui.jsx, shown only where the device can speak and gone once every clip of the page is recorded, never printed; section 4). The question as it stood: Whether the voice says the lessons' sounds and word endings as sounds (pass MM; since pass MO the app makes its own sounds; brought up to date in pass MP from your notes on the 32 rows). Where: every spoken line that holds a sound or a word's ending: First Sounds and Letters' First sounds and Rhyme time, for four-year-olds, kindergarten's Rhymes and letter-sound lessons, grade 1's sounding-out lines, and the stories that say a sound slowly, such as Sam's "Sss!". What you heard in pass MO, row by row on the voice check page: sss and fff "very close" but "a little like static", and you asked for "fragments like sss sss sun"; zzz with "static behind it"; t, p and k "like a tap on the rim of a drum"; ig and og spelled out by your laptop's voice (igg as "i g g", ihg as "i h g", og as "o g"), while plain ig "Actually sounds correct this time"; buh "like boo", and duh, guh and juh with the uh "emphasized more than" the consonant; "Ball, bed and bear" said too quickly; row 25's words not matching what it said; and in row 32 the a of "a says" "spoken so quickly ... the letter a is just easily missed", cuh strange, and the a of "Cuh, a, nnn" said as the letter. You heard rows 11 to 32 only on your laptop. What changed in pass MP, on the same page and link: rows 1, 2 and 5 come as two short breaths (sss, sss), zzz with more voice and less hiss; rows 8, 9 and 10 end in a short, soft uh (tuh, puh, cuh); rows 12, 13 and 14 play those new sounds in their lines; rows 19 and 20 say their three example words one at a time, slowly; row 25 shows the words it says; row 32 says each letter on its own, slowly (C, A, N), with the new cuh; a green note under each of these rows says what to listen for; and the page now says when your browser has no voice, the likely reason rows 11 to 32 were silent on your phone: a page opened inside another app's browser (the Claude app's, for instance) may have no voice, while Safari and Chrome have one. Rows 33 to 50 are new sounds and lines for Q60. Rows 16 to 18, 22, 23 and 27 to 30, where the voice spelled ig and og or made buh sound like boo, are unchanged on purpose: they are what a lesson hands the voice today, and rows 38 to 48 play the app's own buh, duh, guh, juh, ig, og and at beside them, so Q60 is where you choose; they are still worth hearing on your phone, whose voice may say them differently from your laptop's. Example: open the page's link in Safari or Chrome on your phone with the sound on and tap row 1: you should hear two short hisses with a breath between, sss, sss. Choices: (a) every row sounds right, and Q51 is done; (b) some rows still sound wrong: tell Claude the numbers and what you hear, and Claude changes those sounds or what the voice is handed, never what the screen shows. When the recorded clips are made in ElevenLabs, the same choices go into its pronunciation dictionary (EARMARKS A41). Claude recommends listening on your phone in Safari or Chrome this time, rows 1 to 32 first, since some voices differ from your laptop's (your phone spelled ig in pass MN where your laptop said it well). In pass MN Mikey wrote "Q51 - I will check this out, please keep repeating this question until complete": it stays open, and every delivery message lists it in full until he says it is done.
- Q60. Answered in pass MQ (Mikey: "Q60 - B"): (b), read as the choice's own example, "38 to 44 yes, the vowels not yet", since the answer named no rows, and told to Mikey at the top of the delivery, built in: the lessons say the app's own buh, duh, guh and juh and the endings ig, og and at made whole (SPEECH_SOUNDS, soundOfNotation and rimePieces in logic.mjs), where the voice had been handed buh, duh, guh, juh, igg, a lone at and og inside its sentence; the five short vowels stay on trial (TRIAL_SOUNDS), and on the voice check page rows 38 to 48 are lesson rows now, the vowels' rows (33 to 37, 49 and 50) on trial (section 4; EARMARKS A42). The question as it stood: Which of the app's new sounds the lessons should use (pass MP, after your answer to Q57: "the app makes the five short vowels itself ... with new rows on the voice check page for you to hear before any lesson uses them"). Where: the voice check page's rows 33 to 50: rows 33 to 37 the five short vowels (a as in apple, e as in egg, i as in igloo, o as in octopus, u as in up), rows 38 to 41 buh, duh, guh and juh, rows 42 to 44 the endings ig, og and at, each made by the app like sss, and rows 45 to 50 lesson lines said with them as a lesson would say them. Who meets them: four-year-olds and kindergarten in the letter-sound and rhyme lessons (buh, duh, guh, juh, ig, og, at) and six-year-olds in grade 1's ten sounding-out lines (the vowels). What a child hears today: the phone's voice says buh, duh, guh and juh (you heard buh as "boo" and the uh louder than the d), ig handed to the voice as igg and og inside its sentence (your laptop spelled both), and in "Cuh, a, nnn. Can." the a between the app's own sounds goes to the voice, which says the letter's name, so the word can be heard nearer cane than can. Example: tap row 19 ("Ball, bed and bear all start with buh." as lessons say it today) and then row 45 (the same line with the app's buh), and row 32 and then row 49 (the run with the app's short a). Choices: (a) the lessons use every new sound; (b) the lessons use only the rows you name, for example "38 to 44 yes, the vowels not yet", and the rest wait; (c) none yet, until the recorded voice (Q61). What changes: (a) and (b) change only what a child hears in those lines, never what the screen shows, and each sound the lessons take moves from the trial rows into the lesson rows of the page. Claude recommends (b) after listening on your phone: a made vowel is the hardest sound to make well, so it may pass later than the consonants and endings.
- Q61. Answered in pass MQ (Mikey: "Q61 - C"): (c), kept: the plan stands, and every line is recorded after the standards review (section 10, NEW-CHAT's step 5), with Q51's sounds heard again then (EARMARKS A41). Nothing was built. The question as it stood: When to bring in the recorded voice for the sounds and the game welcomes (pass MP, from your notes on Q51's rows 11, 12, 15, 17 and 21: "Perhaps these are better with Eleven labs", "This would be way better with Eleven too because the cadence of the sentences is very unnatural", "I think this would just sound better with Eleven.  Less static and more natural sounding I assume.  You can also give it context to describe what you're trying to accomplish which should improve the natural sound", and on the game welcomes, "With Eleven, we can make this game audio exciting and fun"). Where: every line a child hears; the plan (NEW-CHAT, step 5) records the ElevenLabs audio after the standards review, from the audio ledger (docs/AUDIO-LEDGER.csv, 9,952 clips, about 1.65 million characters with their tags, of which the game welcomes are 343 clips and about 39,000 characters). What a child hears today: the phone's own voice for the words and the app's made sounds between them, two different voices in one line ("would sound better if the mmm was the same person who was reading the word moon"). What ElevenLabs can do for the sounds (from its documentation): Eleven v4 can be handed a sound in the phonetic alphabet between slashes, such as /s/ or /æ/, though how well depends on the voice, so it needs a test; its Voice Changer turns a recording of a person saying the sounds into the chosen voice, keeping the person's timing; and a pronunciation dictionary keeps a word said one way every time. Example: a lesson line "Sun. Sss. The word sun starts with the sound sss." recorded whole in one voice, the hiss as part of the line. Choices: (a) a small test now: about forty clips (the ten sounds the lessons use, the new ones of rows 33 to 44, and the voice check page's lesson lines) and one early-years story, The other mitten (pre-K 3's story S119), in the voices you choose, so you can hear whether one voice says the sounds well, how the calm, unhurried story direction sounds, whether the calm holds around the story's [excited] and [rushed] lines (91 early story clips carry one, for a character's moment), and whether each character keeps one voice from one paragraph's clip to the next, before anything else is recorded; (b) record the game welcomes and the phonics lines now, and the rest after the standards review; (c) keep the plan: everything after the standards review. What changes: (a) and (b) need you to run tools/audio-generate.mjs on your computer with your ElevenLabs key (the app's sandbox cannot reach ElevenLabs), and Claude would add the respellings and phonetic spellings the test needs; (c) changes nothing now. Claude recommends (a): it costs very little, a few thousand characters, and it settles the question your notes keep raising before the big recording begins.
- Q62. Answered in pass MQ (Mikey: "Q62 - A"): (a), built in: the duck and the bee wear a thin deeper-gold outline, #94741A at 1.5 units, wherever they are drawn, in both themes, the bee's pale wings too, and the duck's beak is tucked under its outline; #94741A reads 4.4 to 1 on white, 3.1 or better on every name picture's tint and 3.2 to 3.4 on the dark theme's paper and tiles (section 8). The question as it stood: Whether the duck and the bee get a thin darker outline (pass MP, found while drawing the owl's ears). Where: the duck and the bee pictures, two of the twelve sign-in animals, which pre-K's questions use as answers ("Tap the animal that says quack.", "Tap the animal that says hoot." over a duck and an owl), on white answer tiles in the light theme, for three- and four-year-olds. What a child sees today: the duck's yellow reads 1.5 to 1 against a white tile and the bee's 1.9 to 1, so the duck is the palest thing on the screen, where the bar this app keeps for a picture a child must see is 3 to 1 (Q54 gave the rabbit and the candle their outlines for this reason, and Q56 the yellow shapes). Example: in "Tap the animal that says hoot." the owl stands out and the duck nearly fades into its tile. Choices: (a) give the duck and the bee a thin darker outline, a deeper gold, the way Q54 outlined the rabbit, in both themes; (b) keep them as they are. What changes: (a) changes how the light theme looks, which is why this is yours to answer, and only the edges of the two drawings. Claude recommends (a), for the same reason as Q54 and Q56.
- Q63. Answered in pass MQ (Mikey: "Q63 - A"): (a), built in: a game closed before Begin is logged with begun false and counted in no game break (makePlayedEvent's begun and isGameBreak in logic.mjs): not the report's "Game breaks taken" line, the weekly note or the changes since a report was seen; a visit logged before pass MQ, with no begun, counts as it always did, and a game the child opened stays open on the list (section 8, Play and reading in the report's summary). The question as it stood: Whether a game watched only through its welcome counts as a game break (pass MP, round one's game check). Where: the educator's report, under the summary, in the line "Game breaks taken: N.", and the weekly note's count of games played, for every student. What happens today: since pass MB, when you asked for the "Game breaks taken" line, a break has been counted whenever a game is opened and closed, so since this pass's welcomes a child who opens a game, watches its welcome of about half a minute and closes it before tapping Begin counts one break, the same as a child who played for five minutes. Example: a kindergartner opens Which Sense, watches the pointer drag the words down to the ear, then taps the X, and the report says "Game breaks taken: 1." Choices: (a) count a break only once the child has tapped Begin; (b) keep counting every game opened and closed. What changes: (a) changes only what the report and the weekly note count; nothing a child sees changes, and a game the child opened stays open on the list as before (the visit is still kept in the record, marked as not begun). Claude recommends (a): the welcome is the game's instructions, not play, so the count stays the number of games actually played.
- Q64. Answered in pass MQ (Mikey: "Q64 - It's actually okay as is"): (b), kept: the speaker under a game keeps its gold ring and pale gold circle in the light theme (section 8). Nothing was built. The question as it stood: Whether the speaker under a game loses its gold ring in the light theme (pass MP, round one's screen check). Where: the speaker under every game since this pass, which reads the instructions again and which the welcome's closing line names, in the light theme, for every student; in the dark theme it has no ring. What a child sees today in the light theme: Which Sense shows the new borderless speaker beside its question, as you asked ("Keep it borderless like all of the other icons on the page to reduce clutter and make it look sleek"), and under the board the instructions speaker in a gold ring on a pale gold circle, the way the youngest games' speaker has looked since September, so one page has a ringed speaker and a borderless one. Example: open Which Sense in the light theme: the speaker beside "Which sense tells you about a barking dog?" has no ring, and the one under Instructions has a gold one. Choices: (a) the speaker under a game loses its ring and its pale circle in the light theme too, drawn like the speaker by the question; (b) keep the ring in the light theme. What changes: (a) changes how the light theme looks, only that one speaker under the games, which is why this is yours to answer; the speakers in lessons and stories keep theirs. Claude recommends (a), since your words ask for the page's icons to be borderless alike.
- Q65. Answered in pass MQ (Mikey: "Q65 - B"): (b), built in: the games for pre-K to grade 2 close their welcome with "To hear this again, tap the speaker. Have fun!" (GAME_INTRO_CLOSING_YOUNG, by gameBand), and from grade 3 up a welcome keeps Mikey's line; the audio ledger lists the young line as a clip of its own (section 7). The question as it stood: How long the youngest games' welcomes should be (pass MP, round two's alignment read; round one had left their length for you to hear). Where: the welcome every game plays as it opens, before Begin; this question is about the 35 games for pre-K to grade 2 (Kite, Pairs, Red and Blue, Step on the Circles, Which Sense, Walk the Robot and the rest), which three- to seven-year-olds open. What a child hears today: three to five lines that explain the game while it plays itself, then your closing line, with the speaker named where you wrote 'instructions', since your later note gives the speaker that job: "If you wish to hear these instructions again, simply tap the speaker near the bottom of the page. Have fun!" A young welcome runs 53 to 92 words, about half a minute (Which Sense about 52 seconds on a computer, where it adds the number keys), and the closing line is 20 of those words, a fifth to more than a third of a young welcome; few three-year-olds know the word instructions. Example: Kite says "Welcome to Kite! This game helps you count in order. Tap the dots one by one, one, two, three, and a picture appears. The gold dot is always the next one to tap." and then the 20-word closing line before Begin. Choices: (a) keep every welcome as it is; (b) the youngest games close with "To hear this again, tap the speaker. Have fun!", and grade 3 up keep your line; (c) as (b), and the eight young welcomes with four or five lines before the closing (Which Sense, Spot It, Find the Letter and Find the Tool five; Walk the Robot, Share the Cookies, the space shooter and Grow the Plant four) trimmed to three. What changes: only what a child hears and the caption shows before Begin; (b) takes about five seconds off every young welcome, and (c) ten to twenty more off those eight. Claude recommends (b): older students keep your words, the youngest hear words they know and reach Begin sooner, and Skip already ends any welcome.
- Q66. Answered in pass MQ (Mikey: "Q66 - A"): (a), built in: a wrong answer's red-brown edge shows with motion on too, beside the wobble, in all those games, a dashed edge on a button or card and a ring on Spot It's and Teach the Robot's pieces, and the six welcomes and their help say a wrong answer "gets a red-brown edge" (Spot It's, "a red-brown ring"); the game-intro test reads the edge with motion on (section 8). The question as it stood: Whether a wrong answer shows its red-brown edge with motion on too (pass MP, round four's second read). Where: every game whose wrong answer wobbles a button or a card, 37 kinds and 65 games (round three found thirty games, Which Sense, Whose Turn Next, Spot It and the rest, where the wobble was the only sign), and Spot It's and Teach the Robot's pieces, for every student. What a child sees today: with motion on, a wrong answer wobbles for half a second; on a phone or tablet set to reduce motion (on an iPhone, Settings, Accessibility, Motion, Reduce Motion), a wobble cannot play, so since this pass that answer wears a dashed red-brown edge for the same half second instead (Spot It's and Teach the Robot's pieces a ring). Six welcomes and their help (Spot It, What Comes Next, Build the Chord, Sort the Because, Cut the Fillers and Ask the Right Question) say a wrong answer "wobbles", which is what most devices show, while the Step on games, whose squares show a red-brown edge either way, say a wrong step "gets a red-brown edge", and the map says a wrong tap "turns the place you touched red for a moment". So if your phone is set to reduce motion, you hear "wobbles" and see an edge. Example: in Whose Turn Next, when Ava has the talking stick, Ben is next; tap Dee instead: with motion on, Dee's button shakes; with less motion, it gets a dashed red-brown outline. Choices: (a) the red-brown edge shows with motion on too, beside the wobble, in all of those games, and the six welcomes and their help say a wrong answer "gets a red-brown edge"; (b) keep it as it is. What changes: (a) changes how the light theme looks for that half second in all 65 games, a dashed edge beside the wobble, and on Spot It's and Teach the Robot's pieces a ring (a few, such as The Better Deal and In order, already wear a clay border of their own, so there it would add a second edge), which is why it is yours to answer, and six welcome lines (six new recordings for the voice) and six help lines; (b) changes nothing. Claude recommends (a): every child sees one sign, the words are true on every device, and an edge that holds still is easier to see than a quick shake.

<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass MR, word for word: begin -->
- Q67. Answered in pass MR (Mikey: "Q67 - B but let's make it fun.  When they say it with you, the voice should do something silly like whisper it, say it slow, say it low four times in a silly voice, say it ultra fast three times, draw it out the next time etc.  There can be variations between the events where this applies to keep it interesting and fun.  This page will be very important to mark with our not final audio message"): (b), made fun, built in: eight young vocabulary lessons (Learning words, Words for people, More learning words and Words that go together, with the four halves Q70 split from them, Count and sort, Things that happen, Rhymes and shapes, and Same and opposite) end 31 lines with a cue that names the new word and then say it in a silly voice for the child to say along, a tiny whisper, nice and slow, four times in a low, silly voice, three times super fast, stretched out long, a squeaky little voice or a happy cheer (SAY_WITH_ME), no lesson using one voice twice in a row, and each shows the note that its audio is not final in a panel under its buttons; the scripts test holds every cue. The question as it stood: Whether the youngest lessons invite a child to say a new word aloud (pass MQ, the standards review: Domain II's vocabulary outcomes ask that a child "understands (receptive) and uses (expressive)" words, and pass MQ's four vocabulary lessons, Learning words, People and events, More learning words and Words that go together, teach the understanding half, since the app never listens, so the using half is left to the child's own talk). Where: the spoken lessons of pre-K 3 and pre-K 4 (Very First Steps, Listen and Point, First Steps, and First Sounds and Letters), for three- and four-year-olds, wherever a lesson brings in a new word, such as grandpa, sort or opposite. What a child hears today: the voice names the word and goes on; in People and events, "This is a grandpa. A grandpa is the dad of your mom or your dad." The research: in Text Talk, the read-aloud method Isabel Beck and Margaret McKeown built for young children, the work on each new word began with its use in the story and its meaning, and each activity also included "having children repeat the word so they had a phonological representation of what they were learning" (Beck and McKeown, 2001, "Text talk: Capturing the benefits of read-aloud experiences for young children", The Reading Teacher, volume 55, issue 1, pages 10 to 20): a word said aloud gives a child its sound to hang the meaning on. Example: "This is a grandpa. Say it with me. Grandpa!" and then a short pause, about two seconds, before the next line. Nothing is recorded: the app uses no microphone, and the pause runs the same whether the child speaks or not, so the rule that read-aloud is output only holds. Choices: (a) keep the lessons listen-only; (b) add "Say it with me" to the new words of those four lessons only, about thirty words (grandpa, sort, opposite, vehicles and the rest), as a trial you can hear; (c) add it wherever a pre-K lesson first teaches a word. What changes: only what a child hears, a few seconds more on each such line, and those lines' recordings in the audio ledger; (b) changes four lessons, and (c) every pre-K lesson that teaches a word. Claude recommends (b): it is what the research did, it costs little, and you can hear how it feels in a lesson before it spreads.
- Q68. Answered in pass MR (Mikey: "Q68 - C"): (c), built in with his new closing words from the same message: all 35 young games close "To hear this again, tap the speaker at the bottom. But most importantly, HAVE FUN!!!" (GAME_INTRO_CLOSING_YOUNG). The question as it stood: Which speaker the youngest games' closing line means, in the six games with a second speaker beside their question (pass MQ, round one's screen check, after Mikey's answer to Q65). Where: the spoken welcome that opens six games for young learners, pre-K 4's Find the Letter and five from the kindergarten to grade 2 electives (Which Sense, Find the Tool, Spot It, Share the Cookies and Grow the Plant), heard by a young learner each time a game opens. What a child hears today: one line teaches the speaker beside the question ("Need to hear the question again? Tap the speaker next to the words."), and then, as Q65's (b) closes every young welcome, "To hear this again, tap the speaker. Have fun!", which means the speaker under the game, the one that reads the instructions again; the pointer moves to that speaker as the line is said, but the words alone do not say which of the two it is, so a child who only listens may tap the one beside the question and hear the question instead. The older games' line names its place: "If you wish to hear these instructions again, simply tap the speaker near the bottom of the page. Have fun!" Choices: (a) keep the line as it is, the pointer showing which speaker; (b) in those six games only, say "To hear this again, tap the speaker at the bottom. Have fun!"; (c) say that line in all 35 young games, so every young welcome ends the same way. What changes: only the last line's words, heard and shown in the caption, and its recording in the audio ledger (one line in (c), two versions in (b)). Claude recommends (c): it names the place as the older line does, a young child hears one closing everywhere, and it is one recording.
- Q69. Answered in pass MR (Mikey: "Q69 - C"): (c), built in: red-brown on a game's piece means a wrong answer, and what (c) left as they are stays; 36 games' pieces wear an edge of their own, Better Deal's coupons are blue and violet, the Valid, Reason and flag buttons have neutral ink edges, the struck filler word is gray, and Split the Search's part above the line is lavender. The question as it stood: Red-brown marks that are not a wrong answer, now that every wrong answer shows a red-brown dashed edge (pass MQ, after Mikey's answer to Q66; round one's screen check found it, and round three's reads counted it). Where: 42 games in 14 kinds, from pre-K to college, where red-brown already on a piece a child picks either doubles Q66's edge or means something else (counted from the code with tools/game-colors.mjs in rounds four and five; the dot, path, Find the Letter and Look Closely boards turn a wrong piece red-brown too, as the sign itself, with no second edge). What a player sees today, in two kinds of place. In 36 games a wrong piece already turns its own border red-brown for a moment (a red-brown ring in kindergarten's Big and Small and pre-K 3's Red and Blue): Pay the Price (kindergarten), the eight sort-into-two games such as Noun or verb, the nine In order games, the three Build a sentence games (grades 3 to 5), the nine Find the evidence games, the three Fix it games and The periodic table; since Q66's (a), "Q66 - A", the dashed edge shows outside that border too, so a wrong piece wears two red-brown edges. And six boards show red-brown that means something else: The Better Deal draws its dollars-off coupon with a red-brown dashed edge and red-brown words all the time (it is the right answer on about 3 boards in 10), Valid or Not, Reason or Not and Green Flag or Red Flag draw their left button (Not valid, Does not, Red flag) with a red-brown border, Cut the Fillers strikes out each filler a child cuts, a right tap, in red-brown, and Split the Search (college) turns each card a player opens red-brown when its number is above the one to find, and blue when below. Example: The Better Deal's first board asks which coupon saves more on $140 headphones, 25% off or $15 off; the $15 off coupon looks marked wrong before anyone taps it, and a wrong tap on it wobbles it inside a second dashed red-brown edge. Choices: (a) keep everything as it is; (b) in the 36 games whose wrong piece already turns red-brown, leave the dashed edge out, so the piece wears one edge, its own, which stays still with less motion too; (c) as (b), and give the six boards' red-brown another color (the dollars coupon, the three left buttons, the struck filler and Split the Search's cards above the number), so red-brown on a button means only a wrong answer (left as they are: the red-brown name a card shows while it is dragged left in seven swipe games, and the red-brown line, bar or ring of five boards, Build the Fund, Break Even, Fill the Jar, Shape the Sound and Push to the Beat). What changes: (b) changes how a wrong piece looks in those 36 games, and (c) also how the six boards look; both change the frozen light theme, and the dark theme with it. Claude recommends (c): it keeps what Q66 was for, one sign for a wrong answer that every child can count on, and a coupon that looks marked wrong but is right three times in ten teaches the opposite.
- Q70. Answered in pass MR (Mikey: "Q70 - C but I'm not incredibly strict on the length.  If more educational value or high quality explanatory context comes from extra length, I would always choose the extra length."): (c), built in, no half cut for length: the nine long pre-K lessons, pre-K 3's Talking with a friend, Two steps, Two ideas, Learning words and People and events (now Words for people), and pre-K 4's Let's play together, Three steps, More learning words and Words that go together, are eighteen whole lessons, each half with its own spoken script, story, coloring page, five or more question kinds and citations split "in part". The question as it stood: Whether this pass's five vocabulary lessons should be shorter (pass MQ, round three's alignment read). Where: pre-K 3's Learning words, People and events and Talking with a friend, and pre-K 4's More learning words and Words that go together, spoken lessons a three- or four-year-old hears one line at a time, with a tap between lines. What a child meets today: 14 to 21 lines, 225 to 378 words, where the older pre-K lessons' middle length is 9 lines and 131 words; pass MO's four lessons for Domains I and II are about as long (Two steps 18 lines, Two ideas 21, Let's play together 18, Three steps 17), and NEW-CHAT's house rules say young learners' lessons stay short. With the slower young voice, More learning words is about three minutes of listening across its 21 lines. They are long because each covers an outcome with many words (More learning words has count, sort, match, a pair, trace, corner, side and a three-word direction). Choices: (a) keep them as they are, and record the exception for a lesson that teaches an outcome's word list; (b) split the three longest, More learning words (21 lines), Talking with a friend and Words that go together (18 each), into two lessons each, every new lesson with its own story, coloring page and share of the questions; (c) as (b), and every other pre-K lesson of 14 lines or more: Learning words (17 lines), People and events (14) and pass MO's four. What changes: (b) adds three lessons, three stories and their pictures, and (c) nine; a child meets shorter lessons and more of them. Claude recommends (b): a three- or four-year-old listens best in short stretches, the house rule asks for it, and the three longest are where it matters most, while (c) can follow once you have heard how the split ones feel.
- Q71. Answered in pass MR (Mikey: "Q71 - yeah, let's make users turn the phone upright.  I looked at some screens and didn't like the extra scrolling and squeezed content.  Let's make sure it stays too.  I was wondering why it stopped one day"): (a), built in: the rules came out of the stray braces browsers drop, so a phone held sideways shows a drawing of a phone turning upright and the words asking for it, over every screen, until it is turned; tests/site.test.mjs holds the rule and four screens, so it stays. The question as it stood: Whether a phone held sideways asks to be turned upright, as Mikey decided on September 16 (pass MQ, round three's screen read found the ask never shows). Where: every screen of the app on a phone turned on its side, for learners and educators alike; tablets and laptops never see it. What a phone shows today: the app itself, squeezed into a short, wide window (390 pixels tall on a common phone), because the stylesheet rule that shows the ask sits inside a stray pair of braces that browsers drop, so the screen his decision asked for (DECISIONS, 2026-09-16: "A phone held sideways is asked to turn upright rather than shown a squeezed layout") does not show; it did not show in pass MP's build either, and when it stopped is not recorded, since no test and no row of RULES-AUDIT held the rule. Example: in Which Sense on a phone 667 pixels wide held sideways, the board is 255 pixels wide, and when "· 2 in a row" joins the header, the answer boxes drop 21 pixels, then rise again on the next wrong tap. Choices: (a) keep his rule: the braces come out, and a phone held sideways shows a drawn phone with a turning arrow and the words "Please turn your phone upright." over the whole app until it is turned; (b) retire the rule: the ask's code and its record go, the sideways layouts stay, and the game headers that wrap when a streak shows (about twenty) keep their longest height so nothing moves. What changes: (a) what every phone shows sideways, the ask in place of the app, and the browser tests' sideways checks move to a short laptop window; (b) nothing a phone shows today but the steadier headers; either way a test and a RULES-AUDIT row hold the answer, so it cannot be lost again. Claude recommends (a): it is his rule, made for a reason that still holds, an app designed upright reads best upright, and each pass that measures sideways layouts spends time on a screen a child holding a phone upright never sees.
- Q72. Answered in pass MR (Mikey: "Q72 - When tapping the correct answer the audio says very quickly "the plant is growing" then without a beat enters the next question.  It is way too fast of a transition.  The red wrong click text is good where it is.  Also, see the screenshot for the word sunshine bleeding out through a button and there is another example where the centered text is pushed left by the speaker icon.  I never want this to happen anywhere in the app and try to correct every example I find.  For my answer to your question, I like the location above the tiles and think that holding a place for it is a good idea."): read as (b), the hint's room held above the tiles, and built in with the rest of his words: after a right answer the praise is said to its end and a beat follows, never moving on sooner than 2.2 seconds after the tap; every tile's word fits its tile; a speaker never pushes centered words off center (SpokenRow); and every game is swept for both faults at eight sizes, in a wide font and after a wrong tap (check.sh 13g). The question as it stood: Where Grow the Plant's hint goes after a wrong tap (pass MQ, round five's reads; older than the pass). Where: Grow the Plant, kindergarten's agriculture game, for every learner who opens it, on every device. What a child sees today: after a wrong tap, a hint in red-brown appears between "Tap what the sunflower needs." and the five tiles ("Look again: the plant is droopy, and the soil is dry."), so the tiles drop about 55 pixels on a phone, 32 on a laptop, just as the child tries again, and come back up on the right answer; since this pass they no longer move on a right answer given first. Example: on a phone, a child taps sunshine for a droopy bean; "Look again: the plant is droopy, and the soil is dry." appears above the row of five tiles and pushes it down about 55 pixels, so a finger going back to where the water tile was can land on the hint instead, and nothing happens. Choices: (a) show the hint under the tiles, so nothing above them moves and only what sits below them shifts while it shows; (b) keep it above the tiles but hold its room on every plant, so nothing moves, though the board stands 33 to 55 pixels taller at rest; (c) leave it as it is. What changes: (a) where the hint shows; (b) the board's height on every plant; both change the frozen light theme a little, and the dark theme with it. Claude recommends (a): nothing moves under the child's finger, the board at rest stays as it is, and the hint stays next to the tiles it is about.

<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass MS, word for word: begin -->
- Q73. Answered in pass MS (Mikey: "Q73 - A"): (a), nothing changed: the four Wonder voices' stand-in portraits stay flat and outlined, matching the flat paintings to come (Q33), while the app's other people keep pass MR's more lifelike drawings. The question as it stood: Whether the four Wonder voices' stand-in portraits should be redrawn in the new, more lifelike style (pass MR, after Mikey's "Can we make the images of people look a little more realistic?  I know we will replace them eventually but do the best you can with the placeholders."). Where: the answers screen of every Wonder question, after a learner answers: the answers of the four Wonder voices, Frederick the Scientist, Chloe the Artist, Georgette the Grandparent of Faith and Mike the Skeptic, each under a small round head-and-shoulders portrait the app draws until Leonardo paints it (U2 to U5 in the art ledger). What a learner sees today: flat portraits, outlined in dark ink, in a few plain colors, each with a small badge of how that voice sees things (Frederick a flask, Chloe a palette, Georgette a heart, Mike a question mark), the style Mikey chose for their paintings in Q33 ("If you are talking about the Wonder Question portraits, flat is okay.  We can make a prompt for the flat version of the 3D character so it still matches in a way."). This pass redrew the people the app draws as people to look more like people (the doctor, baby, teacher, grandma and grandpa icons, Whose Turn Next's four friends and the face game's face: real proportions, soft shading on the skin, eyes that catch the light, brows and hair with shape) and left these four flat, because the paintings that will replace them are to be flat; the pre-K lessons' feelings faces stay simple round faces, one clear sign of a feeling each for a three-year-old, and the seesaw's riders and the lemonade stand's customers stay small tokens. Example: a grade 3 learner answers "Is anything true for everyone, everywhere? Or is everything just someone's opinion?" and sees Frederick the Scientist's answer under a flat, outlined drawing of him with a flask badge; with (b) the same drawing would have shaded skin, a light in each eye and shaped hair, like Whose Turn Next's friends. Choices: (a) keep the four stand-ins flat, matching the flat paintings to come; (b) redraw the four stand-ins in the new lifelike style now, while their paintings stay flat as Q33 chose; (c) redraw them and change their painting prompts (U2 to U5) to the lifelike style too, in place of Q33's flat one. What changes: (b) and (c) change the four portraits on every Wonder answer screen in both themes; (c) also changes the four art ledger rows Leonardo will paint from. Claude recommends (a): a stand-in should look like what will replace it, so the Wonder screens do not change style twice, and the flat style was chosen to match the characters' 3D sheets.
- Q74. Answered in pass MS (Mikey: "Q74 - B"): (b), built in: a small "Keep going sideways" link under the ask hides it until the app is closed, kept in memory only and never stored, so a student whose phone is fixed sideways is never locked out (WCAG 2.1's 1.3.4 Orientation), while every other visit still asks. The question as it stood: Whether a phone held sideways may keep going sideways after the ask to turn upright (pass MR, round one's screen check, after Mikey's answer to Q71: "yeah, let's make users turn the phone upright.  I looked at some screens and didn't like the extra scrolling and squeezed content.  Let's make sure it stays too."). Where: every screen of the app, for anyone using a phone held on its side; tablets and laptops never see the ask. What they see today: the screen turns to a plain page with a drawing of a phone turning upright and the words "Please turn your phone upright.", and nothing can be tapped until the phone is turned. Why it is asked: the accessibility guidelines US public schools are asked to meet (WCAG 2.1 at level AA, which the Department of Justice adopted in 2024 for public schools' web content and the apps they provide, vendors' apps included; the rule's deadlines could not be checked online again this pass) have a rule, 1.3.4 Orientation, that a page must not work in only one orientation unless that orientation is essential, so a student whose phone is fixed sideways on a wheelchair or desk mount, or who cannot turn it, could not use the app on that phone at all. Example: a grade 4 student with a phone clamped sideways to a wheelchair tray opens a lesson and sees only "Please turn your phone upright."; with (b) a small link under those words, "Keep going sideways", lets the student carry on until the app is closed, while every other phone still sees the ask. Choices: (a) keep the ask as it is, with no way past it; (b) keep the ask, and add a small "Keep going sideways" link under it that hides it until the app is closed; (c) take the ask away. What changes: (b) adds one link to the ask's page and nothing else; (c) brings back the squeezed sideways layouts Mikey disliked. Claude recommends (b): nearly everyone still turns the phone as Mikey wants, and a student who cannot is never locked out, which is what the guideline asks.
- Q75. Answered in pass MS (Mikey: "Q75 - B"): (b), built in: Which Sense, Share the Cookies, Spot It and Find the Letter hold after a right answer as Grow the Plant does, the praise said to its end (Spot It with a spoken cheer of its own), a beat of 1 second, and the next question never sooner than 2.2 seconds after the tap (afterSaid; each board marked data-right-hold while it holds). The question as it stood: Whether the other young games should wait after a right answer the way Grow the Plant now does (pass MR, round three's alignment read, after Mikey's answer to Q72: "When tapping the correct answer the audio says very quickly "the plant is growing" then without a beat enters the next question.  It is way too fast of a transition."). Where: the four other young games that speak their next question aloud, kindergarten's Which Sense, Share the Cookies and Spot It, and pre-K 4's Find the Letter, played by the youngest children. What a child sees today: after a right tap in Which Sense or Share the Cookies the right answer shows for a moment (Which Sense shows "Yes: your nose.") and the next question is spoken 0.9 seconds after the tap; Spot It speaks its next ask the moment the right shape is tapped; Find the Letter says its praise and, about half a second later, the next letter (Whose Turn Next and Find the Tool move on after 0.9 and 1.1 seconds too, but say nothing aloud unless a child taps Find the Tool's Hear it speaker); Grow the Plant, since this pass, says its praise to the end, waits a beat of 1 second and never moves on sooner than 2.2 seconds after the tap. Example: in Which Sense a child hears "Which sense tells you about a sweet-smelling rose?" and taps the nose; "Yes: your nose." shows for 0.9 seconds, then the next question is spoken; with (b) it would stay for 2.2 seconds first. Choices: (a) leave the other games as they are; (b) give those four games Grow the Plant's hold; (c) give every game the hold. What changes: (b) makes each question in those four games a second or two longer; (c) also slows the older games, many of them races against their own clock. Claude recommends (b): Mikey's reason for Grow the Plant holds for every young game that speaks, and an older game's clock is part of its fun.
- Q76. Answered in pass MS (Mikey: "Q76 - C"): (c), built in: the pre-K plans list every outcome of the 2027 recommendations for each age, domain by domain, each with the lessons that teach it or shown as Not yet covered, and every Head Start preschool goal as well (Claude's reading of the reason Q76 gave for the (c) he chose, raised with him as Q78); both pre-K grades read "Still being written" on the map and its printout until every row is taught, pre-K 3 at 35 of 123 outcomes (26 of 59 Head Start goals) and pre-K 4 at 35 of 132 (27 of 59) after this pass's three lessons. The question as it stood: Whether the standards map should count pre-K against every outcome in the guidelines, not only the ones its lessons teach (pass MR, round one's standards check and round three's alignment read; older than this pass). Where: the educator's Standards map and its printout, the Pre-K 3 and Pre-K 4 rows. What an educator sees today: "Pre-K 3 ... 36 of 36 covered" and "Pre-K 4 ... 34 of 34 covered", because the pre-K plans list only the outcomes the lessons teach so far, 34 of pre-K 3's 123 outcomes in the 2027 recommendations and 34 of pre-K 4's 132; the rest wait in the earmarks list, not on the map. The other grades are counted the same way today: their plans list only the standards their lessons cite (grade 2's lists 24 Texas standards, 7 of them in math, and no grade's plan holds a Texas standard no lesson teaches), so every grade's Texas row reads "N of N covered", though CLAUDE.md and the README describe the plans as listing every published standard; pre-K is the first grade whose every outcome is transcribed (docs/standards) and mapped (EARMARKS). Example: a pre-K director prints the map for a visit and reads "36 of 36 covered" for pre-K 3, though about a quarter of the guidelines' outcomes are taught so far. Choices: (a) leave the count as it is; (b) keep the plans as they are and add a line under each pre-K grade saying how many of the guidelines' outcomes are taught so far (34 of 123); (c) list every outcome of the 2027 recommendations in the pre-K plans, the untaught ones shown as not yet covered, and mark pre-K as still being written until every one is taught. What changes: (b) adds one line to the map; (c) changes the pre-K counts to the true share (34 of 123 and 34 of 132) and makes pre-K the first grade the coverage check reads against its whole framework, the others following as each grade's review transcribes its standards. Claude recommends (c): a printed record should never read as complete when it is not, and pre-K's outcomes are already transcribed and mapped, so pre-K can be counted against them now, with the other grades following grade by grade.
