<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass MT, word for word: begin -->
- Q77. Answered in pass MT (Mikey: "Q77 - A - it is valuable to learning and will sound better with Eleven when we add playful, interesting and silly audio tags.  Instead of "Say different with me" we can change it to Let's say it together!  (In an excited and emphasized voice).  First, let's say it slowly (and draw it out way more slowly).  Now, let's whisper it twice (this whisper should also say it slowly but not as slow as the first slow one).  Now let's say it four times using silly voices (each of the four should use a different pitch, some high, some low, some in different accents like British or Australian but sounding like it's still from the same person and drawn out in various durations).  Now let's say it three times super fast!  (Make it faster than it is now).  Okay, let's try stretching it out now (another version of slow but really emphasizing each part of the word more).  Great job!  One last time, nice and clear.  (This last one is normal speed. - The "say it slowly" should be three times as drawn out as it is now and the four times in a row should have brief pauses between each one too).  Make the other versions of this similar in playful conversational tone and just as silly yet useful."): (a), built in: Things we do keeps its game, every new pre-K vocabulary lesson plays it (a pre-K lesson citing the 2027 II.D.1 or II.D.2 or Head Start's P-LC 6, the eight older ones SAY_ALONG_WAITS names keeping their lines), and every game is his, word for word as he wrote it for different, the other words' versions in the same shape and tone with other silly ways for a step (a sleepy giant, a tiny mouse, a slide, a bounce, a cheer with a whistle and a crowd, play dough). The question as it stood: Whether pre-K 4's new lesson Things we do keeps its say-along game (pass MS, round one's lesson check). Where: First Sounds and Letters, pre-K 4's reading course, the lesson Things we do (action words), for four-year-olds. What a child hears and sees today: three of its lines end in the game Mikey asked for in Q67 and explained in this pass, one word in many silly voices and then clear, with the strip under the line lighting the syllable being said: action (sliding down, a whisper, a cheer), scissors (low, fast, way up high) and lunchtime (stretched out, fast, slowly, a whisper). Why it is asked: Q67's (b), which Mikey chose ("Q67 - B but let's make it fun."), read "add "Say it with me" to the new words of those four lessons only, about thirty words (grandpa, sort, opposite, vehicles and the rest), as a trial you can hear", and he did not choose its (c), "add it wherever a pre-K lesson first teaches a word"; since Q70 the four lessons are eight halves, and giving the game to a new vocabulary lesson reaches toward the (c) he passed over, so it is Claude's reading of his answer, not his words, told to him in this pass. Mikey has not yet heard the game in a lesson: pass MR's silly voices never reached one (found this pass: the lesson read each sentence alone), and Q67's (b) was "a trial you can hear", recommended so "you can hear how it feels in a lesson before it spreads". Example: after "Some words tell what we do. We call them action words. You read a book, you kick a ball, and you sleep in a bed." the voice says "Say action with me, in silly voices! Slide it down, from high to low. Action, action, action. Whisper it. Action. Cheer it like a big game! Action! Action! Action! Now say it nice and clear. Action." Choices: (a) keep the game in Things we do, and give it to every new young vocabulary lesson from now on; (b) keep it in Things we do, and ask again for each new lesson; (c) take it out of Things we do, so only the eight lessons Q67 and Q70 named play it. What changes: (a) and (b) change nothing on the screen today; (c) takes the three games and their strips out of Things we do, and each line ends after its own words. Claude recommends (b): you have not heard the game in a lesson yet, and Q67's (b) was a trial to hear before it spreads, so each new lesson waits for your word until you have; once you have heard it, (a) would save the asking, and the scripts test keeps the game to a list of lessons either way, so it spreads only by a choice.
- Q78. Answered in pass MT (Mikey: "Q78 - A - it's okay to have more than what's required by the state too for better outcomes."): (a), nothing changed: Head Start's goals stay counted in full for a school outside Texas, as Texas's 2027 outcomes are, now his answer rather than Claude's reading, and his reason a standing rule (RULES-AUDIT section 5, more than a state requires). The question as it stood: Whether the standards map counts pre-K against every Head Start goal too, for a school outside Texas (pass MS, after Mikey's answer to Q76). Where: the educator's Standards map and its printout, the Pre-K 3 and Pre-K 4 rows, for a school whose state is not Texas (a Texas school's map shows Texas's 2027 outcomes, any other state's the Head Start goals). What an educator sees today, since this pass: Q76 (c) asked for every outcome of Texas's 2027 recommendations; this pass listed Head Start's 59 preschool goals in full as well, domain by domain, by Claude's reading of the reason Q76 gave for the (c) Mikey chose ("a printed record should never read as complete when it is not"), so a school outside Texas reads pre-K 3 as 26 of 59 goals covered and pre-K 4 as 27 of 59, each with "Still being written", where through pass MR it read every row covered, since the plans held only the goals the lessons cite. Example: a pre-K director in Ohio prints the map for a visit and reads "Pre-K 3 ... 26 of 59 covered" and "Still being written", the 33 goals no lesson teaches yet listed under their domains as Not yet covered. Choices: (a) keep Head Start counted in full, as Texas is; (b) count Head Start by the goals the lessons cite, as before this pass, with Texas still in full. What changes: (b) brings back a full count for a school outside Texas, its map and printout no longer say "Still being written", and the untaught goals leave them. Claude recommends (a): the reason holds for every state's educator, and Head Start's goals are already transcribed word for word (docs/standards), as Texas's outcomes are.
- Q79. Answered in pass MT (Mikey: "Q79 - A"): (a), for the pass that builds II.E: II.E is cited whole once a lesson teaches, models and asks for every part of it, as II.B.1 is. The question as it stood: How the next standards pass cites pre-K's II.E outcomes, which describe a child's own sentences (pass MS, round three's alignment read; asked now so the pass that builds II.E can start from the answer). Where: the educator's Standards map and its printout (a Texas school's map, which lists the 2027 outcomes), and the sources under the new lessons, for pre-K 3 and pre-K 4; II.C and II.E are the next lessons of the standards review. Who meets it: an educator reading the map or a printout. What an educator sees today: II.E.1 to II.E.3 are listed as not yet covered at both ages. They describe what a child does: pre-K 3's II.E.1 reads "typically uses simple sentences of three to four words, usually in correct word order." and pre-K 4's "typically uses complete sentences with increasing length and age-appropriate grammatical complexity, usually in standard word order." The app speaks to a child and never hears one, so a lesson can teach and model such sentences, ask a child to say them along (as the say-along does a word), and ask which of two is in the right order, while the child's own talk happens with a grown-up. The house has read such outcomes two ways: this pass cited pre-K 4's II.B.1 ("uses language for multiple purposes and in various settings") whole, a lesson teaching each purpose with words to say and a child using them the lesson at work, as pre-K 4's II.B.2 is; pass MQ cited II.D.1's using half, a child saying the words, in part, as the child's own talk. Example: a lesson models "I want the ball." and "The dog ran fast.", has the child say each along, and asks which says it in the right order, "The dog ran fast." or "Dog the ran fast."; read whole, the map shows pre-K 3's II.E.1 covered by that lesson; read in part, its citation says the child's own sentences are the child's and the classroom's, and EARMARKS keeps that part waiting as a grown-up's. Choices: (a) cite II.E whole once a lesson teaches, models and asks for every part of it, as II.B.1; (b) cite it in part, as II.D.1's using half, the child's own sentences left to the classroom. What changes: both show the rows covered once the lessons are built; (b) adds "in part" and the part it leaves to each citation and keeps that part on the earmark list. Claude recommends (a): it is the house's reading for an outcome that names no halves (RULES-AUDIT's halves row), it matches how II.B.1 was cited this pass, and a lesson that has the child say each sentence along and pick the one in order teaches what the outcome asks as far as an app can, which the citation can say.
<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass MT, word for word: end -->

<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass MU, word for word: begin -->
- Q80. Answered in pass MU (Mikey: "Q80 - A"): (a), nothing changed: every new word in the nine say-along lessons keeps his whole game, and the eight lessons his answer to Q81 brought in play it whole on every word too. The question as it stood: Whether the longest say-along lessons play Mikey's whole game on every new word (pass MT, after his answer to Q77). Where: pre-K 3's Listen and Point and pre-K 4's First Sounds and Letters, the nine vocabulary lessons that play the say-along, for three- and four-year-olds. What a child hears today, since this pass: his game on every new word, word for word on different and with Claude's silly ways on the others; on a phone's voice each game takes about 30 to 55 seconds, by how fast the voice talks (about 35 at an everyday pace), about twice as long as pass MS's games (about 15 to 35), so Words for people (pre-K 3, six new words: baby, teacher, doctor, grandma, grandpa and people) spends about four minutes saying words together (about three and three quarters to four and a half, by the voice), Count and sort, More learning words and Words that go together (five words each) about three minutes each, and the other five lessons (two or three words) one to two and a half. Next moves on at any moment, for a child or a grown-up. Example: in Words for people a three-year-old hears "Some words name people. This is a baby. A baby is very little, and can drink milk from a bottle. Our word is baby." and then about 35 seconds of saying baby together, then the teacher's line and about 40 seconds of teacher, and so on six times. Choices: (a) keep the whole game on every new word; (b) play the whole game on a lesson's first three new words and a short game on the rest ("Let's say it together!", slowly, three times super fast, "Great job!" and nice and clear, about 15 to 20 seconds); (c) split each lesson of more than three games into two lessons, as Q70 split the long ones. What changes: (a) nothing; (b) Words for people's last three games and the last two of each five-game lesson about 20 seconds shorter each, Words for people about three minutes; (c) four lessons become eight, each with two or three games, and the course, its plans and its dots show the new halves. Claude recommends (a) until you have heard Words for people whole in the preview: your answer to Q70 ("If more educational value or high quality explanatory context comes from extra length, I would always choose the extra length.") favors the length when each step teaches, and each way here does; if it drags for a three-year-old when you hear it, (b) keeps every word's practice and saves about a minute.
- Q81. Answered in pass MU (Mikey: "Q81 - A"): (a), built in: the eight older pre-K vocabulary lessons play his game on 39 words, which words each plays being Claude's reading of "the words each teaches" (the words its citation names as taught, each once in its course: Big and little's big, little, middle-size, biggest and littlest; Listen and tap's ten things; Animal sounds' six sound words; Colors' four colors; Big, bigger, biggest's smallest, middle-sized, biggest, tiny and huge; Helpers all around's five helpers; Which came first?'s first and second; and Second and last's last and middle), and SAY_ALONG_WAITS is empty, held so by the scripts test. The question as it stood: Whether the eight older pre-K vocabulary lessons get Mikey's say-it-together game too (pass MT, round one's alignment read, after his answer to Q77). Where: pre-K 3's Big and little, Listen and tap and Animal sounds, and pre-K 4's Colors, Big, bigger, biggest, Helpers all around, Which came first? and Second and last, for three- and four-year-olds. What a child hears today: each new word said in the lesson's own lines, with no game. Q77's (a) gave the game to Things we do and to every new vocabulary lesson, and these eight were built before it, so a test lets them wait by name. Example: in Colors a four-year-old hears "This color is red, like a strawberry." and the lesson goes on to blue; with the game, "Our word is red." and about 35 seconds of saying red together would come between. Choices: (a) give the eight the game in the next pass, on the words each teaches, with your answer to Q80 deciding how many whole games a lesson plays; (b) give each the game only when a pass next changes that lesson for another reason; (c) leave them as they are. What changes: (a) and (b) add about 35 seconds for each word that gets a game at an everyday pace (30 to 46: Colors' four colors about two and a quarter minutes, Helpers all around's five helpers, with long words like firefighter, about three and a half, and Listen and tap's ten things about five and a half, unless Q80 shortens the later ones); (b) spreads that over passes as lessons are touched; (c) nothing, and the test keeps naming the eight. Some history: in pass MR, Q67 offered (c), the say-along wherever a pre-K lesson first teaches a word, and you chose (b), four lessons as a trial you could hear; Q77 (a) then gave it to every new vocabulary lesson. Claude recommends (a): your answer says the game "is valuable to learning", the eight teach the same kind of words as the nine that play it, and doing them together with your answer to Q80 keeps every vocabulary lesson the same shape.
<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass MU, word for word: end -->

<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass MV, word for word: begin -->
- Q82. Answered in pass MV (Mikey: "Q82: A"): (a), nothing changed yet: once the recordings are made (after the standards review, Q61), a few takes of the cheer's whistle and of its crowd are made with ElevenLabs' sound-effects tool, Mikey picks one of each by ear, and the app plays them in place of its own, keeping its own wherever a file is missing (EARMARKS A60). The question as it stood: Whether the say-along cheer's whistle and crowd come from ElevenLabs' sound effects when the recordings are made (pass MU, after your notes on the voice check page, "65, the whistle gets cut off too quickly." and "66 the clapping sounds like static.", and your line after the rows you could not hear, "Doesn't matter too much since we will be using Eleven Labs."). Where: the say-it-together game's cheer, one of the four ways of its third step, in 20 games across 16 pre-K lessons, for three- and four-year-olds. What a child hears today: the voice cheers the word three times like a crowd at a big game, with a fan's whistle between the cheers and a crowd clapping after the last, both made by the app from numbers; since this pass the whistle is drawn out on one breath that swells and fades, nine tenths of a second, and the crowd is 44 people clapping in a small hall for two and a half seconds with two whistles from the crowd, each measured against recordings of the real thing, but a made crowd is still a stand-in, and it has no voices cheering in it (pass MT's had a hush of voices under its claps). Example: in Helpers all around a four-year-old hears "Now let's cheer it like we're at a big game! Firefighter!", a whistle, "Firefighter!", a whistle, "Firefighter!", and the crowd. Choices: (a) when the recordings are made, Claude asks ElevenLabs' sound-effects tool for a few takes of each (one fan's two-finger whistle on a single breath, about a second; about forty people clapping and cheering in a school gym, about two and a half seconds), you pick one of each by ear, and the app plays them in place of its own, keeping its own wherever a file is missing; (b) keep the app's own whistle and crowd, and Claude keeps tuning them to your notes on the voice check page, first putting back under the claps the hush of cheering voices you called decent; (c) drop the whistle and the crowd, so the cheer is its three sayings alone. What changes: the screen is the same in every choice; (a) changes nothing until the recordings are made, then the two sounds are real recordings, with a few minutes of listening for you; (b) brings back the hush of voices under the claps, and each later change waits for your ear; (c) makes the cheer about four seconds shorter (its two whistles of nine tenths of a second and the crowd's two and a half) and plainer, the most excited of the third step's four ways without its sounds. Claude recommends (a): a crowd recorded once, cheering as well as clapping, will sound more real than any crowd made from numbers, the recordings are coming anyway (Q61), and the app's own sounds stay as the fallback, so nothing goes quiet if a file is missing.
<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass MV, word for word: end -->

<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass MX, word for word: begin -->
- Q83. Answered in pass MX (Mikey: "Q83 - A (I want the correct form and don't want to make it confusing)"): (a), built in: the seven question kinds keep "Which one says it right?" and their reasons, and every form the questions offer that we do not say is now said in its lesson first, as the form we do not say (RULES-AUDIT section 5's row of pass MW, which (a) now holds for every form): One cat, two cats says "When there is just one, we say one cup, and not, one cups. And for two, we say two cups, and not, two cup." and "We say, yesterday I kicked the ball, and not, yesterday I kick the ball."; Words that change, the longest pre-K lesson at sixteen lines, would have passed twenty with its forms said first, so by Mikey's Q70 it became two whole lessons, Words that change (plurals and the past tense) and She, he and they (pronouns, agreement and am, is and are), each with five kinds, a story, a coloring page and a painting; Claude's checks, not the answer, reworded two yes or no questions ("Do we say, the two bus stopped?" for "Do we say, there are two bus?", and "Do we say, one fish, two fish?") and the reasons of three more. The question as it stood: Whether pre-K's grammar questions keep asking "Which one says it right?" (pass MW, round one's lesson check; rewritten from round two's content and alignment reads). Where: pre-K 3's One cat, two cats and pre-K 4's Words that change, practice for three- and four-year-olds. What a child meets today: seven question kinds show two sentences, one in the form the lesson teaches and one not, and ask "Which one says it right?"; after a wrong tap the voice says why and asks for another try ("Saying, one dog run, does not fit one dog. With one dog, we say runs. Have another try."), which More Info shows again under Next after the right tap; and three more kinds ask about the same forms, two yes or no kinds answered No ("Do we say, there are three ball?", "Do we say, yesterday I goed to the park?", "Do we say, there are two bus?") and "Which one tells what already happened?", whose reason says that "I kick the ball." does not tell it already happened. Why it is a question: most of the forms these questions mark wrong are among the forms that Analyzing Dialects of English (the pages of Dr. Oetting's D4 Child Language Lab at Louisiana State University) lists as described in the literature as possible in African American English and Southern White English, both heard in Texas: a verb's -s left off (here, one dog run), a verb's -s with a plural subject (here, two dogs runs), is with a plural subject (here, we is happy), a plural's -s left off after a number (here, there are two dog), a past -ed left off (here, yesterday I kick the ball) and a regular past for an irregular verb (here, yesterday I goed to the park); the -s of runs has been studied in four children three to five who speak African American English ("Language Use and Development in Third-Person Singular Contexts: Assessment Implications", Language, Speech, and Hearing Services in Schools 52(1), 2021). Pre-K 3's Texas outcome asks for the forms used correctly (II.E.2, "begins to use regular plurals and regular past tense correctly."); pre-K 4's 2027 words dropped the 2022 guidelines' "correctly uses" and ask for "a variety of age-appropriate grammatical forms (e.g. plurals, past tense, pronouns, subject-verb agreement) in spoken language.", which does not say whose forms (Claude reads it as the school forms). Longer sentences' whole-sentence question offered "The duck in the pond." as the part of a sentence, a whole sentence where is is left out (Labov, Language, 1969); since this pass its parts are ones no dialect makes whole ("The big brown dog."), so that question is not part of this one. The lessons' story All done! already frames a form the way (b) would: Femi's "I paint a picture!" is never called wrong; Dad just cannot tell it already happened. Example: in Words that change, a four-year-old who hears "my brother run fast" at home taps "One dog run." and hears that it does not fit one dog. Choices: (a) keep "Which one says it right?" as it is, which pre-K 3's "correctly" supports and which asks a three-year-old to think about no home or school; (b) say it as the way we talk at school: "Which one do we say at school?" in the seven prompts, "At school, do we say ...?" in the yes or no questions and "At school, ..." in the reasons a child hears ("At school, with one dog, we say runs."), with a sentence for grown-ups in each lesson that these forms are regular in many homes and the lesson teaches the school form (the words are yours: "at school" may read oddly to a homeschooling family, the first audience docs/VISION.md names, where "in our lessons" would do as well); (c) leave out every question whose wrong form is on that list, which keeps about a quarter of One cat, two cats' questions (12 of its 44: the seven about one thing, "Do we say, there is one cups?" and four other yes or no questions) and less than half of Words that change's (20 of 45: its eight pronoun questions, four about one thing, two agreement sentences and six yes or no questions), and the past tense almost nothing at either age. Under (a) and (b) a child who taps the home form misses the question, which counts toward mastery as any miss does. What changes on the screen: (a) nothing; (b) the words of the prompts, the yes or no questions and the reasons in those ten kinds, and a sentence in two lessons' grown-up text, the answers the same; (c) three quarters of One cat, two cats' questions and more than half of Words that change's gone, or replaced with new kinds. Claude's recommendation: (b), since it teaches exactly the forms the outcomes ask for, as the way we talk at school, without telling a child that the way their family talks is wrong; the voice the app uses with families, and its exact words, are yours to choose.

<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass MZ, word for word: begin -->
- Q84. Answered in pass MZ (Mikey: "Q84 - B"): (b), built in: each of the 57 pre-K lessons that named a prerequisite in pass MY names the lesson its ideas build on, or none, with its reason in requiresWhy beside it, naming each lesson it requires by its title; 36 keep the lesson they named, 10 name another, the lesson their idea builds on (Count the words needs Longer sentences, Words in a sentence Say it in a sentence, Learning words Two ideas, Two steps Listen and tap, Longer sentences Things we do, Trace L, T and F Trace a big letter, and Match the things, the water animals, the land animals and the shapes Same and different), and 11 name none (Triangles, Big and small, A voice to fit the place, Words that rhyme, My voice goes up and down, Say it in a sentence, Say it to fit, Which came first?, More learning words, Things we do and My voice tells more); every new prerequisite is one the lesson needed in pass MY's graph, directly or through the lessons those needed, so no lesson open for a child who followed the gates locks, which the rules test proves by walking 300 children through pass MY's gates and reading every lesson open to them on the screen's own rule, though two kinds of educator action can still lock one of the ten relinked lessons (section 7's Q84 row; Q87); nine of the lessons that changed were built before pass MO, past the lessons built since pass MO that the question's What each changes named, as the choice's own words, "for each pre-K lesson", cover; and the lessons built since name what they build on the same way (section 7). The question as it stood: Whether each pre-K lesson names, as what it requires, the lesson its ideas build on (raised in pass MY, from Mikey's question on how a split lesson affects mastery and memory checks). Where: the pre-K lesson lists, Very First Steps and Listen and Point for pre-K 3, First Steps and First Sounds and Letters for pre-K 4. A three- or four-year-old and the grown-up beside them meet it at two moments: when a lesson shows locked, and when a child who missed a lesson twice is sent back to another lesson for a check (loop back, and after a missed Quick Review the lessons the reopened lesson requires come first in the memory checks). What they meet today: pre-K courses are sets of parallel activities (linear: false), so a lesson locks only behind the lessons its requires list names, and loop back sends a child to the last one it names. Since pass MO each new pre-K lesson has named the one built just before it (43 of the 57 pre-K lessons that name one), so the lists follow the order lessons were built, not what they teach. Example: Count the words opens only after 14 lessons in a row are passed, among them three-step directions, My voice tells more and the grammar lessons, none of which counting words needs; it required Words that change until pass MX's split and She, he and they since, so a four-year-old who misses counting words twice is sent back to pronouns, must pass that again, and returns with no help on counting; in pre-K 3, a child who keeps missing Words in a sentence goes back to One cat, two cats. Choices: (a) keep the lists as they are, so lessons open in the order they were built and loop back goes to the lesson built before; (b) name for each pre-K lesson the lesson its ideas build on, or none (Count the words would need Longer sentences, since it counts the words of a sentence and needs what a sentence is; Words in a sentence would need Say it in a sentence; She, he and they keeps Words that change), each link with a written reason a test holds, chosen so that no lesson already open for a child locks; a child can then tap more open lessons in each skill group, still listed in course order, and a child who keeps missing goes back to a real cause; (c) keep the build-order lists for locking, and give loop back a separate pointer to the lesson a struggling child should revisit. What each changes: (a) nothing; (b) which pre-K lessons are open at once and where loop back sends a child, for the lessons built since pass MO; (c) loop back alone, with a second list kept in step with the first by hand. Claude recommends (b): locking and loop back should answer the same question, what does this lesson need, and today the answer is whatever was built last; (c) keeps that answer for locking and adds a second copy to keep in step, which the house rule against two copies warns about. The trade-off with (b) is that a group may show four or five open lessons at once instead of one. Pass MY's two new lessons name what they build on, which for them is also the lesson built before (Two words make one needs Words in a sentence, Clap the beats needs Count the words), and pre-K's III.B.2, words that sound alike or the same, waits for this answer, since by build order and by idea its lessons would need different ones.
- Q85. Answered in pass MZ (Mikey: "Q85 - B"): (b), built in: a row of the rules audit that no longer says what the code does is corrected in place once section 9 names it, its old words kept word for word here, in a block of their own held by a digest; pass MZ corrected section 7's rows on the round, on when a module opens, on memory checks (pass LD's "the extra question") and on reflections, the last sentence of pass MY's memory-check row, and section 2's line of pass MY, which said Q84 and Q85 ask (section 9). The question as it stood: Whether a row of the rules audit (docs/RULES-AUDIT.md) that no longer says what the code does may be corrected in place once its section 9 records the change, as your answer to Q53 ("Q53 - B") allows for a kept CLAUDE.md line (raised in pass MY). Where: docs/RULES-AUDIT.md, the file every chat reads first for the standing rules; no child or educator sees it. What it holds today: a few rows written in earlier passes that the code has since outgrown, kept word for word because its own rule says nothing in it is deleted or changed without your approval. Example: section 7's row of pass LD says a mastered module's questions return as "the extra question" in later rounds, six times in ten from the same course, where since Mastery v2 a round asks two, from every lesson passed and not only the starred ones, the first leaning toward the lessons behind a missed Quick Review and then toward lessons missed lately; pass MY, answering your question on split lessons, found it, corrected docs/ALGORITHMS.md (which said the same), and added a new row beside the old one saying what the code does. Choices: (a) keep every old row word for word, with a new row beside it whenever it goes stale (as today); (b) correct a stale row in place once section 9 names it, its old words kept word for word in docs/RULES-AUDIT-HISTORY.md, as CLAUDE.md works since Q53; (c) ask you each time. What each changes: (a) nothing, and a reader must notice the newer row; (b) the file stays true row by row, with nothing lost, since the old words are kept and named; (c) a question for every stale row. Claude recommends (b): a chat reads this file to learn the rules, and a row that says one where the code asks two misleads the next chat, while the history keeps every old word for your check.

- Q86. Answered in pass NA (Mikey: "Q86 - yes, B"): (b), built in: docs/VISION.md and section 1 of the rules audit say what the app does, their aims and the heading Master, then move kept: a module opens once everything it needs is passed, in one round, never added up across rounds, unless an educator chooses to open it, and mastery, the star, is a second pass on another day; a reflection comes after every two different lessons worked, however many rounds each took, and after a hard round, one not passed or with three misses in a row, the failure and feelings questions come first and the hard-days ones next; and How to tell if a change is right calls a change wrong if it lets a student move past something not passed. Section 1's heading says it keeps the vision in short. Their old words are kept word for word below, held by a digest, and the docs sync test holds both texts to the gate, the star and the reflection rule the code has; a sweep for the two rules found the learner's My progress screen saying a lesson's best score needs 4 "to master it" and a locked one "Opens when the module before it is mastered.", both now saying what the app does (section 9). The question as it stood: Whether docs/VISION.md, and section 1 of docs/RULES-AUDIT.md, which keeps it in short, may say what the app does where two of its rules no longer do (raised in pass MZ, while correcting the rows Q85 names). Where: the vision every chat reads before judging a change; no child or educator sees it. What it says today, under What the product must always do: "Master, then move. A module opens only when everything it needs is mastered. Mastery is measured in one sitting, never patched together across days.", and "Reflection questions come round after every second finished round, pass or fail"; and, as the last sentence of How to tell if a change is right, "If it lets a student move past something they have not mastered, it is wrong." What the app does: since Mastery v2 (September 14, 2026) one passing round opens what needs a lesson, and the star, mastery, is a second pass on another day; and since your rule restated on September 28 a Wonder question comes after every two different lessons worked, however many rounds each took. Example: a four-year-old who passes Count the words once today can open Clap the beats today, though Count the words gets its star only on another day; and a child who fails one lesson twice in a row has worked one lesson, so no Wonder question comes yet. Choices: (a) keep the sentences as they are; (b) correct them to say what the app does, keeping their aim and the heading Master, then move (a module opens once everything it needs is passed, and mastery is a second pass on another day; a reflection after every two lessons worked; a change is wrong if it lets a student move past something they have not passed), their old words kept in docs/RULES-AUDIT-HISTORY.md; (c) change the app back to what they say. What each changes: (a) nothing, and a reader who judges a change by the vision is told two things the app does not do; (b) the vision and the app agree, with nothing lost; (c) the next lesson would wait for a star on a second day, which you decided against in Mastery v2, and a reflection would again come after every second finished round, pass or fail. Claude recommends (b): every chat judges a change by this text, so it should describe the app you built, and the old words stay for your check. The README said the same two things and is corrected in pass MZ, since it has no rule that keeps its words.
- Q87. Answered in pass NA (Mikey: "Q87 - A"): (a), nothing in the app changed: the two kinds of educator action that can lock one of the ten relinked lessons, a hand unlock on a lesson's old path or an erase of the lesson it now builds on, stay as docs/ALGORITHMS.md and section 7's Q84 row write them, each now saying Mikey kept it so, and a relinked lesson opens again when the lesson it builds on is passed or moved past, or when the educator opens it by hand. The question as it stood: Whether a pre-K lesson that pass MZ relinked by idea should also open, as it did before, when the lesson it required until pass MZ is passed (raised in pass MZ, round one's code check). Where: a pre-K child's lesson list, and an educator who opens or erases a lesson by hand. What happens today: ten pre-K lessons now require the lesson their ideas build on, in place of the lesson built before them (Count the words requires Longer sentences, where it required She, he and they). For a child who followed the gates, nothing that was open closes; the rules test walks 300 children through pass MY's gates to prove it. But two kinds of educator action can now leave one of the ten locked: opening by hand (Unlock it anyway) its old prerequisite, or a lesson that one needed, for a child who then passes it or is moved past it, while the new one was never passed; or erasing the new prerequisite (Yes, erase it) after the old one was passed. Example: an educator opens She, he and they by hand for a four-year-old who passes it; under pass MY's links Count the words would open, and now it stays locked until Longer sentences is passed or the educator opens Count the words itself by hand. Choices: (a) keep it as it is, the two kinds of case written in docs/ALGORITHMS.md and section 7's Q84 row; (b) let each of the ten also open when its pass MY prerequisite is passed, a second way in that only the gate reads (loop back keeps the new link), the walk adding an unlock and an erase; (c) something else you name. What each changes: (a) nothing a child who follows the gates sees, and an educator who overrides a gate may find one of the ten locked and open it with one tap; (b) your words for Q84 (b), no lesson already open for a child locks, hold in every case, at the cost of a second list on ten lessons that every later reader must understand. Claude recommends (a): both cases start with an educator overriding a gate, the way back is one tap, and a second way in would keep pass MY's build-order links alive in the gate, the very thing Q84 set out to remove.

Answered in pass ND (Mikey: "Q88 - A"): (a), kept as built: in pre-K 3's Sounds in a word and pre-K 4's The very first sound and Sound by sound every hiss plays one breath of 0.32 seconds, in their lines, their stories and their banks' questions wherever those are asked (ONE_BREATH_LESSONS and oneBreathOn), and the audio ledger asks Eleven for one breath there; nothing in the app changed for the answer, and section 2's line since pass NC, section 4's row of pass NC and section 6's row on the short breaths, which said Q88 asks, are corrected in place (section 9). The question as it stood: A hiss said as one breath in the three phoneme lessons (pass NC, round one's lesson check). Where: pre-K 3's Sounds in a word (Listen and Point) and pre-K 4's The very first sound and Sound by sound (First Sounds and Letters): their lesson lines, their questions wherever they are asked, and their stories, for three- and four-year-olds. What a child heard before: your pass MP note suggested that the app's sss, fff and zzz play as short fragments rather than one long hiss ("sss sss sun"), so a hiss said alone plays two breaths of about a third of a second each with a little quiet between. In these lessons that went wrong: The very first sound says "But the very first sound of frog is just one sound. Fff.", and the child heard fff, fff, two breaths, right after being told it is one sound; and in a question about frog the right answer, fff, played twice, while the wrong one, fff-rrr, played once. What a child hears since pass NC: in these three lessons every hiss is one breath of about a third of a second, still a short fragment and never the long hiss you heard as static, as a hiss inside a word's sounds ("Sss, oh, puh.") plays in every lesson since this pass, except grade 1's Read the word and Sh, ch and th, whose short vowels are on trial ("Sss, i, ks.", "Fff, i, shh."), until EARMARKS A42; a hiss alone in every other lesson keeps its two breaths, and the audio ledger asks Eleven for one breath in these three lessons too. Choices: (a) keep one breath in the three phoneme lessons, as built; (b) two breaths everywhere, as your pass MP note had it, with the lines that say "just one sound" reworded so they no longer say it beside a hiss, a hiss inside a word's sounds ("Sss, oh, puh.") keeping its one breath, so that in these lessons a lone Sss would be two breaths and the Sss in soap's run one; (c) something else you describe. Claude recommends (a), since these lessons teach a child to hear one sound as one, and one breath is still the short fragment your note suggested.

Answered in pass NE (Mikey: "Q89 - A"): (a), kept as built: in pre-K 3's Letters and sounds and pre-K 4's Hear it in the name and At the end of the name every hiss plays one breath of 0.32 seconds, in their lines, their stories and their banks' questions wherever those are asked (ONE_BREATH_LESSONS and oneBreathOn), and the audio ledger asks Eleven for one breath there; nothing in the app changed for the answer, and section 2's line since pass ND and section 4's row of pass ND, which said Q89 asks, are corrected in place (section 9). The question as it stood: One breath for a hiss in the three letters lessons (pass ND; Claude's design, built as (a)). Where: pre-K 3's Letters and sounds (Listen and Point) and pre-K 4's Hear it in the name and At the end of the name (First Sounds and Letters): their lesson lines, their questions wherever they are asked, and their stories, for three- and four-year-olds learning the sound each letter says (S says sss, F says fff, Z says zzz). What a child hears in the rest of the app: since your pass MP note ("sss sss sun") a hiss said alone plays as two short breaths of about a third of a second each, and since your answer to Q88 every hiss in the three phoneme lessons plays as one, since a lone Fff in two breaths was heard as two sounds where a line said "just one sound". What a child hears in these lessons since pass ND: every hiss is one breath of about a third of a second, as in the phoneme lessons, since each lesson teaches that a letter says one sound; At the end of the name's fourth line, "S says sss, the sound at the end of its name.", plays one sss, and "Which sound does it say?" plays one breath for each hiss it offers. The audio ledger asks Eleven for one breath there too. Claude recommends (a): a letter's sound is one sound, a child who hears S say sss, sss may count two, and one breath matches the phoneme lessons the letters lessons build on.

- Q90. Answered in pass NF (Mikey: "Q90 - A"): (a), built: every one of the four colors wears a thin edge of its own shade in both themes, on the answer tiles and on a lesson's paper alike, darker where it sits on something light (white, the light theme's tiles and the dark theme's paper) and lighter on the dark theme's tiles, each 3 to 1 or better on every surface it sits on and the fills unchanged (SWATCH_EDGE and SWATCH_TILE_EDGE in src/ui.jsx, run by the scripts test); Q56's yellow and green edges and pass ND's red and blue tile edges are four of the eight, the other four pass NF's; the Wonder answer's little color square wears the tile's edge of its own color too; section 2's pass MP line and section 8's pass MP row and tile row, which said a color takes an edge only where it fell short, are corrected in place (section 9). The question as it stood: A thin edge on all four colors of the colored shapes and squares (pass ND's round one screen read, EARMARKS A94; a question of taste, raised in pass NE). Where: the color lessons and questions of pre-K 3 and pre-K 4 (Red and blue, Yellow and green, Colors, and any question whose answers are colored shapes or color squares), and wherever else the same shapes and squares are drawn, kindergarten's Our flag and holidays and grade 1's Read the word, Read the sentence, Flat shapes and Rules, laws and signs among them, so (a) changes those too, on the answer tiles and on a lesson's paper, in both themes, for children from pre-K 3 to grade 1, most of all three- and four-year-olds learning to name red, blue, yellow and green. What a child sees today: since your answer to Q56 every yellow shape and square wears a thin darker yellow edge in both themes, and on the dark theme's paper the green ones a deeper green edge; since pass ND a color got an edge on an answer tile only where it read under 3 to 1 there, the green on the light theme's tiles and the red and the blue on the dark theme's tiles (a lighter rim there, since on a dark tile only a lighter edge shows). So on a light tile the yellow and the green are framed while the red and the blue are flat, and on a dark tile the yellow, the red and the blue are rimmed while the green is flat. For example, in the light theme Yellow and green's "Tap the red one." over a red, a green and a blue square shows the green square framed and the other two flat; in the dark theme the same question shows the red and the blue rimmed and the green flat. Choices: (a) a thin edge on all four colors in both themes, on tiles and paper alike, each a shade of its own color that reads where it sits (darker on a light tile or paper, lighter on a dark tile), so the four read as one set; (b) keep it as is, an edge only where a color needs one to read; (c) something else you describe. Claude recommends (a): four colors drawn one way read as one set, an edge in a color's own shade never changes the color a child names, and an edge on some colors and not others can look like a hint.
<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass NF, word for word: end -->

<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass NG, word for word: begin -->
- Q91. Answered in pass NG (Mikey: "Q91 - A"): (a), built: on a device with no voice the 24 letters banks GROWN_UP_CARD_BANKS names (pass NF had counted 22; round one's reads found Write it by its sounds' last letter and yes or no) show a card for the grown-up where their answers go, the whole question to read aloud and a Ready button that hides the words and shows the answers, Check answer waiting for it, a which-sound question's buttons numbered in the order the card lists its sounds, and Show the words again bringing the card back; what prints above the answers is what a device with a voice prints, one print for every seed of a bank but Write it by its sounds' yes or no, which prints first or at the end (the rules test; tests/e2e/grown-up-card.mjs, check.sh step 13n); EARMARKS A96 done. The question as it stood: Letters questions on a device with no voice (pass NF's round one screen read, EARMARKS A96; raised in pass NF). Where: on a phone, tablet or computer whose voice is off or missing, a grown-up reads each question aloud to the child. That reaches the letters questions of pre-K 3's Letters and sounds and Look and write's question about a name heard, and pre-K 4's Hear it in the name, At the end of the name, Start or end of the name, Write it by its sounds and Hear it in a word: 22 kinds of question in seven lessons, for three- and four-year-olds learning which letter says which sound. What a child sees today: with no voice the app prints the sound or the word the voice would have said, so the grown-up can read it out, and where the answers are letters, its capital is the answer. For example, Hear it in a word shows "Listen to this sound. Cuh. Which letter says that sound?" over the letters C, W and H, where with a voice it shows "Listen to the sound. Which letter says the sound you hear?" and the voice says cuh. A child who cannot yet hear which letter says cuh, but can match shapes, taps the C because Cuh starts with it. Read over seeds 1 to 400, matching the printed capital answers 17 of the 22 kinds: 13 every time, the letter questions of Hear it in the name and Start or end of the name every time but on K's and X's (their sounds are written cuh and cuh-sss, which start with neither letter, so a capital can be matched on the other 318 and 309 of the 400 seeds), and those two lessons' yes or no questions 90 and 89 times in 100, where chance is 33 to 50. The other five show a letter and ask which sound it says, and print the sounds on the answer buttons in small letters (huh, chuh and wuh under W), so a child who matches shapes finds the answer where the small letter looks like the big one (wuh under W, sss under S, on 71 to 189 of the 400 seeds), and a child who knows each capital's small letter finds it every time but on K's and X's in Hear it in the name and Start or end of the name (K says cuh and X says cuh-sss, which start with neither small letter: 318 and 309 of the 400 seeds). So on such a device these lessons can be passed without the skill they teach. Holding these kinds out of a round on such a device would leave five of the seven lessons one kind of question and the other two four, where every lesson asks at least five kinds. Kindergarten's Letter sounds shows its word written with a voice too, so it is not part of this question (EARMARKS A111). Choices: (a) with no voice, these questions show a card where the answers go, headed For the grown-up, with the words to say and a Ready button; the grown-up reads the words aloud and taps Ready, which hides the words and shows the answers, so the words and the answers are never on the screen together, and in the five which-sound questions the card lists the sounds in the buttons' order for the grown-up to say, two in Letters and sounds and three in the others, and the buttons show their numbers, 1 and 2 or 1, 2 and 3, in place of the sounds; every lesson stays open and every round stays full, at the cost of one tap more for the grown-up on each of these questions, and the build adds a flag these banks set, a rules check that nothing the child can see beside the answers holds the answer's capital or its sound, and a browser check; (b) the six letters lessons, all but Look and write, wait for a device with a voice: a child on a device with no voice could not pass them, Look and write, which requires Letters and sounds, would stay locked, and since kindergarten's courses stay out of sight while any pre-K lesson is unmastered, that child would not see kindergarten on that device; (c) keep it as it is. Claude recommends (a): it keeps every lesson open on every device, and the child answers by the sound the grown-up says, as the lessons mean, for one extra tap a question.
- Q92. Answered in pass NG (Mikey: "Q92 - B"): (b), built: the page's first script, written in ES5, puts a note in place of the Loading word when the app cannot start (its script unreadable, or stopped before its first screen), never once the app has drawn; three calls to Object.hasOwn were written the older way, so the app runs where the note says it does; the README names Safari 16.4 (iPadOS and iOS 16.4), Chrome and Edge 80 and Firefox 78; tests/e2e/too-old.mjs (check.sh step 13o) holds the scripts to ES5, the built page to no newer feature and the note to showing only when the app cannot start; EARMARKS A113 done. The question as it stood: The oldest iPads and iPhones (pass NF, EARMARKS A113, found when the advisor read round two's code fix; raised in pass NF). Where: anyone who opens the app's page, from GitHub Pages or a school's own web host, on an iPad that stops at iPadOS 15 (the iPad Air 2, from 2014, and the iPad mini 4, from 2015), on an iPhone or iPod touch that stops at iOS 15 (the first iPhone SE, the iPhone 6s and 6s Plus, the iPhone 7 and 7 Plus, and the seventh and last iPod touch), or in any Safari older than 16.4, which came out in March 2023. What they see today: the page's Loading word, its letters bobbing, and nothing after it. The app never starts, and nothing says why. Since pass MN the rule that decides where a sentence ends (SENTENCE_BREAK) is written with a kind of pattern called a lookbehind, and so are three other lines (a lesson's sentences, a line of an educator page and, since pass NF, the line that makes a count of one read "1 dot is"), and a browser that cannot read a lookbehind stops the whole app before its first screen. So does a Chrome or Edge before version 80 or a Firefox before 78, all from 2020, since the page also uses a newer pattern that Chrome and Edge read only from 80. Every iPad Pro, every iPad from the 5th generation on, every iPad Air from the 3rd and every iPad mini from the 5th, and every iPhone from the iPhone 8 on can update to iPadOS or iOS 16.4 or later, which reads those four lines, as about 97 percent of the browsers in use worldwide do (caniuse.com). On the older iPads and iPhones another browser does not help: Apple's App Store rule 2.5.6 says "Apps that browse the web must use the appropriate WebKit framework and WebKit JavaScript", so Chrome on an iPad Air 2 reads the page as its Safari does. A Mac that stops at macOS Catalina or earlier cannot get Safari 16.4 either, but there Chrome and Firefox use their own engines. For example, a class with a cart of iPad Air 2s opens the page, and every one of them shows Loading and goes no further, while a newer iPad on the same cart, kept up to date, opens the app. No check reads which browsers the page loads on, nobody has opened it on such a device, and whether the page uses anything else those browsers lack is unread, so writing the four lines another way might not be enough. Choices: (a) try to serve those devices: the four lines are written another way that splits and tidies exactly as they do now (the rules test and the browser tests hold what they split and fix), a check holds the built page to no lookbehind, and the rest of the page is read against the oldest Safari to serve, since only that read can say whether the page then runs on iPadOS 15 (how a page of almost 12 megabytes runs on an iPad more than ten years old is unread too), and anything more that read finds comes back to Mikey before more is changed; (b) say so plainly: on a browser that cannot run the app, a small script that runs before the app's own puts a note where the Loading word is, for example "This device or browser is too old for The Wise Human. On an iPad or iPhone, it needs iPadOS or iOS 16.4 or later, and another browser there will not help. Some older iPads and iPhones, such as the iPad Air 2, cannot update that far. On a computer, open it in a current Chrome, Edge, Firefox or Safari.", the README names the same versions, a check holds the note's script to what an old browser can read and sees the note show when the app cannot start, and nothing changes on a device where the app runs; (c) keep it as it is, with EARMARKS A113 saying why. Claude recommends (b): a teacher with those iPads learns at once why the app does not open and what will open it, and a newer iPad that has not been updated is told to update, where today the Loading word asks them to wait, and nothing changes on a device where the app runs; (a) changes the rule every voice, read-along and lesson line splits by, and needs a read no device here can test, so it is worth its cost if Mikey knows of schools or families he means to serve that still use these devices, and it can follow (b) in any later pass.
- Q93. Answered in pass NG (Mikey: "Q93 - A", mid-pass): (a), the Guide, planned and not yet built: between lessons one of the four Wonder voices says one short tip chosen by rules from the device's own record, every tip written and checked in a pass, nothing new stored, no network, nothing to pay for; built as a program after the pre-K earmarks, starting with the letters lessons (EARMARKS L21). The question as it stood: A personal guide for each learner, in place of an AI tutor (pass NG, from Mikey's question: "What would it take for me to introduce a personalized AI tutor for each student and what might that look like?  How would that fit in to student privacy concerns?"; raised in pass NG). Where: between lessons, on a learner's own screens, after a round's result and when a course opens, with a matching sentence on the educator's report. Who: every learner from pre-K 3 up, and their educator. What they see today: after a round, the result and a line of encouragement, for a pre-reader a story as the reward, and a Wonder question after every two lessons worked; the educator's report says how each lesson went and lists Action Items; nothing yet speaks to a child about their own pattern of mistakes. A chat tutor like Khanmigo would break each of Mikey's four conditions: it needs a server to keep its API key off the device (cloud hosting); it sends each child's words to a model company, which under the FTC's amended COPPA Rule (effective June 23, 2025, compliance by April 22, 2026) needs separate verifiable parental consent for a disclosure to a third party that is not integral to the service, a written information security program and a written data retention policy; it costs per message (the Ultra plan's $200 a month of API credits are for building apps, expire each billing cycle and stop the requests when used up); and it shows a child words no reader checked, which rule 5 forbids. Chrome's on-device model reaches web pages only on desktop computers with 22 GB free (Chrome 148), not iPads or phones. The evidence: a two-year randomized trial of Khanmigo in 18 Tennessee middle schools found about 0.06 to 0.08 standard deviations a school year, gains that "resemble those from Khan Academy practice without AI assistance", and students messaged it in 17 percent of the sessions in which they made a mistake ("The binding constraint appears to be engagement"; EdWorkingPaper 26-1551, August 2026, not peer reviewed); Alpha School's software is adaptive mastery practice, and it "does not use chatbots" (DeepLearning.AI's The Batch, September 10, 2025). For example, with (a): a four-year-old chose duh for B twice yesterday in Hear it in the name; today, when the course opens, Georgette says, "B and D got mixed up a little. Let's say them slowly. Buh, ball. Duh, dog.", with the ball and the dog on the screen, and the educator's report says, "Chose duh for B twice. Try saying ball and dog together at snack." Choices: (a) a Guide, with no AI at run time: rules in src/logic.mjs choose one short tip from the device's own record (a wrong choice a child keeps picking, a skill missed twice, a return after days away, a streak, a review that is due), every tip written and checked in a pass like the stories and said by one of the four Wonder voices, only the facts it fills in (a lesson's name, a count) taken from the record; nothing new stored, no network, nothing to pay for, a few hundred kilobytes; built as a program after the pre-K earmarks, starting with the letters lessons, whose wrong choices already carry reasons, and measured from the record (after a B and D tip, does a child choose duh for B less often?); (b) a chat tutor like Khanmigo, with the costs above; (c) an on-device model for older students on desktop Chrome only, off by default, rewording explanations already checked, read again in 6 to 12 months; (d) nothing for now. Claude recommends (a): no trial tests tips like these directly, but the trials found that children rarely ask a tutor for help, and a guide speaks first; it keeps every promise the app makes about privacy and accuracy, costs nothing to run, and the app can measure whether it helps.
- Q94. Answered in pass NG (Mikey: "Q94 - C", mid-pass): (c), no Hint button for now; hints are read again at grade 2's review (EARMARKS L22), where the evidence and this question's design wait. The question as it stood: A Hint button (pass NG, from Mikey's question: "Based on your educational expertise, should we provide students with a "Hint" button on questions?  Something that helps them think about the question in another way without actually giving away the answer or giving a strong implication as to which one is most likely correct.  If you think yes, should we omit the hints from the memory checks and 21-day quizzes to ensure real mastery?"; raised in pass NG). Where: a practice round's question screen, a Hint button under the choices. Who: learners in the courses they read themselves, grade 2's three reader courses and every course from grade 3 up (pre-K 3 to grade 1 and grade 2's two read-aloud courses are spoken to the child). What they see today: no hint; after a wrong answer, a reason why that choice does not fit, and More Info explaining every choice. The evidence: in tutoring software help "helps, but only so much" (Aleven, Roll, McLaren and Koedinger, 2016): students skim hint levels in under a second, a hint that makes the answer guessable hurts learning (Vaughn and Kornell, 2019), the standard student models count a hinted question as wrong (Wang and Heffernan, 2011), and retrieving with fewer cues leaves a stronger memory (Carpenter and DeLosh, 2006: final recall of .42, .36, .35 and .30 after one to four cue letters); with two or three pictures nearly any hint points at one of them, which is why pre-readers keep the help they have, a spoken reason after a wrong tap and another try, only the first counting. For example: "Which is bigger, 3/8 or 3/5?" Hint: "Picture each fraction as a pizza cut into that many slices. Which slices are bigger?", the same hint for every question of that bank, so it cannot know which choice is right. Choices: (a) one hint per question, in practice rounds only, written bank by bank as each course is reviewed (the way More Info is), a hinted right answer counting toward neither the pass nor the star and never held against the child (four of the other five still pass), no hint in the memory checks, the 21-day Quick Review, the checkpoint, placement, the quick check or the star's second pass, every hint logged and shown to the educator ("used a hint on 3 of 10; right after 2"), and a check that no hint names or describes a choice; (b) the same, except that a hinted right answer counts toward the pass, never the star: gentler, but a pass would then say less about what a child can do alone; (c) no hints for now, read again at grade 2's review. Claude recommends (a), as an experiment the reports can judge: if children who use hints pass the star round and the memory checks about as often as others, hints stay; if not, they come out.

An open question whose text a pass brings up to date keeps its text as it stood before, word for word, in a block of its own (pass MO, round five's record read: Q51's pass MN text, which pass MO rewrote for the app's own sounds and the voice check page; the question is still open, in section 11).

- Q51. Whether a voice spells a rhyme's ending as letters (pass MM, round one's lesson check A and round three's final reads). Where: First Sounds and Letters' Rhyme time, its lesson lines and the explanations of its 740 heard questions, for four-year-olds, and kindergarten's Rhymes, which say the endings the same way. What a child hears today: lines such as "Dog. Log. Both end in og, so dog and log rhyme too." and "Sun and fun both end with un, and pig and wig both end with ig." are spoken by the device's voice, and by Eleven v4 once the clips are recorded. Og, ig, un, op, en and ed are not everyday words, so a voice may spell one ("oh, gee") where the line means a sound, and O and I are letters this course names. This workspace has no voice to listen with, so Claude cannot hear it. Example: on your phone, with the sound on, open Rhyme time and listen to its second to fifth lines, which say all six endings; in ElevenLabs, make the same lines. While you listen, hear Buh and sss too: its held sounds are said as the stretched start of a word (Sam's hiss, "Sss!", as the start of sun, and Mia's "mmm" as the start of moon), and its title the same way. Choices: (a) every ending sounds like the end of its word, so nothing changes; (b) a voice spells some: tell Claude which endings, on which voice, and Claude gives each a spelling the voice says as a sound (as the screens already say mmm as the start of moon) and adds it to Eleven's pronunciation dictionary for the recorded clips. What changes: (b) changes how those lines are written for the voice, never what the screen shows. Claude recommends listening to all six on your phone and in ElevenLabs before the clips are made, and saying how the held sounds in Buh and sss came out. In pass MN Mikey wrote "Q51 - I will check this out, please keep repeating this question until complete": it stays open, and every delivery message lists it in full until he says it is done.
<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's open questions as pass MN left them, before pass MO brought them up to date, word for word: end -->

<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's open questions as pass MO left them, before pass MP brought them up to date, word for word: begin -->
- Q51. Whether the voice says the lessons' sounds and word endings as sounds (pass MM; since pass MO the app makes its own sounds). Where: every spoken line that holds a sound or a word's ending: First Sounds and Letters' First sounds and Rhyme time, for four-year-olds, kindergarten's Rhymes and letter-sound lessons, and the stories that say a sound slowly, such as Sam's "Sss!". What you heard in pass MN, on your phone: "Sss" and "fff" said as the letter over and over, tuh heard as "two", mmm heard as "moon", ig spelled out (you heard it as "ip"), and at heard almost as "et". What a child hears since pass MO: the app plays its own sounds for the held sounds and the crisp ones (mmm, nnn, sss, fff, zzz, lll and rrr, and t, p and k), made by the app itself, so every phone plays the same ones, between the phone voice's words; the voice is handed ig as "igg", and at is said as a word of its own where a line names it as an ending ("One ends in at, and the other ends in og."). The voice check page, linked in the delivery message, plays each sound and line the way a lesson does, in 32 numbered rows: rows 1 to 10 are the app's own sounds, 11 to 21 lesson lines, and 22 to 31 the choices only your phone's voice can settle (ig handed over as igg, ihg or ig; at in the line or alone; buh, duh, guh, juh and tuh as the voice says them); row 32 is a grade 1 line for Q57, its vowel said by your phone's voice alone. Example: open the page on your phone with the sound on and tap row 12, "Sun. Sss. The word sun starts with the sound sss.": you should hear the word sun, then one short hiss, then the sentence, and the hiss again at its end, never letters. Choices: (a) every row sounds right, and Q51 is done; (b) some rows sound wrong: tell Claude the numbers and what you hear (for example "8 too soft, 23 sounds better than 22"), and Claude changes those sounds or what the voice is handed, never what the screen shows. When the recorded clips are made in ElevenLabs, the same choices go into its pronunciation dictionary (EARMARKS A41). Claude recommends listening to all 32 rows on your phone, since rows 22 to 31 are choices only your phone's voice can settle. In pass MN Mikey wrote "Q51 - I will check this out, please keep repeating this question until complete": it stays open, and every delivery message lists it in full until he says it is done.

A row of the audit that a pass replaces, at Mikey's word, keeps its text as it stood before, word for word, in a block of its own (pass MS: the say-along row, which his note on Q67 rewrote, one word in many silly voices).

| **Say it with me (Mikey, pass MR, Q67: "Q67 - B but let's make it fun.  When they say it with you, the voice should do something silly like whisper it, say it slow, say it low four times in a silly voice, say it ultra fast three times, draw it out the next time etc.  There can be variations between the events where this applies to keep it interesting and fun.  This page will be very important to mark with our not final audio message"):** a young vocabulary lesson named in SAY_WITH_ME_LESSONS (Q67's four and the four halves Q70 split from them) ends a line that brings in a new word with a cue that names the word, then says the word the cue's number of times in one of SAY_WITH_ME's voices (a tiny whisper, nice and slow, four times low and silly, three times super fast, stretched out long, squeaky, a happy cheer), so the child says it along in the pause before Next (Beck and McKeown's Text Talk had children repeat each new word, Q67); no lesson uses one voice twice in a row; nothing is recorded or heard by the app, so read-aloud stays output only; each such lesson shows the note that its audio is not final in a panel under its buttons (round one's screen check: above them it pushed Next below a phone's fold); each voice has its device shape and its recorded voice's tag | src/logic.mjs (SAY_WITH_ME, SAY_WITH_ME_LESSONS, sayWithMeCue), DECISIONS MR | test (scripts: every cue names the word said just after it and ends its line, a new voice each time, the cues only in those lessons, with six near misses) |

| **Say it with me, one word in many silly voices (Mikey, pass MR, Q67: "Q67 - B but let's make it fun.  When they say it with you, the voice should do something silly like whisper it, say it slow, say it low four times in a silly voice, say it ultra fast three times, draw it out the next time etc."; and pass MS: "I was talking about applying all of that to a single spoken word.  Not really one word as a whisper, another later fast, another later slow etc." ... "The very last pronunciation can be a clearer more normal version.  Other variation ideas that are silly could be very high pitched, a changing pitch where it starts high and ends low, a chant as if you were at a sporting event etc." ... "it would be nice to see just the letters that are being pronounced be highlighted during this part so they can follow along easier."):** a young vocabulary lesson named in SAY_ALONG_LESSONS (Q67's four, the four halves Q70 split from them and, since pass MS, Things we do, by Claude's reading of his answer for a new vocabulary lesson, raised with him as Q77) ends a line that brings in a new word, said on the line first, with a cue that names it ("Say different with me, in silly voices!") and then a game with that one word: three to five of SAY_ALONG_VOICES' silly ways, each its call and the word said that way (a whisper, slowly, four times low and silly, three times super fast, stretched out long, way up high, sliding down from high to low over three sayings, and cheered three times like a ball game with a clap between), none twice, and last "Now say it nice and clear." and the word clear; no two words of a lesson play the same ways in the same order, and every such lesson plays all eight; a pause follows each way (sayAlongEcho: three fifths of the time the voice took and 400 ms more, 0.7 to 1.8 seconds), for a child who says it along to breathe and one who echoes a short way to say it back, while a long way, such as four times low, is said along, since its pause stops at 1.8 seconds (round three's alignment read: the row had said the child says each way back); a strip under the line shows the word with its call and lights the syllable being said (SAY_ALONG_SYLLABLES, Merriam-Webster's division; a word of one syllable whole), the light following logic's guess of the device voice's pace, corrected by the voice's starts and ends, since a device voice never says where it is inside a word (the recordings' timings later, EARMARKS L12); the lesson hands the voice a line's sentences together, so each form reaches it in its voice's shape (through pass MR each sentence was read alone, so the silly voices never reached a lesson); nothing is recorded or heard by the app, so read-aloud stays output only; the note that the audio is not final stays in a panel under the buttons; each form is a clip of its own in the audio ledger, asked for in its voice's tag alone. Since round one's checks no word's game plays its two slow ways, stretched out and slowly, side by side (the one flat moment in lunchtime and bedtime); the light learns the voice's pace by logic's sayAlongPaceNext, two fifths of the way a form at a time, between half and 2.2 times the guess, and since round three only from a saying of two syllables or more whose start the voice reported, the 700 ms guess armed only until a voice has reported a start this visit; the strip's big word is named whole for a screen reader as a picture of the word; and the voice check page's say-along rows are held to the lessons' lines word for word. Since round one's screen check four times low and three times fast are said a saying at a time, each saying a piece of its own, lit from the voice's own start to its own end and filling its own ring (said as one piece, their light fell behind the voice and went out before the last saying in all fourteen lines that play low); the light waits for the voice to begin each saying, its first syllable lit meanwhile (sayPieces tells the strip whether the voice has begun a piece; a network voice can take 400 ms, and the light had run through a short word before it was heard); the label above the word keeps the height of its tallest text, the cue and every call in one cell, so nothing under the child's finger moves at 320 pixels (Next had jumped 19 pixels in 11 of the 34 lines); each saying is a ring, the one being said filled green; and with no voice the calls are steps, one to a line. The row as pass MR left it is kept word for word in docs/RULES-AUDIT-HISTORY.md | src/logic.mjs (SAY_ALONG_VOICES, SAY_ALONG_LESSONS, sayAlong, sayAlongParts, SAY_ALONG_SYLLABLES, sayAlongTimeline, sayAlongEcho, sayAlongPaceNext), src/ui.jsx (SayAlongStrip, SAY_ALONG_SHAPES, sayPieces and whether the voice has begun a piece), tools/audio-ledger.mjs, tools/voice-check.mjs, DECISIONS MR and MS | test (scripts: every cue opens a whole game that ends its line, three to five ways and the clear one last, none twice, no two words with one order, every lesson all eight, the syllables written and joining to their word, the word said before its cue, each form in its shape on the device voice and the same when read a sentence at a time, each form's own tag in the ledger, with 14 near misses, 15 since round one's screen check (four times low and three times fast said in one piece), and since round three every line in order at 40, 62 and 80 ms a letter lighting each saying's last syllable before the voice ends it (277 sayings; the old rule left 4, 2 and 1 short); rules: the clap only inside a chant, and measured; question-screens: the light follows a stand-in voice through a game, proven failing on the light stuck on a first syllable; since round one's checks, rules: a lesson's chant read as the lesson reads it, a sentence a text, keeps its cheers and claps together and waits the child's turn, proven failing with speakSequence's together planted false; scripts: the two slow ways never side by side, with a near miss, the pace rule's values, and the voice check page's rows against the lessons' lines, with a near miss; question-screens: the strip's word named whole for a screen reader; since round one's screen check, question-screens: a voice that takes 400 ms to begin, every saying of four times low and three times fast lit through and no syllable past a saying's first lit before the voice begins it, the strip's height and Next's place held at 320 pixels through a whole game, and with no voice the calls one to a line, all three proven failing on round two's strip (its light, 104 and 85 pixels, its dotted list) and the light's wait on a light planted to run from the queue) |
| **Pre-K is counted against every outcome (Mikey, pass MS, Q76: "Q76 - C"):** the pre-K plans list every outcome of the 2027 recommendations for each age, domain by domain (PREK_OUTCOMES, copied from docs/standards by tools/prek-outcomes.mjs), and every Head Start preschool goal, domain by domain (listing Head Start's in full too is Claude's reading of the reason Q76 gave for the (c) he chose, a printed record should never read as complete when it is not, raised with him as Q78); each row names the lessons that teach it, gathered from PREK_TAUGHT (the plans as they stood through pass MR, where a new pre-K lesson's code is added), or none, shown as Not yet covered; a plan is still being written (planned) while any of its rows waits, and the map shows "Still being written" under the grade, on the screen and the printout, and it turns ready by itself when its last row is taught; the other grades' plans still list the standards their lessons cite until each grade's review transcribes its standards whole | src/curriculum.mjs (PREK_OUTCOMES, PREK_TAUGHT, prekPlans), tools/prek-outcomes.mjs, src/ui.jsx (the map), CLAUDE.md, DECISIONS MS | test (coverage: the copy agrees with docs/standards both ways, every taught row's words exact, a plan planned while any row waits; click-through: pre-K 3 counted against its 123 outcomes, still being written, its domains listed and an untaught outcome not yet covered) |
