Pass MU replaced one row at Mikey's word: the say-along row (the eight older lessons, his answer to Q81; every new word's whole game, his answer to Q80; and the cheer's whistle and crowd made again, his notes on rows 65 and 66).

<!-- RULES-AUDIT rows as pass MT left them, before pass MU replaced them, word for word: begin -->
| **Say it together, one word in five silly ways (Mikey, pass MR, Q67: "Q67 - B but let's make it fun."; pass MS, his note on Q67: "I was talking about applying all of that to a single spoken word.  Not really one word as a whisper, another later fast, another later slow etc." ... "it would be nice to see just the letters that are being pronounced be highlighted during this part so they can follow along easier."; and pass MT, his answer to Q77, word for word in DECISIONS MT: "Q77 - A - it is valuable to learning and will sound better with Eleven when we add playful, interesting and silly audio tags.  Instead of "Say different with me" we can change it to Let's say it together!  (In an excited and emphasized voice).  First, let's say it slowly (and draw it out way more slowly).  Now, let's whisper it twice (this whisper should also say it slowly but not as slow as the first slow one).  Now let's say it four times using silly voices (each of the four should use a different pitch, some high, some low, some in different accents like British or Australian but sounding like it's still from the same person and drawn out in various durations).  Now let's say it three times super fast!  (Make it faster than it is now).  Okay, let's try stretching it out now (another version of slow but really emphasizing each part of the word more).  Great job!  One last time, nice and clear.  (This last one is normal speed. - The "say it slowly" should be three times as drawn out as it is now and the four times in a row should have brief pauses between each one too).  Make the other versions of this similar in playful conversational tone and just as silly yet useful." and on row 64: "maybe a longer session of claps or cheers with whistling in place of the claps would work"):** a young vocabulary lesson named in SAY_ALONG_LESSONS (Q67's four, the four halves Q70 split from them, and Things we do, kept by Q77 (a)), and every new pre-K vocabulary lesson from pass MT on (Q77 (a): a pre-K lesson that cites the 2027 II.D.1 or II.D.2 or Head Start's P-LC 6 or P-LC 7 plays the game, the eight older ones SAY_ALONG_WAITS names keeping their lines, held to those eight, Q81 asking about them; (a)'s "every new young vocabulary lesson" is read as pre-K, Claude's reading, since every lesson the question named was pre-K), ends a line that brings in a new word, said on the line first and the line's own words ending on it ("Our word is match.", since round one's lesson check), with Mikey's game for that word (word for word on different; on the other words his shape and his openings, the flavors Claude's, at his "Make the other versions of this similar in playful conversational tone and just as silly yet useful."): "Let's say it together!", said excited and emphasized; five steps in order, each called in his openings ("First, let's ...", "Now, let's ...", "Now let's ...", "Now let's ..." and "Okay, let's try ... now") and then the word said that way, one way a step from that step's slot of SAY_ALONG_VOICES: first slowly, three times as drawn out as pass MS's slow (rate 0.16), or like a big, sleepy giant; second whispered twice, slowly but quicker than the first, or squeaked twice like a tiny mouse, with a brief pause between; third four times in four silly voices, each its own pitch and pace with a brief pause between (low and drawn out first, then high and quick, and on the recording an Australian and a British accent in the same voice), slid down from high to low over three sayings, bounced up high and down low four times with a brief pause between, or cheered three times like a crowd at a big game, a fan's whistle between the cheers and the crowd clapping and whistling after, the app's own sounds; fourth three times super fast, in one breath and quicker than pass MS's (rate 2.2); and fifth stretched out, each part stressed, or stretched out like play dough (not bubble gum, a choking hazard the stories test keeps out of pre-K); then "Great job!", said as a cheer; then "One last time, nice and clear." and the word at the young voice's ordinary pace. Learning words plays his own version on different, word for word (slowly, whispered twice, four silly voices, super fast, stretched out). No two words of a lesson play the same five ways; a lesson of two games or more plays both flavors of the first, second and fifth steps and as many silly thirds as it has games, up to all four; and every way is played somewhere. A pause follows each way (sayAlongEcho: three fifths of the time the voice took and 400 ms more, 0.7 to 1.8 seconds), the child's turn. A strip under the line shows the word, the call above it ("Great job!" while it is said) and a ring for each saying of a way said more than once, and lights the syllable being said (SAY_ALONG_SYLLABLES, Merriam-Webster's division; a word of one syllable whole), with no fade, its ink and fill changing in one frame (since round one; the read-along's lit sentences too); the light follows logic's guess of the device voice's pace, learned for each of the voice's shapes and for all (sayAlongLearn: a shape's first heard saying of two syllables or more sets its own pace, and only a rate a phone says as asked, 0.4 to 1.6, moves the pace of all), super fast's three sayings walked a little ahead in their one breath (SAY_ALONG_JOINED_LEAD); the lesson hands the voice a line's sentences together, and a way's brief pause holds across them (pauseAfter); nothing is recorded or heard by the app; the note that the audio is not final stays in a panel under the buttons; each form is a clip of its own in the audio ledger with its way's tag, each of the four silly voices and each bounce its own, and the opener, the calls and the praise their own (SAY_ALONG_TALK). <!-- docs/VISION.md and RULES-AUDIT rows as pass MZ left them, before pass NA corrected them in place, word for word: begin -->
- **Master, then move.** A module opens only when everything it needs is mastered.
  Mastery is measured in one sitting, never patched together across days.
- **Reflect often, and for a reason.** Reflection questions come round after every
  second finished round, pass or fail, and every one is written toward one of four
  aims: curiosity about the world; failure as the way learning happens; naming and
  handling feelings; and the truth that hard days are a normal part of being human
  and that people get through them. After a failed round, the questions about failure
  and feelings come first. No question is ever offered a third time. The pool must
  grow with the curriculum, and a check says so when it falls behind.
If it stores something about a child we did not need, it does not belong. If it lets
a student move past something they have not mastered, it is wrong.
## 1. The vision (docs/VISION.md, kept here in full because every change is judged by it)
- **Master, then move.** A module opens only when everything it needs is mastered. Mastery is measured in one sitting, never patched together across days.
- **Reflect often, and for a reason.** Reflection questions come after every second finished round, pass or fail, each written toward one of four aims: curiosity about the world; failure as the way learning happens; naming and handling feelings; and the truth that hard days are a normal part of being human. After a failed round the failure and feelings questions come first. No question is offered a third time. The pool grows with the curriculum, and a check says so when it falls behind.
What it is not: not a replacement for a teacher, and not a claim. We do not say students outperform anyone until real classrooms show it. Who it is for, in order: microschools and homeschooling families; families supplementing a school; daycare centers with the pre-K material; later, trades and workplace training with the child-facing parts switched off. How to tell if a change is right: does it help a child master something, help an educator see clearly what happened, or free up time for real life? If it does none of those, it does not belong. If it stores something about a child we did not need, it does not belong. If it lets a student past something unmastered, it is wrong.
| **A pre-K lesson requires what its ideas build on (Mikey, pass MZ, his answer to Q84: "Q84 - B"):** pre-K's courses are linear: false, so a pre-K lesson is gated only by the lessons its requires names; each names the lesson its ideas build on, or none, and each that names one, or named one in pass MY, says why in requiresWhy beside it, in whole sentences that name each lesson it requires by its title, so a lesson opens when its foundation is passed and loop back sends a child who keeps missing it to that foundation. Since pass MO each new pre-K lesson had named the lesson built just before it (Count the words, which counts the words of a sentence, looped back to She, he and they). Of the 57 pre-K lessons that named one in pass MY, 36 keep it, 10 name the lesson their idea builds on instead (Count the words needs Longer sentences, Words in a sentence Say it in a sentence, the four matching lessons after Match the vehicles Same and different, Learning words Two ideas, Two steps Listen and tap, Longer sentences Things we do, and Trace L, T and F Trace a big letter), and 11 name none, each saying why (a lesson with none never loops back, and its Action Item comes after four misses). A lesson pass MY delivered may name only a lesson it needed then, directly or through the lessons those needed, so no lesson open for a child who followed the gates locks: where the lesson an idea builds on was not one it needed in pass MY, so a child can have it open with that lesson not passed (Big and small, whose size words Big and little teaches), the lesson names none and says so. Nine of the 21 lessons that changed were built before pass MO (the four matching lessons after Match the vehicles, Trace L, T and F, Which came first?, Triangles, Big and small and Words that rhyme): Q84's (b) said "for each pre-K lesson", while its What each changes named the lessons built since pass MO, so the delivery tells Mikey how far his answer reached (round two's final read). Two kinds of educator action can still lock one of the ten lessons that now name another lesson: a lesson on its old path (its old prerequisite, or a lesson that one needed, as Words that change is for Count the words) was opened by hand (Unlock it anyway) and then passed, or moved past with Move Forward, while the lesson it now builds on never was; or the lesson it now builds on was erased (Yes, erase it) after the old one was passed. It opens again when the lesson it builds on is passed or moved past, or when the educator opens the lesson itself by hand; opening the lesson it builds on by hand does not open it (round one's code check, widened by round two's final read, pass MZ; Q87 asks whether to add a second way in). - **Since pass ND (Domain III's letters and their sounds, and Domain IV's spaces between words):** pre-K 3's Letters and sounds teaches the sounds of M, S and B, and pre-K 4's Hear it in the name and At the end of the name the sounds of ten letters heard in their own names, B, T, K, J and Z at the start and M, S, F, L and R at the end, each sound the app's own clip, every hiss one breath in the three lessons (Q89 asks Mikey) and lll never beside rrr; pre-K 3's Space between words and pre-K 4's Spaces between words show writing as print with a little space after each word and ask by a tap which writing has its spaces, how many words it has and which one is a letter, a word or a picture, nothing a child writes asked for or kept; in a lesson on a wide screen a drawing beside its group of dots grows with them (EARMARKS A88), and since round one a letter beside a drawing grows with it; writing as print stays on one line, fitted to its box; and every drawing a bank puts on an answer tile reads at its edge on the resting, right and wrong tiles of both themes (EARMARKS A89, tests/e2e/tile-edges.mjs).
**One breath for a hiss in the three letters lessons (pass ND; Claude's design, Q89 asks Mikey):** pre-K 3's Letters and sounds and pre-K 4's Hear it in the name and At the end of the name teach that a letter makes one sound (S says sss, F says fff, Z says zzz), so every hiss in them plays one breath of 0.32 seconds, in their lines, their stories and their banks' questions wherever those are asked, as in the three phoneme lessons (ONE_BREATH_LESSONS names the six lessons, and oneBreathOn reads it on every screen); a hiss alone in every other lesson keeps the two breaths of pass MP's row in section 6. The audio ledger asks Eleven for one breath in their lines and stories. The rules test reads every hiss in the six lessons' texts and questions as one breath, and tests/scripts.test.mjs reads the ledger's breaths in every lesson line with a hiss (69 line parts, 38 hisses of one breath, 36 of them in the six lessons, and 36 of two) and the six lessons' stories (10 rows), each by its own list of the six, with their plants.
| **Domain III's III.C.2 and III.C.3, read (pass ND, the standards review; Claude's readings):** PK3 III.C.2 ("recognizes some distinct letter-sound correspondences with adult assistance.") and III.C.3 ("produces some distinct letter-sound correspondences with adult assistance."); PK4 III.C.2 ("recognizes at least 20 distinct letter-sound correspondences.") and III.C.3 ("produces at least 15 distinct letter-sound correspondences."). A letter-sound correspondence is a letter and the sound it says, recognized when the child finds the letter for a sound heard, the sound for a letter shown, or the picture whose first sound a letter says, and produced when the child says it in a turn of their own; pre-K 3's "some", with the voice the adult assistance, is met by three letters (M, S and B, whole), and pre-K 4's twenty and fifteen are begun by ten (in part, the rest named). A letter's sound is the app's own clip (SPEECH_SOUNDS: m, n, s, f, z, r, l, t, p, k, b, d, g and j), never a lone letter a voice reads as its name, and the short vowels wait while the app's own are on trial (Q60, EARMARKS A42), so the first letters are consonants; pre-K 4's are letters whose names hold their sounds, heard at the start (B, T, K, J and Z) or the end (M, S, F, L and R) of the name, since children learn those sounds sooner (Treiman, Tincoff, Rodriguez, Mouzaki and Francis, 1998, Child Development 69(6)). Next by the same reading: P and D, N and X, then C, G, H and W, whose names do not hold their sounds, then the vowels, each said by the word it starts. | the three letters lessons' sources; PREK_TAUGHT; EARMARKS' 2027 map | the coverage test (the plans' rows); the rules test (the twenty-five banks re-derived from what each question says and shows by the test's own table, ND_LETTERS, with their near misses, and everything a bank uses said in its lesson) |

since round one's screen read, where the room grew 4 pixels at a time until a word joined a lone last one, which took up to 93 pixels, two fifths of a line), and the say-along word, which never wraps, shrinking with the room, kept on a wrapper outside the strip since Say it again draws the strip anew; a block the cue does not reach keeps no room, so a tall phone, a tablet and a laptop look as before, and a feedback box keeps its full width and color; the cue decides as each state is drawn, before the paint, so no word shows under it and then moves, and once a person touches, scrolls, types or taps, the room holds until the next state; We should always use the newest versions of each model.  Please continue" and "It should also act as the advisor moving forward and documentation should update to reflect this."):** a simple task whose result can be checked (a research sweep that cites the pages it read, a count, a regeneration) may run on a Sonnet 5.5 agent; the final read, the overarching read of the whole pass, and creative writing may run on Fable, where quality gains even at a higher cost; the content, code and screen reads stay on the lead's own model unless a pass records why; since pass NF every agent and every check runs on the newest version of its model (Fable 5.2, Opus 5.5 and Sonnet 5.5 when pass NF wrote this, where the rule had named Fable 5.1), and Fable is the advisor the lead and its agents consult at decision points (Claude Code's advisor tool): the project's .claude/settings.json sets advisorModel to "fable", the alias Claude Code resolves to its newest Fable (claude-fable-5-2 in Claude Code 2.1.322), so a Claude Code session opened in the folder has it, and a cloud chat's advisor, which its own session sets, is made Fable with /advisor fable typed before its opening message (after /model fable on a plan that bills Fable to usage credits, and /model opus after it, to keep Opus as the main model; these steps, the decision points and the record of each pass's advisor are Claude's and Claude Code's, from his two sentences), since a conversation keeps the advisor it began with until /clear or /compact (Claude Code's own reply when Mikey typed /advisor fable mid-pass NF, whose chat stayed on Opus 5.5 until its context was compacted, and then took Fable 5.2); the pass's DECISIONS entry names the model each check ran on, and the advisor its session had. The questions answered when pass NH opened (Mikey's message: "Q95 - A", "Q96 - A" and "Q97 - A"), each as it stood in section 11, with what was built.

<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass NH, word for word: begin -->
- Q95. Answered in pass NH (Mikey: "Q95 - A"): (a), built: a new chat's lead reads a pass packet in place of the whole of RULES-AUDIT, EARMARKS and INDEPENDENT-CHECKS, made by tools/pass-packet.mjs at the start of the pass from the areas the pass touches, every line those areas touch copied word for word with its file and line number and an index of every other line by its title, and what every pass needs copied whatever the areas; the full files stay the only source of the rules, the alignment read and the final read still read them whole, tests/pass-packet.test.mjs (check.sh step 3c) holds every copied line to its file byte for byte and every line of the three files to the packet once, and each DECISIONS entry from pass NH on counts the findings traced to a line the packet left out (the docs sync test). Measured in pass NH, the packet is about half of the three files for a pre-K pass's areas, not the fifth the flow review estimated. The question as it stood: What the lead reads at the start of a pass (pass NG's weekly flow review, run on Fable; raised in pass NG). Where: not in the app, but in how each chat for The Wise Human begins and how much of the week's usage a pass spends. Who: Mikey, and the build. What happens today: the opening message asks a new chat to read docs/NEW-CHAT.md, docs/RULES-AUDIT.md (about 672 KB), docs/EARMARKS.md (about 286 KB), docs/INDEPENDENT-CHECKS.md (about 199 KB) and the latest DECISIONS entry, and every session and every agent loads CLAUDE.md and its parts (about 82 KB; each size measured again at the end of pass NG, where round two's final read found 198 for a file of 196): about 1.3 MB, near 300,000 tokens, more than a lead can read whole before it works, so it reads them in parts, and whatever it holds it pays to read again, cached, on every turn of a pass; the chat compacts when that context fills (pass NF's did twice, and so did pass NG's). For example, a pass that builds a pre-K lesson needs RULES-AUDIT's rows on lessons, banks, stories, pictures and audio, not its rows on the educator's reports or licensing, which the tests hold anyway. Choices: (a) a pass packet: a tool copies, word for word, the RULES-AUDIT and EARMARKS rows the pass's areas touch, with an index of the rest, the lead reads that packet and opens a full file where the index points, the full files stay the only source of the rules, the alignment read and the final read still read them whole, a test holds the packet's rows to the full files byte for byte, and each pass counts any finding traced to a row the packet left out; (b) keep it as it is. Claude recommends (a): the flow review estimates the lead would start near 80,000 to 100,000 tokens instead of about 300,000, with fewer compactions mid-pass and a cheaper turn all pass long, which leaves more of the week for Fable's reads; the risk, a rule the packet leaves out, is held by the tests (most rules are tests) and by the two reads that still read everything. CLAUDE.md's long pass lines could later move word for word to topic files, as Claude Code's own guide suggests for a long manual, but Claude Code loads such a file (a path-scoped rule) only when a session reads files matching its paths, and every pass reads the same two source files, so that waits for the packet's measured effect.
- Q96. Answered in pass NH (Mikey: "Q96 - A"): (a), built: the eight letter-name kinds joined GROWN_UP_CARD_BANKS (32 banks), so on a device with no voice they show the grown-up's card, the words to read aloud and Ready, then the letters, and left GROWN_UP_CARD_WAITING, which since the pass's round one code read holds the seven picture banks beside a letter, waiting on Q98; Big letters' yes or no works as Q97 (a) decides; EARMARKS A114 done. The question as it stood: Eight more letters questions on a device with no voice (pass NG's round one code read, measured by the lead; EARMARKS A114; raised in pass NG). Where: pre-K 3's A and B, and pre-K 4's Big letters, Trace a big letter, Trace more big letters and Trace L, T and F, on a phone, tablet or computer whose voice is off or missing. What a child sees today: with a voice, the question prints "Tap the letter you hear." and the voice says the letter's name. With no voice the app prints the name instead: "Tap the letter B." above the letters A and B, or "Is this the letter E?" beside a big E. A child who matches shapes taps the B, or says Yes when the two letters match, and is right every time (400 of 400 seeds in each of the eight kinds, where tapping the first answer or always saying Yes is right 116 to 212 times), without knowing the letter's name. Q91 counted the kinds in the seven lessons about the sounds letters say, so these eight, which ask a letter's name, did not get the card; they wait on GROWN_UP_CARD_WAITING, and the rules test holds every letters bank of a read-aloud course to the card's list or that one. Choices: (a) the same card as Q91 (a): with no voice, these eight show the card with the words to read aloud and Ready, then the letters; (b) keep it as it is. Claude recommends (a): it is the same give-away the card narrows in Q91's thirteen letter kinds, for one tap more (corrected after round two's final read, from "the same give-away Q91 closed", since by Q97 the card closes it in none of the eleven kinds that keep their letter on the screen). The yes or no kind among them would work as Q97 decides.
- Q97. Answered in pass NH (Mikey: "Q97 - A"): (a), built: in the eleven kinds, and in Big letters' yes or no (Q96's) and, by Claude's reading of the same way around the card, A and B's letter in a sign word, the picture and the letter hide while the card is up, nothing prints above the card, the card says what the voice says without the letter shown, and without its name in the eleven kinds about a letter's sound (GROWN_UP_CARD_HIDES; the sign word kind's card only the letter to find, "Tap the letter B."), and Ready shows the picture or the letter, the question alone (a which-sound question's "Which sound does this letter say?"; the sign word kind's setup too, which names no letter) and the answers; the rules test works out every hiding card's words from its question, and tests/e2e/grown-up-card.mjs reads the card standing alone before Ready; EARMARKS A115 done. The question as it stood: The letter beside the grown-up's card (pass NG's round one screen read and round two's final and screen reads, measured by the lead; EARMARKS A115; raised in pass NG; sent mid-pass at about 08:20 and corrected at about 09:26, since the first text named the yes or no kinds alone and had two wording errors). Where: on a device with no voice, eleven of the card's 24 kinds of question keep the letter they ask about on the screen above the card while the card is up. These are the six yes or no kinds ("Does S say that sound?" in Letters and sounds, Hear it in the name, At the end of the name, Start or end of the name and Hear it in a word, and "Do we write this letter first?" or "at the end?" in Write it by its sounds) and the five which-sound kinds ("Which sound does it say?" in the same five lessons). What a child sees, as built this pass: the card says, for example, "This is the letter S. Listen to this sound. Sss. Does S say that sound?", or "This is the letter W. Which sound does it say?" with "Button 1, huh. Button 2, wuh. Button 3, chuh." The big letter stays above the card, since the question is about that letter. Ready hides the words and shows the answers. So the words and the letter are on the screen together, and a child can find the answer before Ready without hearing anything, then carry a Yes or a button's number across. Over seeds 1 to 400: in the yes or no kinds, a child who answers Yes whenever a word on the card longer than one letter starts with the letter shown is right 400 times in Letters and sounds and in Hear it in a word, 359 in At the end of the name, 323 in Hear it in the name, 304 in Start or end of the name, and 261 in Write it by its sounds, where always saying Yes is right 200 times (188 in Write it by its sounds); in the which-sound kinds, a child who knows each capital's small letter and carries the number of the sound that starts with it is right 400 times in Letters and sounds, At the end of the name and Hear it in a word, 318 in Hear it in the name and 309 in Start or end of the name, where always tapping the first button is right 129 to 212 times. These are Q91's own figures for these kinds, so the card does not close Q91's give-away in them. In the card's other thirteen kinds the answers are letters, and they are never on the screen with the words, so a child would have to remember a shape across Ready. Choices: (a) in these eleven kinds, the letter is hidden while the card is up, and the card shows what the voice says without the letter or its name: in a yes or no kind, for example, "Listen to this sound. Sss." and Ready, which then shows the letter, "Does this letter say that sound?" and Yes and No, for the grown-up to read; in a which-sound kind, "Listen to these sounds. Button 1, huh. Button 2, wuh. Button 3, chuh." and Ready, which then shows the letter, "Which sound does this letter say?" and the numbered buttons; in Write it by its sounds, "Let's write a word by its sounds. Listen. Box." and Ready, which then shows the picture, the letter and "Do we write this letter first?" (or "at the end?"), nothing the voice says lost there. A child who matches shapes would then have to remember one across Ready, as in the other kinds. The cost: on such a device the letter's name is not read out before the question, as the voice does, and in a which-sound question the grown-up says the sounds before the child sees the letter. (b) Keep it as built. Claude recommends (a): it closes the way around the skill that the card was built to close, and the child still hears the sounds and sees the letter before answering.

- Q98. Ball. Jar. Where: on a phone, tablet or computer whose voice is off or missing, in pre-K 3's Letters and sounds and pre-K 4's Hear it in the name, At the end of the name, Start or end of the name, Hear it in a word and Write it by its sounds, the seven kinds of question whose answers are pictures and which show a letter: "Which one starts with the sound that J says?" beside a big J, above a ball, a jar and a table, and in Write it by its sounds "Which one ends with the sound that D says?" and "Which word are these letters for?" beside B and D. Which one starts with the sound that J says?", the names in the pictures' order and the letter beside them, so a child who matches shapes can find the name that starts with the letter shown (the J of Jar) and tap the picture in that place without hearing anything: right on all 400 seeds in six of the seven kinds and on 309 in Start or end of the name, where tapping the first picture is right 112 to 201 times. Choices: (a) the card, as in Q97 (a): with no voice these seven show the grown-up's card first, and the letter hides while it is up; the card says what the voice says without the letter or its name ("Now listen to these three. Ball. Jar. Table."), and Ready shows the letter, the question ("Which one starts with the sound this letter says?") and the pictures, so a child who matches shapes would have to remember which place held a name starting with that shape across Ready, the same thing remembered as in the which-sound kinds; (b) the card as in (a), with the names on it in an order of their own (by the alphabet, say) rather than the pictures' order, so a remembered place gives nothing away, and the grown-up names each picture by pointing at it after Ready; (c) keep it as it is. Claude recommends (a): it is Q97's own design, built and tested this pass for the which-sound kinds, and it closes the give-away at the cost Mikey accepted there (on such a device the letter is not read out before the question, and the child hears the names before seeing the letter); (b) asks more of the grown-up, who must find each picture's name, for a smaller gain than the step from the print to (a).

| **Domain III's III.C.2 and III.C.3, read (pass ND, the standards review; Claude's readings):** PK3 III.C.2 ("recognizes some distinct letter-sound correspondences with adult assistance.") and III.C.3 ("produces some distinct letter-sound correspondences with adult assistance."); PK4 III.C.2 ("recognizes at least 20 distinct letter-sound correspondences.") and III.C.3 ("produces at least 15 distinct letter-sound correspondences."). A letter-sound correspondence is a letter and the sound it says, recognized when the child finds the letter for a sound heard, the sound for a letter shown, or the picture whose first sound a letter says, and produced when the child says it in a turn of their own; pre-K 3's "some", with the voice the adult assistance, is met by three letters (M, S and B, whole), and pre-K 4's twenty and fifteen are begun by ten (in part, the rest named), by fourteen since pass NE and by eighteen since pass NF (the rows below). A letter's sound is the app's own clip (SPEECH_SOUNDS: m, n, s, f, z, r, l, t, p, k, b, d, g and j), never a lone letter a voice reads as its name, or since pass NF, for H and W, the device voice's own huh and wuh (VOICE_SOUNDS, beside shh), as every lesson has said them since pass MO, and the short vowels wait while the app's own are on trial (Q60, EARMARKS A42), so the first letters are consonants; pre-K 4's are letters whose names hold their sounds, heard at the start (B, T, K, J and Z) or the end (M, S, F, L and R) of the name, since children learn those sounds sooner (Treiman, Tincoff, Rodriguez, Mouzaki and Francis, 1998, Child Development 69(6)). Next by the same reading: P and D, N and X, built in pass NE, then C, G, H and W, whose names do not hold their sounds, built in pass NF, then Q, V and Y, then the vowels, each said by the word it starts. - Q99. Answered in pass NJ (Mikey: "Q99 - A", with his words on the core cast's skin: "For reference, Mike the skeptic has Olive toned tan skin, Savanah, Jaxon and Harlow are a little more pale white.  Georgette is the same pale white.  Frederick and Chloe are also white but you can choose the tone.  You might already know this since you saw the reference images I provided a long time ago when deciding to do this."): (a), built: a house rule that every new picture row writes each person's skin in plain words, never a word for where anyone is from, the same in every row the person appears in, held by the ledger test for every row from pass NI's serials on (S4135 on, and the next serials of every kind); the seven core cast's skin in docs/CHARACTERS.md's Skin section, his words for Mike (olive-toned tan skin, hyphened as a describing word) and for Savanah, Jaxon, Harlow and Georgette (pale white skin), and Claude's choice at his word for Frederick (fair skin with a rosy tone) and Chloe (light skin with a warm golden tone), written into the fourteen character sheets (C1 to C14) and the four Wonder portraits (U2 to U5), and into docs/LEONARDO-BRIEF.md's cast list; the older rows gain skin as their lessons are read again, and the early build's 1,145 rows painting a person with none before it is painted (EARMARKS L23). The reference images he gave long ago are not in the project; the sheets' words were written from them. The question as it stood: Skin in the painted people's looks (pass NI's story read; EARMARKS A123; raised in pass NI). Where: the Leonardo prompts in docs/ART-REQUESTS.md that paint the people in every story's pictures and coloring scenes, in every course; you paste them into Leonardo when you paint. Who: you, when you paint, and every child who looks at a story's pictures. What the rows say today: each person's look is written out in every row they appear in, their hair and their clothes, so a girl in a white hoodie with black locs looks the same in all four pictures of her story, but no older row says what color her skin is, so Leonardo chooses it again for each picture, and a child's skin can change from one picture of a story to the next, or a mom and her son can come out looking unrelated. This pass's writer added skin to its new people in plain words, the same in every row: for example S4135 now reads "A small girl with shoulder-length black locs and warm brown skin, in a white hoodie, ...", her grandma "long silver locs tied back and warm brown skin", and Hector and his dad "light tan skin". About 2,760 older rows paint a person (counted by the words for people in each row's scene: 2,185 story pictures, 313 long-story pictures, 173 coloring pages and 91 lesson paintings), every one still marked Needed in the ledger. The seven core cast members (Mike, Chloe, Frederick, Georgette, Savanah, Jaxon and Harlow) are painted from their character sheets, and docs/CHARACTERS.md writes no skin for them; under (a) Claude would ask you what to write for each before any row names it, and would never choose it. Choices: (a) keep it as a house rule: every new row from now on writes each person's skin in plain words like these, never a word for where anyone is from, the same in every row they appear in, and the older rows gain it lesson by lesson as each is read again (none is painted yet); (b) take it out of the new rows and leave skin to Leonardo, as every older row does. Claude recommends (a): it keeps one person the same across a story's pictures, which the house already asks of hair and clothes for that reason, and plain words keep it a description of how someone looks, never a label.

| Reflections (Wonder): after every two different lessons worked since the last one was answered, pass or fail, each lesson counted once however many rounds it took (WONDER_EVERY; Mikey's rule restated 2026-09-28); the question answered fewest times first, then the theme met longest ago; after a rough patch, a round not passed or three misses in a row inside one (MISS_STREAK), the failure and feelings questions come first and the ups-and-downs ones next, ahead of the rest; the pool is shared by stage; never a repeat before the pool is used; no question offered more than twice; pre-readers only get the questions with short voices (simple), four since pass MH (its row below), and since pass MG hear each one's closing line on one more card after its voices, spoken as it appears (Mikey, Q10: "yes"; wonderPreReaderCards and wonderCardSpoken in logic), nineteen closings reworded by the Wonder check so each makes sense heard once (a grown-up named wherever one sends a child outside, the lessons' mad for cross), and spoken once however often the screen redraws (the click-through counts it); every course has at least one; nothing offered until approved; four perspectives and a closing question; neutral on religion; a pre-reader's round that finishes a course goes to the celebration, which ends at home, so a Wonder question due then waits for the next finished round (pass MF; Mikey, pass MG, Q25: "keep it"). Corrected in place in pass MZ (Mikey's answer to Q85): until then this row said after every two finished rounds, the theme met longest ago first, a failed round alone, and two-voice questions; its old words are in docs/RULES-AUDIT-HISTORY.md | VISION, ALGORITHMS | test (section 8 of the rules test checks each of these by name, except the twice limit, which is in the code without a named test: **verify**) |
- **My Classroom:** add a student by school-issued ID, choose a level (Early years pre-K to grade 2; Elementary 3 to 5; Middle school 6 to 8; High school and beyond), a sign-in picture with a colour, an optional four-digit student PIN, Wonder on or off; rename, change picture, hide, merge; open the report. Quick checks on or off. Walk through as a student (every module open, nothing recorded). Wonder questions to review (nothing reaches a child until approved). Reading lists, science experiments, practical life skills, backup, contact.
**The app's own sounds are measured against recordings (pass MU, Mikey's notes on the voice check page: "39 has a weird click sound but the duh sounds good.  Guh in 40 has the weird clicking noise too but sounds correct otherwise.  Through 44 they all sound better but have odd accompanying sounds."; "65, the whistle gets cut off too quickly.  It should be drawn out a bit more and slightly change the amplitude of the whistle sound to sound natural as if a real breath was required.  66 the clapping sounds like static."):** nobody can listen where the sounds are made, so each fault he heard is found in numbers first, and each fix is held by a measure the rules test reads, with a plant for each fault. The clicks: pass MT's d and g released about 10 dB over their uh, with an attack of a quarter of a millisecond after silence, d's with energy up to 16,000 cycles a second; since pass MU a d, g or j releases into its uh, weighed as the ear hears it (A-weighted, since round one's screen check found the first fix's d and j still 8 to 9 dB over their uh in the click's band): its part between 2,500 and 6,000 cycles a second, where a phone's click lives, no more than about 3 dB over the uh (its plain power against the uh as heard), the whole release 0 to 10 dB over it, with almost nothing over 7,000 cycles and no gap before the uh (juh's hush begins under its release); and the endings ig, og and at release at least 6 dB under their vowel, as heard too, where pass MT's popped a few decibels over it. The whistle: nine tenths of a second on one breath that builds, swells and is spent (2 to 9 dB of swing, loudest between a fifth and half a second in), swooping up to a held note near 2,750 cycles a second and sliding down as the breath runs out. The crowd: 44 people clapping in a small hall for two and a half seconds, each clap one of the eight hand shapes in Jylhä and Erkut's table (DAFx 2008, Table 1, based on Peltola, Erkut, Cook and Välimäki's synthesis of hand clapping, 2007), with the hall's echo and two whistles from the crowd, breaking out over its first third of a second rather than on a cue (round one's screen check); its brightness (its energy centred near 1,800 cycles, where pass MT's centred near 2,600), its share over 4,000 cycles, its peakiness, its claps a second and its swells are set against forty recordings of clapping from the ESC-50 set (Piczak 2015), used only to measure and never shipped. A sound whose end a phone never reports holds a reading its own length and 400 ms more (soundGuardMs), not as long as the longest sound. The voice check page's rows 39 to 44, 65 and 66 play them for Mikey. The questions answered when pass NK opened (Mikey's message: "Q100: B" with his own words for the line, "Q101:  Let's try C this time and see how much usage it takes.", "Q102: B is okay, only if you think this doesn't introduce risk that would lead to reduced accuracy or quality.", "Q103: A" and "Q104:  B and keep at the bottom"; his whole message is in DECISIONS' pass NK entry), as they stood in section 11.

- Q100. Answered in pass NK (Mikey: "Q100: B \"Every question starts on, and you can turn off any you'd rather skip.\" To All questions are active by default.  However, you can turn off any you'd rather skip."): (b), with his own words, built: his line, "All questions are active by default. However, you can turn off any you'd rather skip.", on the first week tour's Wonder Questions card and in the tip beside Wonder Questions when an educator adds a student, where Claude's line of pass NJ stood, the tip keeping his earlier "(see bottom of My Classroom)" after it (Q106); the rules test holds both places to his line. The question as it stood: The Wonder Questions tour card's last line, and the same line in the Wonder Questions tip (raised in pass NJ, when your answer turned every Wonder question on by default). Where: the first week tour an educator meets on a tablet or a laptop, its card on Wonder Questions, beside the classroom's Wonder Questions card glowing gold; and the tip that opens from the i beside Wonder Questions when an educator adds a student. Who: every new educator. What it says today: your words, "Wonder questions are deep, thought-provoking questions sprinkled between learning modules. They're designed to promote curiosity, reflection and critical thinking and once a student finds themselves failing modules, the questions are re-prioritized to cover emotional resilience and frame failure as an effective way to learn.", and then, on a line of its own, Claude's: "Every question starts on, and you can turn off any you'd rather skip." Until this pass that line was yours, "Students only see the questions you approve.", which became untrue once every question started on, so Claude put its own line in its place; the tip's last sentence, "Students only see the questions you approve (see bottom of My Classroom).", became "Every question starts on, and you can turn off any you'd rather skip (see bottom of My Classroom)." in the same way. By your rule the tour cards' words are yours and never change, only a typo fixed with you told; this line is the one exception, made because the old line would tell a new educator the opposite of what the app now does, and you can undo it. Choices: (a) keep Claude's line in both places; (b) give your own words for that line, or drop it, in both places. Claude recommends (a), or your own words if you prefer: either keeps the card and the tip true.
- Q101. Answered in pass NK (Mikey: "Q101:  Let's try C this time and see how much usage it takes.  Although, it would be nice if we could remember roughly how much is used per lesson so I can say something like \"let's only use 30% usage on this pass.\"  I might reach a point where I've used 70-80% but still wish to fit in a short pass to be productive without getting cut off.  Most passes would not have these limitations, only when I get near the end of usage.  I'll let you know what this next pass uses and we will have a better idea on this calculation."): (c), built: pass NK built six lessons in two waves, each wave built, read by a round of reads on a frozen copy and saved as a checkpoint, then one final read, one check.sh and one set of records; its DECISIONS entry gives the usage at the start (42 percent of his week for all models, 44 for Fable) and how to work out a lesson's share once he reports the end (section 9's row on six lessons a pass). The question as it stood: How much each pass builds (pass NJ's weekly flow review, run on Fable, and your question when you opened pass NJ: "How long do you think, at this rate, it will take to complete up through 1st grade?"; raised in pass NJ). Where: not in the app, but in how much a pass builds, how often a delivery reaches you, and how much of your week's usage each one takes. Who: you, and the build. What happens today: since pass NE a pass that builds lessons builds two (passes NC and ND built five each), each lesson with its banks, its story and its Wonder questions, and every pass pays the same costs whatever it builds: reading the pass packet, about two and a half hours of check.sh, the reads of the records, the preview and the zip. You see about 30 percent of your week used a pass. Pass NI, with two lessons, came to at least $285 at API prices (its first six hours uncounted), and passes NG and NH, which built no lesson but other things, to about $190 each, so what every pass pays could be as much as two thirds of a two-lesson pass (a rough bound from three passes; the flow review judges it the larger part). The flow review puts what is left through grade 1 at about 94 passes (41 to 159), about 7 months at three passes a week (3 to 12), if a pass that builds lessons builds about three, the average of the passes that built any; at today's two the same count comes to about 120 passes by Claude's arithmetic on the review's table (about 112 to 124), nearer 9 or 10 months. The pre-K lessons still to write (about 90) are its largest part, about 30 of the 94; kindergarten's review is about 20, and grade 1's review and earmarks about 25. For example, the pre-K lessons alone take about 45 passes at two lessons a pass and about 15 at six, though each bigger pass takes more of your week; the review estimates that six to eight lessons a pass would bring the 94 to about 65. Choices: (a) keep two lessons a pass; (b) try it once: the next pass that builds lessons builds four, in two waves of two, each wave written, checked and read on its own and saved as a checkpoint, then one final read, one check.sh and one set of records; you say what share of your week it used, and the pass after it settles on four, six or eight; (c) go straight to six to eight lessons a pass, in two waves, with no trial first. Claude recommends (b): what every pass pays is paid once however much a pass builds, so the lessons built in a week should rise, but how far depends on a number only you can see, the share of your week a bigger pass uses, and a long pass can run out of usage or lose its context midway (pass NC ran out of usage at five lessons), which a checkpoint after each wave limits. With bigger passes you get fewer deliveries, each with more in it to look at, and a question waits a little longer for the pass that reads your answer.
- Q102. Answered in pass NK (Mikey: "Q102: B is okay, only if you think this doesn't introduce risk that would lead to reduced accuracy or quality.  You can offer more suggestions if you find any later."): (b), built: the records written once, at the pass's end, after the last content was checked, each fact once where it belongs with a pointer elsewhere; every count NEW-CHAT, HANDOFF, the Project Context and RULES-AUDIT give of the app as it stands written between hidden markers that tools/record-counts.mjs, new, fills in from the code; the docs sync test holding every marker and every pointer, each with a plant, which answers the risk he named (section 9's row on the records written once). The question as it stood: Each fact written once in a pass's records (pass NJ's weekly flow review, run on Fable; raised in pass NJ). Where: not in the app, but in the documents every pass writes (DECISIONS, NEW-CHAT, HANDOFF, the commit message, WHATS-NEW, RULES-AUDIT's rows, CLAUDE.md's pass lines and the Project Context) and in how much of each pass goes to them. Who: you, the next chat, and the build. What happens today: a pass writes the same facts, its counts above all (courses, modules, stories, tests, checks), by hand in several of those files, and its readers then hold each copy against the others: since pass NG about 54 of about 121 findings (45 percent) were about the records alone, against about 51 that a learner would meet, and pass NG needed a fourth read for its records only. For example, a pass that adds a lesson changes the module count in NEW-CHAT, HANDOFF, DECISIONS and the Project Context, and a reader finds the one copy left at the old count. Choices: (a) keep it as it is; (b) each fact written once, where it belongs (a pass's story in its DECISIONS entry, a rule in its RULES-AUDIT row), with a short line elsewhere that points to it; the counts filled in by a tool from the code, in RULES-AUDIT's rows too, which otherwise change only at your word or to say what the code already does (Q85); CLAUDE.md's new pass lines say the rule a coder must follow and point to the RULES-AUDIT row for its detail; and the records written once, after the last content is checked, rather than corrected through three rounds of reads. Under (b) nothing already written is removed or shortened, nothing that stands in only one place becomes a pointer, and the docs sync test holds every pointer to the line it points at. It touches your words of pass MS on CLAUDE.md ("I would rather that than you having to summarize to save space.   I don't want important information to skip through the cracks."): nothing in CLAUDE.md would be summarized or cut; only its new pass lines would be written shorter, their detail kept word for word in the row they point to. Claude recommends (b): it aims at the largest kind of finding since pass NG, and the flow review estimates it would save a tenth to a seventh of a pass; the risk is that a new chat or an agent reads less by default, since CLAUDE.md is what every agent loads, which is why each rule itself stays there and only its detail moves.
- Q103. Answered in pass NK (Mikey: "Q103: A"): (a), built: nothing to build; his order stands, young learners first trying the app after build 1's pictures and sounds (section 10). The question as it stood: When young learners first try the app (raised in pass NJ, from your new order: "I think I'm going to change the order of our build a little bit so that I can have some young learners test for us."). Where: the app as it stands, on a tablet, with a few children of pre-K age and a grown-up beside each. Who: a few young learners, and you. What happens today: by your new order children first try it after grade 1's review and build 1's pictures and sounds, about 7 to 10 months away by the flow review's count and the lessons a pass builds (Q101; 3 to 12 at the extremes), and then the time the pictures and sounds take; until then no child uses it, while about 90 more pre-K lessons are built in the same shape as today's. The app already works without the paintings and the recordings: its own drawings stand in for the pictures, and the device's voice and the app's own letter sounds speak. A case study of testing an app with three- and four-year-olds in two nurseries, in the proceedings of IDC 2023 (Read and others), used 20-minute sessions in groups of two to seven, smiley faces for whether the children liked it, no names, recordings or video kept, parents' consent through the nursery, and a child's wordless no respected. For example, three four-year-olds each try two of pre-K 4's letters lessons on a tablet while you watch where they tap, what they cannot hear and when they lose interest; a screen that stops every child changes how the next ninety lessons are built. Choices: (a) keep your order: children first try it after build 1's pictures and sounds; (b) a short early look as well: a few children try the pre-K lessons built so far, as they are, with a one-page guide from Claude for the grown-up beside them (what to watch for and write down, and the study's tips), and what you see shapes the lessons still to build, your order otherwise unchanged and the full test, with pictures and sounds, still after grade 1. Claude recommends (b): what a child shows is cheapest to fix before the lessons still to come copy it, and nothing else in your order moves; what an early look cannot tell you is how the paintings and the recorded voices land, which waits for the full test.
- Q104. Answered in pass NK (Mikey: "Q104:  B and keep at the bottom"): (b), built: on a screen under 1,000 pixels wide the countdown chip is hidden while a sheet is open until its last ten seconds, then shows at the foot; the sheet's room at its top back to 24 pixels, and while the chip can show 68 at its foot, so a sheet scrolled to its end has no word or button under it (since round one's screen read); hidden by its opacity, in the accessibility tree, taking no taps (section 8's row on the countdown chip). The question as it stood: Where the educator's countdown chip sits while a sheet is open on a phone or a tablet (raised in pass NJ, when its screen reads found the chip over a question sheet's only button). Where: the Wonder Questions sheet, the add-a-student sheet and a lesson's story sheet, on any screen under 1,000 pixels wide, while an educator is signed in. Who: every educator on a phone or a tablet. What happens today: in September you had the chip count down the whole minute, since it reminds an educator not to hand the device to a student too soon, and on phones it sits centered at the foot (DECISIONS, September 14). At the foot it lay over the sheet's only button as many sheets opened (226 of 402 Wonder sheets on a 375-pixel phone), so since this pass, while a sheet is open, the chip moves to the top of the screen, into 56 pixels the sheet keeps clear. As a sheet opens the chip is clear of its card and its buttons; but as the educator scrolls, the sheet's lines pass under it (scrolled to its end, words lie under it in 368 of the 402), and the extra room puts the button lower, wholly in view as the sheet opens in 34 of the 402 (125 before this change). Choices: (a) keep the chip at the top while a sheet is open; (b) hide the chip while a sheet is open until its last ten seconds, then show it as now, the sheet's room at its top back to 24 pixels; (c) the chip back at the foot over sheets, and each sheet's button moved to its top, beside its On or Off tag. Claude recommends (b): nothing is covered and the button is back where it was, and the warning still comes when it matters, though for the time a sheet is open it shortens your whole-minute countdown to its last ten seconds.

| **Domain III's III.C.2 and III.C.3, read (pass ND, the standards review; Claude's readings):** PK3 III.C.2 ("recognizes some distinct letter-sound correspondences with adult assistance.") and III.C.3 ("produces some distinct letter-sound correspondences with adult assistance."); PK4 III.C.2 ("recognizes at least 20 distinct letter-sound correspondences.") and III.C.3 ("produces at least 15 distinct letter-sound correspondences."). A letter-sound correspondence is a letter and the sound it says, recognized when the child finds the letter for a sound heard, the sound for a letter shown, or the picture whose first sound a letter says, and produced when the child says it in a turn of their own; pre-K 3's "some", with the voice the adult assistance, is met by three letters (M, S and B, whole), and pre-K 4's twenty and fifteen are begun by ten (in part, the rest named), by fourteen since pass NE and by eighteen since pass NF (the rows below), and since pass NI the twenty are reached, twenty-one letters' sounds across five lessons, each lesson citing III.C.2 in part and naming the others, while the child says thirteen of the fifteen (in part, the rest named; pass NI's row below). A letter's sound is the app's own clip (SPEECH_SOUNDS: m, n, s, f, z, v since pass NI, r, l, t, p, k, b, d, g and j), never a lone letter a voice reads as its name, or since pass NF, for H and W, the device voice's own huh and wuh (VOICE_SOUNDS, beside shh), and since pass NI, for Q and Y, its kwuh and yuh, as every lesson has said them since pass MO, and the short vowels wait while the app's own are on trial (Q60, EARMARKS A42), so the first letters are consonants; pre-K 4's are letters whose names hold their sounds, heard at the start (B, T, K, J and Z) or the end (M, S, F, L and R) of the name, since children learn those sounds sooner (Treiman, Tincoff, Rodriguez, Mouzaki and Francis, 1998, Child Development 69(6)). Next by the same reading: P and D, N and X, built in pass NE, then C, G, H and W, whose names do not hold their sounds, built in pass NF, then Q, V and Y, built in pass NI, then the vowels, each said by the word it starts. Corrected in place in pass NE (Mikey's answer to Q85), its old words in docs/RULES-AUDIT-HISTORY.md, and again in pass NF, its pass NE words there too, and again in pass NI, its pass NF words there too. | the three letters lessons' sources; PREK_TAUGHT; EARMARKS' 2027 map | the coverage test (the plans' rows); the rules test (the twenty-five banks re-derived from what each question says and shows by the test's own table, ND_LETTERS, with their near misses, and everything a bank uses said in its lesson) |

quietStart now starts the count again from now); a set of screens that turns a behavior on is a list in logic, named one by one in a test, and every member of what it is drawn from is placed once, on it or on a named list with a reason (STUDENT_SCREENS had a floor of fifteen that let two drop unseen, and placing every screen found six educator screens the educator's clock never covered, EARMARKS A131); a default changed for everyone is read for the people who chose before it, and what reaches them is said to them (a school that approved only some Wonder questions now has every one on, told in What's new and to Mikey); a one-tap action that undoes many careful choices is read for the mis-tap undone (Turn them all off and then on had cleared every question a school turned off; a question turned off by name now stays off until turned on by name); a build or a level read from a page's place is read against what a child can open (Let's Color opens a page for each lesson passed, so its last four pages, at grade 2's style, open in pre-K: every page is build 1's, and A130 rereads the style levels); a control a pass makes central is read on a phone with every layer the screen can show over it, and in each of its states for words at 4.5 to 1 and a 44-pixel target (the educator's countdown chip lay over the Wonder sheet's only button; an Off row read 2.9 to 1; the link to turn them all off was 16 pixels tall); a guard that only a page's timing shows is held in the code by a read and a plant, since the test page and the product page can time it differently (the tap after a sign-out held back on the product page and never on the test page); and What's new is written before round one's freeze, since every educator meets its popup first (both round one reads found the newest block still asking for approval), and read for what a change costs as well as what it gives (round two's final read: it said nothing was lost at an idle sign-out, where a half-done practice round is); What is written and checked today: the audio ledger (docs/AUDIO-LEDGER.md and .csv) lists every clip with its key, group and tagged words; every clip stays far under Eleven v4's 10,000-character request (longest clip 520 characters, longest story 3,567, and the longest lesson's lines 13,492 in all, each line a request of its own); tools/audio-generate.mjs sends each clip with the words before and after (previous_text, next_text) so a story sounds like one telling, skips clips already made, and saves audio/<key>.mp3; the age band carries a voice direction; the app plays recorded clips once every clip of a story or lesson line exists and keeps the device voice until then.
**The voices a lesson shows (pass MV, the standards review of Domain II: II.C.2 at both ages, a child mimicking and using intonation, and Head Start's P-LC 4 for three-year-olds, a tone and volume for different situations; the design is Claude's):** a lesson that teaches a voice says its example in that voice, so the child hears it before copying it. A script line or a question lists its sentences and the manner of each (voices: [[sentence, manner]], or [[sentence, manner, 'turn']] when the child says it back), and the screen shows the words as written; VOICE_MANNERS in src/logic.mjs holds the eleven manners (a whisper, an inside voice, a big outside voice, a kind, soft voice, a voice up high, a voice down low, asking, telling, one word said strong, a happy voice and a sad one), each with the device voice's rate, pitch and volume and the Eleven tag the audio ledger writes before the sentence ([whispers], [in a high voice, way up high] and the rest); for a young learner the young voice slows a manner with the words around it, so a sentence the child copies comes no quicker than the line before it unless it is said happy, no manner but a happy voice, which can be quick, being quicker than the plain voice (up high at its pace), and a big outside voice is drawn out, as a call is, rather than quick; a voice the device fetches over the network is asked no faster than rate 2, which some are reported to need (since round one's screen check). A device voice cannot truly whisper or call out, and some phones ignore how loud a voice is asked to be, so a question asks by ear only what every device voice can show, pitch, pace, a question's lift and a strong word, never loudness alone; a word said strong is the sentence's one word written in capitals, said as a piece of its own and handed to the voice in small letters, and a question that asks for it prints its sentence in small letters while the device can speak, so the screen never shows the answer; with no device voice (the claude.ai preview, a device without one) a sentence said in a manner prints a cue in parentheses for the grown-up who reads the question aloud (mannerCues: "Listen. I see a dog. (For the grown-up: say it up high.)", headed for the grown-up since round two so it is not read to the child as words to copy), a question asked by ear prints the same setup whatever its answer while the device can speak (since round two: the feelings question printed the happy sentence with "!", printedStory; with no voice the grown-up's cue must say how), asking, telling and a strong word showing by their end marks and capitals (since round one's lesson, screen and code checks), and the words after an answer are said with its question's voices. Held by the rules test (the manners case, each sentence said in its manner's shape and a turn after a 'turn' sentence, each manner's direction beside the plain voice, with ten near misses; the voices heard through the screens' own speak() and speakSequence() in node:vm, pitch and volume with the rate; the no-voice cue over 300 seeds of every bank with voices, with five near misses), the scripts test (every voices entry a sentence of its own words standing once, a manner the table has with its end mark, a strong sentence with one word in capitals, a strong-word question printed small and keyed to its word, a turn exactly where a line asks for one, nothing asked by loudness alone; twelve near misses, the turn rule's both ways since the third read; and a heard question printed alike whatever its answer while the device can speak, over every bank with voices since the third read, with its near miss), the question screens test (the voices played in the real page, and the cue with no voice), the voice check page's rows 73 to 84 and its self-test (its tenth to thirteenth plants: rows with their voices taken off, every shape at the plain pitch, no loudness, and its own copy of a voice up high at the plain pitch), and the docs sync test (the ledger made from the code). EARMARKS A62 holds the older lessons that name a voice without saying it that way.

V's name starts with its sound, vvv, as B's holds its sound; Q's and Y's names do not tell theirs (Q's starts with cuh and Y's with wuh), so each is taught by the words it starts, kwuh in queen and quilt, vvv in van and volcano and yuh in yo-yo and yarn (Merriam-Webster, read November 12, 2026). PK4 IV.C.1 ("writes first name using letters in the proper sequence with adult support as needed.") is taught in part in Write your name: a name's letters always go in the same order, from left to right, the big first letter first, tries checked against a grown-up's writing, each by a tap, and the child's own name, written under a grown-up's on paper, is the child's and the classroom's, since the app keeps no names and nothing a child writes; IV.C.4 is cited in part there for left to right, top to bottom being the tracing lessons', whose four citations now say so. The questions answered in pass NL (Mikey's opening message: "Q105 - B" and "Q106 - let's remove the wonder question segment altogether when adding a new student.   They don't need to see it at that time anymore.", with his words for the two Wonder cards; later in the pass "Q105, yeah the on and turn off can change to Approved and Remove" and, to Q107, raised and sent to him in the pass, "B"; his messages are in DECISIONS' pass NL entry), as they stood in section 11, each with what was built.

<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass NL, word for word: begin -->
- Q105. Answered in pass NL (Mikey: "Q105 - B", and later in the same message, of the page's card as it stood before pass NJ: "We can remove the 402 line and change the remaining text to: Students only see what you approve (yes, let's stick with this terminology over turn on and turn off)."): (b), built first, "View, turn off or turn on below." in place of "View, Remove or Approve below.", and taken out by round one's screen read, since his later words set turn on and turn off aside; built: the card says his line, "Students only see what you approve.", with no count line while any question is on and, with all removed, a line above it saying so (Claude's reading); and at his later word in the pass, "Q105, yeah the on and turn off can change to Approved and Remove", every on and off word of the page in approve and remove, the classroom popup's count too (Claude's reading). The question as it stood: The Wonder Questions page's card line beside the words on its buttons (raised in pass NK, when your words for the card were built). Where: the Wonder Questions page, which an educator opens from the classroom's Wonder Questions card; its card at the top shows the count ("All 406 on"), then your line, "View, Remove or Approve below.", then a Turn them all off button (Turn them all on when all are off), and each question opens in a sheet whose one button says Turn it off, or Turn it on for a question turned off. Who: every educator. What happens today: since pass NJ every question is on from the start and nothing waits for approval, so the sheet's buttons and the page's own count say on and off ("All 406 on", "Off, with 3 turned on"), while the card's line says remove and approve; for example, an educator who reads your line and looks for a Remove button finds Turn it off. Choices: (a) keep your line as it is; (b) your line in the page's words, "View, turn off or turn on below."; (c) the sheet's buttons in your line's words, Remove and Approve, the page's count and the card's Turn them all off unchanged, so on and off would still stand beside your line. Claude recommends (b): one pair of words on the page, the pair that says what a tap does today, though (a) keeps your words exactly and (c) keeps your words and changes the app's.
- Q106. Answered in pass NL (Mikey: "Q106 - let's remove the wonder question segment altogether when adding a new student.   They don't need to see it at that time anymore."): his own answer, built: Add a student has no Wonder segment and no tip, the PIN its last choice; a new student's Wonder questions start on, as every student's do since pass NJ, and each student's own card keeps its switch. The question as it stood: The add-a-student tip's pointer to the Wonder card (raised in pass NK). Where: the tip that opens from the i beside Wonder Questions when an educator adds a student; it ends "All questions are active by default. However, you can turn off any you'd rather skip (see bottom of My Classroom).", your line of this pass and then your words of an earlier pass. Who: every educator adding a student. | **Which model a task runs on (Mikey, pass NC: "If you are able to delegate any simple tasks to Sonnet 5.5 to be more efficient with our usage, you can.   Only do it if it maintains your accuracy, we don't want to make mistakes by doing this.  You can also delegate an agent to Fable 5.1 if it improves quality of work even if efficiency isn't better.  Maybe like the over aching vision agent or agent responsible for writing creatively since Fable is good at that."; pass NF: "You may know this already but I would like to switch from Fable 5.1 to Fable 5.2.  We should always use the newest versions of each model.  Please continue" and "It should also act as the advisor moving forward and documentation should update to reflect this."; and pass NG: "If there is a better structure, when it comes to incorporating multiple models to our process and assigning agents, you can make those changes.  We just want to use each model for it's strengths (where it makes the most sense) with the idea of efficiency, accuracy and prestige in mind.  If Fable 5.2 is better than Opus at a task, for example, we can make sure the corresponding agent uses Fable instead of Opus." and "I think Fable has better research capabilities so it should also be used for the self-improvement research each week and for restructuring our flow if things can be improved."):** the model map, since pass NG (Claude's design within his words, from Anthropic's model guide and the Fable 5.2 benchmarks, read November 11, 2026, and the weekly flow review): every agent and check runs on the newest version of its model (Fable 5.2, Opus 5.5 and Sonnet 5.5 when pass NG wrote this). The lead, which builds the lessons, banks, code and records and spends most of a pass's tokens, runs on Opus, Anthropic's own default for most work, and raises its effort on a hard step before anything moves up a model, the order Anthropic's guide gives. Fable writes the stories and the Wonder questions; runs the weekly research sweep, the weekly flow review, evidence briefs and any research a pass needs, where it leads Opus on Anthropic's agentic-search benchmark (WANDR 82.1 against 72.3); and reads the lessons, the code, the alignment and the final record, where it leads on Anthropic's coding and reasoning benchmarks. Opus reads the screens, which drive a browser through hundreds of screenshots, the one read on the builder's own model (the lead builds on Opus), the Fable code read covering the screen code, and reads what Fable wrote, the stories and the Wonder questions, so there a different model reads than wrote (said so since round two's final read, pass NG, which found the screen reads on the builder's model where this row had said every read was on the other). Sonnet does chores whose result a script can check (a count, a regeneration, a scan with a fixed list), at low effort with a failure run again at high. Fable is the advisor (advisorModel "fable" in .claude/settings.json; a cloud chat's own with /advisor fable before its opening message, since a conversation keeps the advisor it began with until /clear or /compact), consulted about three times a pass, before the pass's design is fixed, when an error keeps coming back and before delivery, since each consult reads the whole conversation uncached and Anthropic's guide measured an Opus executor with a Fable 5.1 advisor at 1.7 points over Opus alone for about 2.1 times the cost; an agent doing a chore is told not to consult it, since an agent inherits the advisor; and where a session's advisor is not Fable (pass NG's chat began with Opus 5.5) a briefed Fable agent does the advisor's checks. Fable's share of each pass is read from the session's logs (tools/usage-by-model.mjs, each reply once, at API prices, since a plan does not publish how it counts) and given in DECISIONS: about 40 percent of a week is the aim, and at 45 no new Fable work starts that week, so a long pass never runs past Fable's limit midway. When Fable's weekly share runs out anyway (on Ultra up to half of the weekly limit; past it Fable stops unless usage credits are on, which an Agent SDK workspace like this one may bill without asking, Claude Code's model configuration page), every Fable role moves to Opus at a higher effort, DECISIONS names each, and the Fable reads that pass missed are owed, listed in NEW-CHAT and done first the next week. The map is a hypothesis until a planted-defect set scores each reader on each model (EARMARKS L19), and a role moves only on that score or on the meter. | **Which model a task runs on (Mikey, pass NC: "If you are able to delegate any simple tasks to Sonnet 5.5 to be more efficient with our usage, you can.   Only do it if it maintains your accuracy, we don't want to make mistakes by doing this.  You can also delegate an agent to Fable 5.1 if it improves quality of work even if efficiency isn't better.  Maybe like the over aching vision agent or agent responsible for writing creatively since Fable is good at that."; pass NF: "You may know this already but I would like to switch from Fable 5.1 to Fable 5.2.  We should always use the newest versions of each model.  Please continue" and "It should also act as the advisor moving forward and documentation should update to reflect this."; and pass NG: "If there is a better structure, when it comes to incorporating multiple models to our process and assigning agents, you can make those changes.  We just want to use each model for it's strengths (where it makes the most sense) with the idea of efficiency, accuracy and prestige in mind.  If Fable 5.2 is better than Opus at a task, for example, we can make sure the corresponding agent uses Fable instead of Opus." and "I think Fable has better research capabilities so it should also be used for the self-improvement research each week and for restructuring our flow if things can be improved."):** the model map, since pass NG (Claude's design within his words, from Anthropic's model guide and the Fable 5.2 benchmarks, read November 11, 2026, and the weekly flow review): every agent and check runs on the newest version of its model (Fable 5.2, Opus 5.5 and Sonnet 5.5 when pass NG wrote this). The lead, which builds the lessons, banks, code and records and spends most of a pass's tokens, runs on Opus, Anthropic's own default for most work, and raises its effort on a hard step before anything moves up a model, the order Anthropic's guide gives. Fable writes the stories and the Wonder questions; runs the weekly research sweep, the weekly flow review, evidence briefs and any research a pass needs, where it leads Opus on Anthropic's agentic-search benchmark (WANDR 82.1 against 72.3); and reads the lessons, the code, the alignment and the final record, where it leads on Anthropic's coding and reasoning benchmarks. Opus reads the screens, which drive a browser through hundreds of screenshots, the one read on the builder's own model (the lead builds on Opus), the Fable code read covering the screen code, and reads what Fable wrote, the stories and the Wonder questions, so there a different model reads than wrote (said so since round two's final read, pass NG, which found the screen reads on the builder's model where this row had said every read was on the other). Sonnet does chores whose result a script can check (a count, a regeneration, a scan with a fixed list), at low effort with a failure run again at high. Fable is the advisor (advisorModel "fable" in .claude/settings.json; a cloud chat's own with /advisor fable before its opening message, since a conversation keeps the advisor it began with until /clear or /compact), consulted about three times a pass, before the pass's design is fixed, when an error keeps coming back and before delivery, since each consult reads the whole conversation uncached and Anthropic's guide measured an Opus executor with a Fable 5.1 advisor at 1.7 points over Opus alone for about 2.1 times the cost; an agent doing a chore is told not to consult it, since an agent inherits the advisor; and where a session's advisor is not Fable (pass NG's chat began with Opus 5.5) a briefed Fable agent does the advisor's checks. Fable's share of each pass is read from the session's logs (tools/usage-by-model.mjs, each reply once, at API prices, since a plan does not publish how it counts) and given in DECISIONS: about 40 percent of a week is the aim, and at 45 no new Fable work starts that week, so a long pass never runs past Fable's limit midway. Where a session keeps no log (passes NK to NM), Mikey's bars at a pass's start and end, each apart, are the measure, and Claude reads Fable's own bar as half the week, so 90 percent of it stands for the 45 percent here (pass NM's reading, not his word). Corrected in place in pass NM (Mikey's answer to Q85), its pass NL words in docs/RULES-AUDIT-HISTORY.md.
