- Q109. Answered in pass NO (Mikey: "Q109 - B"): written into the plan, band by band, in section 10, docs/NEW-CHAT.md and the Project Context: each band's new courses, stories, Wonder questions and games, and its cross-check, just before that band's build, and pre-K 3 to grade 1's right after build 1, with a short round of paintings and recordings for what they add. The question as it stood: Where do the depth program and the cross-check go, now that the paintings and recordings come in four builds? (Raised in pass NN by Claude, from Mikey's four builds.) Where: the plan, in docs/RULES-AUDIT.md section 10 and docs/NEW-CHAT.md; nobody meets it in the app, but it decides what each build's young testers get. Today the plan, at your word, reviews grades 2 to 5 and then paints and records them (build 2), then grades 6 to 8 (build 3), then grade 9 through college (build 4). It does not yet say where two steps go: new courses, stories, Wonder questions and games across the grades (the depth program), and the cross-check of everything against the newest edition of every framework. Before your change they came after every review and before the paintings for grade 2 and up. An example: a new grade 4 game. Under (a) it is built after build 4, and its pictures and sounds come in a closing round of paintings and recordings, so grade 4's testers meet it late. Under (b) it is built while grades 2 to 5 are reviewed, before build 2, so it is painted and recorded with the rest of grade 4. (a) After build 4, in one sweep across the grades, then paintings and recordings for what they add (how the plan reads now, as Claude's reading). (b) Band by band: each band's new courses, stories, Wonder questions and games, and its cross-check, come just before that band's build; pre-K 3 to grade 1's come right after build 1, with a short round of paintings and recordings for what they add. (c) Something else. Claude recommends (b): each band is painted and recorded once, whole, and its testers see every course, story and game it will have; the cost is that build 2 starts later, by as many passes as that band's new courses, stories, Wonder questions, games and cross-check take (not yet counted).
- Q110. Answered in pass NO (Mikey: "Q110 - B"): built sentence by sentence in pass NO wherever the voice lit the words it read (a lesson line, a story and More Info): the words ahead of the voice faint, each sentence full ink as the voice begins it, no box; the say-along's syllables and a Wonder choice being named keep the light green (Q111 asks about the second); word by word waits for the recordings (EARMARKS L25). The question as it stood: Should the words light up one at a time as they are read, like your Vooks video? (Raised in pass NN by Claude, from Mikey's video and his words: "I really like how the words are slightly faint and highlight as they are spoken.") Where: every place the app reads aloud with the words on the screen, a young child's lesson lines, stories and long stories, the questions and the Wonder voices; the child and the grown-up beside them meet it. Today the whole sentence being read gets a light green box at once (your choice in pass MH), and the say-it-together game lights each syllable. In the video the words not yet read are faint, and each word turns full white the moment the narrator says it and stays that way; no box marks it. The catch: a phone's own voice does not reliably tell the app when each word is said (the app reads only when a piece starts and ends; whether a device's voice reports each word differs by browser and is untested here), so exact word timing waits for the ElevenLabs recordings, and ElevenLabs can return each recording with the time of every letter in it (its "with timestamps" request, to be tried with v4 when the recordings start). An example: while "The duck lost her hat." is read, today the whole line is green; with (a) or (b), "The duck" is bright and "lost her hat." faint, then "lost" turns bright as it is said, and so on. (a) Word by word with the recordings: the audio tool saves each recording's word times, and every read-along turns Vooks-style, the words ahead faint and each word bright as it is said, in place of the green box; until then the green box stays. (b) The same as (a), but starting now sentence by sentence: the sentences ahead faint and each sentence bright as the voice begins it, then word by word once the recordings exist. (c) Keep the green box and add the word-by-word brightening inside it, with the recordings. Claude recommends (b): the calm Vooks look reaches every device now, with nothing guessed, and the exact word timing arrives with the recordings.

## 10. The plan, in Mikey's order (updated 2026-10-06, pass LE, 2026-11-12, pass NJ, and 2026-11-14, pass NN)
**Four builds since pass NN (Mikey, November 14, 2026, opening pass NN):** "Build 2 was grade 2 and up through college but I think it should be grade 2 through grade 5.  Build three will be grade 6 through grade 8.  Build four will be grade 9 and above.  These are manageable chunks that will allow testing to grow progressively as the building occurs." So, in place of the paragraph above's order after the early build, the steps run: steps 2 and 3 for grades 2 to 5, then build 2, step 7's illustrations and Eleven audio for grades 2 to 5; steps 2 and 3 for grades 6 to 8, then build 3; steps 2 and 3 for grade 9 through college, then build 4; then steps 5 and 6, with paintings and recordings for what they add, and the Texas page, licensing and the final checks. Where steps 5 and 6 go is Claude's reading, asked of him as Q109 (section 11); the ledgers mark everything from grade 2 up as build 2 until they are split (EARMARKS L24). Q61's "after the standards review" reads, for each later build, after its grades' review.

The lead, which builds the lessons, banks, code and records and spends most of a pass's tokens, runs on Opus, Anthropic's own default for most work, and raises its effort on a hard step before anything moves up a model, the order Anthropic's guide gives. Sonnet does chores whose result a script can check (a count, a regeneration, a scan with a fixed list), at low effort with a failure run again at high. Fable is the advisor (advisorModel "fable" in .claude/settings.json; a cloud chat's own with /advisor fable before its opening message, since a conversation keeps the advisor it began with until /clear or /compact), consulted about three times a pass, before the pass's design is fixed, when an error keeps coming back and before delivery, since each consult reads the whole conversation uncached and Anthropic's guide measured an Opus executor with a Fable 5.1 advisor at 1.7 points over Opus alone for about 2.1 times the cost; an agent doing a chore is told not to consult it, since an agent inherits the advisor; and where a session's advisor is not Fable (pass NG's chat began with Opus 5.5) a briefed Fable agent does the advisor's checks. TEA's review committee has proposed a revision of the Prekindergarten Guidelines (public feedback September 15 to 30, 2026; EARMARKS L17): when TEA adopts it, every pre-K citation is read again against the new text. Since pass MR (Mikey: "I think we should go ahead and implement the new recommendations from the 2027 Pre-K Guidelines Review Committee.") every pre-K citation already quotes the recommendations (section 5), so when TEA adopts final guidelines, only the outcomes that differ from docs/standards' 2027 files are requoted (docs/standards/README.md, the edition rule).

The question answered in pass NP (Q111, answered by number in Mikey's message opening pass NP, "Q111 - A"; DECISIONS' pass NP entry), as section 11 held it, with what was kept.

<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass NP, word for word: begin -->
- Q111. Answered in pass NP (Mikey: "Q111 - A"): (a), kept as built: a Wonder choice keeps its light green and its little grow while the voice names it, and since pass NP the Wonder question, its voice cards and its closing card read along around it, sentence by sentence (section 8's read-along row). The question as it stood: Should a Wonder choice keep its green light while the voice names it? (Raised in pass NO by Claude, from Mikey's answer to Q110.) Where: Let's Wonder! for a child who cannot read yet, after every two lessons; the child and the grown-up beside them meet it. Since your answer to Q110 (b), every sentence the voice reads on a lesson line, in a story and in More Info follows the Vooks look: the words not yet read faint, each sentence full ink as the voice begins it, and no box. Two places still wear the old light green: the say-it-together game's syllables, which you asked for in your own words, and the answer buttons of a Wonder question. This question is about the buttons. While the voice names each choice, that button turns light green and grows a little. Claude kept it on purpose: a child who cannot read finds the button the voice is naming by that light, and buttons whose words are faint can look as if they cannot be tapped yet. An example: today, while the voice says "Say sorry.", that button is green and a little bigger, and the other two look as they always do. Under (b), all three buttons start faint, and "Say sorry." turns full ink, with no green, as the voice says it. (a) Keep it: the green light and the little grow on the button being named, the Vooks look on every sentence the voice reads. (b) The Vooks look on the buttons too: each button's words faint until the voice names its choice, then full ink, the little grow kept and the green gone. (c) Something else. Claude recommends (a): the light is how a child who cannot read joins a spoken choice to the button to tap, a different job from following words, and the say-along's syllables keep the same light at your own words.

| **Which model a task runs on (Mikey, pass NC: "If you are able to delegate any simple tasks to Sonnet 5.5 to be more efficient with our usage, you can.   Only do it if it maintains your accuracy, we don't want to make mistakes by doing this.  You can also delegate an agent to Fable 5.1 if it improves quality of work even if efficiency isn't better.  Maybe like the over aching vision agent or agent responsible for writing creatively since Fable is good at that."; pass NF: "You may know this already but I would like to switch from Fable 5.1 to Fable 5.2.  We should always use the newest versions of each model.  Please continue" and "It should also act as the advisor moving forward and documentation should update to reflect this."; and pass NG: "If there is a better structure, when it comes to incorporating multiple models to our process and assigning agents, you can make those changes.  We just want to use each model for it's strengths (where it makes the most sense) with the idea of efficiency, accuracy and prestige in mind.  If Fable 5.2 is better than Opus at a task, for example, we can make sure the corresponding agent uses Fable instead of Opus." and "I think Fable has better research capabilities so it should also be used for the self-improvement research each week and for restructuring our flow if things can be improved."):** the model map, since pass NG (Claude's design within his words, from Anthropic's model guide and the Fable 5.2 benchmarks, read November 11, 2026, and the weekly flow review): every agent and check runs on the newest version of its model (Fable 5.2, Opus 5.5 and Sonnet 5.5 when pass NG wrote this). The lead, which builds the lessons, banks, code and records and spends most of a pass's tokens, runs on Opus, Anthropic's own default for most work, and raises its effort on a hard step before anything moves up a model, the order Anthropic's guide gives. Fable writes the stories and the Wonder questions; runs the weekly research sweep, the weekly flow review, evidence briefs and any research a pass needs, where it leads Opus on Anthropic's agentic-search benchmark (WANDR 82.1 against 72.3); and reads the lessons, the code, the alignment and the final record, where it leads on Anthropic's coding and reasoning benchmarks. Opus reads the screens, which drive a browser through hundreds of screenshots, the one read on the builder's own model (the lead builds on Opus), the Fable code read covering the screen code, and reads what Fable wrote, the stories and the Wonder questions, so there a different model reads than wrote (said so since round two's final read, pass NG, which found the screen reads on the builder's model where this row had said every read was on the other). Sonnet does chores whose result a script can check (a count, a regeneration, a scan with a fixed list), at low effort with a failure run again at high. Fable is the advisor (advisorModel "fable" in .claude/settings.json; a cloud chat's own with /advisor fable before its opening message, since a conversation keeps the advisor it began with until /clear or /compact), consulted about three times a pass, before the pass's design is fixed, when an error keeps coming back and before delivery, since each consult reads the whole conversation uncached and Anthropic's guide measured an Opus executor with a Fable 5.1 advisor at 1.7 points over Opus alone for about 2.1 times the cost; an agent doing a chore is told not to consult it, since an agent inherits the advisor; and where a session's advisor is not Fable (pass NG's chat began with Opus 5.5) a briefed Fable agent does the advisor's checks. Fable's share of each pass is read from the session's logs (tools/usage-by-model.mjs, each reply once, at API prices, since a plan does not publish how it counts) and given in DECISIONS: about 40 percent of a week was the aim, with no new Fable work past 45, and since pass NN, at Mikey's word ("You don't really need to keep Fable under 90, just under 100%."), Fable's work is planned so its own bar stays just under 100 percent, each pass's Fable work sized to end before the bar does, so a long pass never runs past Fable's limit midway (the sizing Claude's plan). Where a session keeps no log (passes NK to NO), Mikey's bars at a pass's start and end, each apart, are the measure (pass NM read Fable's own bar as half the week, so that 90 percent of it stood for the 45 percent, its reading, not his word, set aside by his words in pass NN). The questions answered in pass NQ (Q112 to Q116, answered by number in Mikey's message opening pass NQ, "Q112 - C", "Q113 - A", "Q114 - A", "Q115 - A" and "Q116 - A"; DECISIONS' pass NQ entry), as section 11 held them, with what was built.

<!-- RULES-AUDIT section 11's answered questions in pass NQ, word for word: begin -->
- Q112. Answered in pass NQ (Mikey: "Q112 - C"): (c), built: the reason after a wrong tap wears no read-along look on its clay strip while the voice reads it, printed in other words or not, no faint and no mark, at full ink as at rest, and Correct on the green strip keeps its faint (section 8's read-along row; EARMARKS A146 and L25); the rules test and question-screens section 20 hold it, pass NP's look put back and caught. The question as it stood: Should the reason after a wrong tap stay at full ink while it is read? (Raised in pass NP by the read-along engineer and round one's screen read, from Mikey's answer to Q110.) Where: the card after a wrong tap in a read-aloud course (pre-K 3 through grade 1, and two of grade 2's five courses), where the voice reads the reason aloud. Who: a child who cannot read yet, and the grown-up beside them. Since pass NP the words the voice has not reached are faint in a question, on the card after an answer, on the Wonder screens and in the whole book, as in lessons, stories and More Info. But the reason after a wrong tap sits on a clay strip whose ink reads only 4.80 to 1 at full strength in the light theme, so its faint cannot show and still read 4.5 to 1: it is 0.97 in the light theme, invisible, and 0.88 in the dark. An example: in Talking with a friend, a child taps "Right in your friend's face" for "How close can you stand when you talk with a friend?". The clay strip says "Right in your friend's face is too close. Give your friend some room. Have another try.", and while the voice reads it the sentences ahead should look faint, as they do on the green strip after a right answer, but here they look the same as the sentence being read. (a) Leave it as built: the marks are there but cannot be seen, the two themes differ, and a check accepts a faint that no one can see. (b) While the voice reads, the reason takes the card's own ink, so its faint shows (0.67 light, 0.55 dark), and takes its clay back when the voice ends: a change of color on every wrong tap, and the clay that says "not this one" is lost exactly while it is said. (c) No faint on the clay strip: the reason stays at full ink while it is read (it is one to three short sentences, said while the child's eyes go back to the tiles); the green strip keeps its faint, and the check then requires no faint mark on the clay. A darker clay is no way out: even at 7 to 1 its faint could only reach 0.81. Claude recommends (c), as round one's screen read did (its SHOULD CONSIDER 1, kept word for word in the pass's scratch folder, r1-screen-opus.md).
- Q113. Answered in pass NQ (Mikey: "Q113 - A"): (a), the standing week: two lesson passes and one pass for the checks' own tools, unless his opening message says otherwise (section 9's row on a pass of six lessons in two waves, docs/NEW-CHAT.md and the Project Context); pass NQ was the first tools pass, building the planted-defect set (EARMARKS L19), check.sh's step times, the floor guard and the coverage receipt (L20), the lead's scratch tools in the tree (L26) and the pass packet's honest count (L27). The question as it stood: Should a standing week be two lesson passes and one pass for the checks' own tools? (Raised in pass NP by Claude, from the weekly flow review's item 6, run on Fable.) Where: not in the app, but in how each week's passes are planned and how much of your week each one spends. Who: you, and the build. Plan the week, not the pass: with about 100 points of the all-models bar a week, and about 4 points a lesson all-in (pass NK's six lessons), a week holds two lesson passes of about eight lessons each (pass NP planned eight and built six, with the read-along beside them) and one pass that builds the checks' own tools, the planted-defect set first (EARMARKS L19), which the review reads as the only way to tell whether a read can be dropped or moved, then check.sh's step times and the guard against a test floor moved the lenient way with no reason given (L20), which it expects to remove most of round three's findings at their source. Both have waited since pass NG. An example: this week's passes would be NP (six lessons), then a tools pass, then a lesson pass. (a) Yes, that standing week, unless your opening message says otherwise. (b) Lessons only, the tools built when a pass has room. (c) You set each week's plan in your opening message. Claude recommends (a): the tools give later passes the evidence to spend less of each week on reads, and a standing plan spends the bar on purpose.
- Q114. Answered in pass NQ (Mikey: "Q114 - A"): (a), built: the look tool counts hairstyle, garment and skin, so two people count as alike only when hair and clothing color, hairstyle and garment all match and their skin does not set them apart, skin read into three families (light, tan and brown), and the hair readings count skin as a mark that sets two apart (Claude's reading); the 80 older pairs and 52 of the 53 wide ones went, the founding case among them, each held as no pair by the ledger test (section 3's look rows; EARMARKS A147). The question as it stood: Should the look tool count more than hair and clothing color, so new pre-K stories can show more kinds of children? (Raised in pass NP by Claude, from round two's story and picture read.) Where: the people in every new pre-K story and its paintings (docs/ART-REQUESTS.md), which you paint in Leonardo. Who: the children who read the pre-K stories, and every writer who picks a new person's look. The look tool keeps two painted people near each other from sharing a hair color and a clothing color, and near the pre-K Reading courses it now leaves no free clothing color for a boy with black, brown or blond hair, so this pass's six stories have four blond children. An example: Clyde, the boy in The pointing job, was first written with red hair, close by eye to red-haired boys near him; off red, blond hair with cream pajamas was the only look the tool would pass, and he became the fourth blond lead of six. (a) Count skin, hairstyle and garment too, so two people set apart by any of them no longer count as alike: more looks free, and more variety. (b) Read only the nearest lessons, by their distance, rather than whole courses. (c) Both. (d) Keep it as it is. Claude recommends (a): children tell people apart by their whole look, and the paintings already write skin, hairstyle and garment, so counting them keeps neighbors distinct while opening the palette, where (b) would let two children a few lessons apart in one course look alike.
- Q115. Answered in pass NQ (Mikey: "Q115 - A"): (a), written into the plan: kindergarten's and grade 1's first long stories are written inside each course's review, before build 1 is painted, as pre-K's were (section 10's step 5; EARMARKS L1 and L9). The question as it stood: When are kindergarten's and grade 1's first long stories written: inside each course's review, before the early build is painted, or right after build 1 with the depth program's new stories? (Raised in pass NP by round three's whole read of EARMARKS, from L1.) Where: the long story a course opens when a child has passed every lesson once, and its next long story, opened when every star is earned. Who: children in kindergarten and grade 1, and you, who paint each one. Pre-K's four courses have theirs; sixteen courses from kindergarten to grade 2 still have none (EARMARKS L1), eleven of them kindergarten's and grade 1's. Two records place them differently. L9 has each course's long stories written inside that course's review (your answer to Q38, "Inside each review"), which puts kindergarten's and grade 1's before build 1; RULES-AUDIT section 10 lists them in the depth program (its step 5), which your answer to Q109, "Q109 - B", puts right after build 1. An example: kindergarten's Letters course would open a story of six paragraphs or more, up to 460 words with six pictures, when a child has passed its last lesson, painted either with the rest of the early build or after it with the depth program's stories. (a) Inside each course's review, before the early build is painted, as pre-K's were: eleven more long stories are written and read before your young testers get build 1, and every early course then ends with its story. (b) Right after build 1, in the depth program: build 1 reaches testers sooner, and kindergarten's and grade 1's courses end with no long story until a short second round of paintings and recordings. Claude recommends (a): the courses' first long stories then ship with build 1, as a course's ending, and only the depth program's further stories wait.
- Q116. Answered in pass NQ (Mikey: "Q116 - A"): (a), done: the British spellings made American in nineteen rows of Claude's own words outside section 2, the sixteen of the nineteen this question counted that stand outside it (section 2's three went with its rewrite) and three with "cancelled", by the spelling test's list and Merriam-Webster's first spelling, quotes of Mikey (his "judgement" in Q17) left as he wrote them; his note on the drawn pictures (section 5 and EARMARKS L15) and his generation-day line (EARMARKS L14) each marked with the record that keeps them word for word, docs/LEONARDO-BRIEF.md's Generation day, as pass MK's DECISIONS entry says; and section 2 rewritten once as the app today, its heading and lines as pass NP left them kept word for word below; each change named in section 9's row for Q85. The question as it stood: May Claude tidy RULES-AUDIT where no rule changes? (Raised in pass NP by round three's whole read of RULES-AUDIT, from the reads pass NM owed.) Where: the rules file every chat reads; no screen changes. Who: you, and every chat after this one. Its rows change only at your word or under Q85 (where the code has outgrown a row), so three tidy-ups have waited since those reads: British spellings in nineteen rows of Claude's own words, six in sections 2 to 6 and thirteen in sections 8 and 9 ("a sign-in picture with a colour", "centres", "centred", "practises", "greys out"); two quotes called yours that only other records hold, not your saved messages as DECISIONS quotes them (section 5's "you can leave the drawn in images for now until I can submit the new ones.", which EARMARKS L15 quotes too, and, in EARMARKS L14 and LEONARDO-BRIEF only, the line on image generation after the standards review); and section 2, "The product, as it stands", which has grown into a run of one line per pass. An example: the row that says a student signs in with "a colour" would say "a color", with its old words kept in the history file and nothing else changed. (a) Yes to all three: the spellings made American, each quote, in RULES-AUDIT and in EARMARKS, checked against your messages or marked with the record it comes from, and section 2 rewritten once as the product today, its pass lines moved word for word to the history file, each change recorded there. (b) The spellings and the quotes only. (c) Leave them. Claude recommends (a), with section 2's rewrite done in a pass that builds the checks' own tools, so no lesson pass carries it.

## 2. The product, as it stands (walked in pass LD; update this map when a screen changes)

- **Welcome:** "Who's learning today?" with the students' pictures; Educator Login; Create account; everything lives on this device.
- **Educator setup:** a four-to-six-digit PIN (recoverable only with a backup), the device's name (part of the backup file name), the state (Texas first; the plan maps TEKS and Common Core).
- **My Classroom:** add a student by school-issued ID, choose a level (Early years pre-K to grade 2; Elementary 3 to 5; Middle school 6 to 8; High school and beyond), a sign-in picture with a colour, an optional four-digit student PIN (since pass NL, at Mikey's answer to Q106, no Wonder choice there: a new student's Wonder questions start on, and each student's own card keeps the switch); rename, change picture, hide, merge; open the report. Quick checks on or off. Walk through as a student (every module open, nothing recorded). Wonder Questions, every one on for students unless the school turns it off on their page (since pass NJ, at Mikey's word; until then nothing reached a child until approved; changed in place, its pass NI words in docs/RULES-AUDIT-HISTORY.md). Reading lists, science experiments, practical life skills, backup, contact. Changed in place again in pass NL, at Mikey's word (his answer to Q106), its pass NK words in docs/RULES-AUDIT-HISTORY.md.
- **Placement (the truth as of pass LE, Mikey):** a student added below grade 3 gets no placement check, so the screens stay simple; a student added at grade 3 or above gets a placement check per subject that places them one notch conservatively; quick checks then let a student skip individual modules. When an early-years student is added, the grade picker says there is no placement check and, once a grade is chosen, a one-line note of what a child starting there should already be comfortable with (pass LE).
- **Student report:** notes (kept with the student, restorable, searchable from Who Needs Help); a summary in sentences (what is mastered per course, confidence, reflections); assigned courses with the recommended set and a way back to it; progress by course; other courses folded by grade and by core and electives; print.
- **Student side:** the overview of courses with modules; a lesson (key idea, paragraphs or a spoken script with a picture per line, example, story-based learning fold, pictures to come); practice (a round of five plus memory checks; pre-readers retry a wrong answer, "Not that one. Have another try."; older students see the explanation); results and stars (since pass MD a pre-reader's first passed round of a lesson with a story shows a book, and the story reads itself aloud on a page with one arrow; since pass ME the round that earns a read-aloud course's last star goes on to a course-finished page, confetti and a landing star, the course named aloud, a big gold book that reads the course's long story on a self-reading page and a smaller arrow that skips, and a Quick Review's cleared fresh try that earns the last star reaches it too; since pass MF the page ends at home, since pass MG on one wide Return home button across the bottom with no drawing (Mikey: "No home icon, just a home button across the bottom"; it replaced pass MF's gold house), the story the page opens ends at home too, from grade 6 up the page's line is calmer, "Well done. You finished" and the course's name (Mikey, Q24: "calmer"), and every course is celebrated, a reader's from a gold Celebrate! on the result or on a Quick Review's result); reflections (Wonder) after every two different lessons worked, pass or fail (corrected in place in pass NA, under Q85's row in section 9, its old words in docs/RULES-AUDIT-HISTORY.md); the course's long story and game when finished; Let's Color and Let's Play folds; certificates.

**The app's own sounds are measured against recordings (pass MU, Mikey's notes on the voice check page: "39 has a weird click sound but the duh sounds good.  Guh in 40 has the weird clicking noise too but sounds correct otherwise.  Through 44 they all sound better but have odd accompanying sounds."; "65, the whistle gets cut off too quickly.  It should be drawn out a bit more and slightly change the amplitude of the whistle sound to sound natural as if a real breath was required.  66 the clapping sounds like static."):** nobody can listen where the sounds are made, so each fault he heard is found in numbers first, and each fix is held by a measure the rules test reads, with a plant for each fault. The clicks: pass MT's d and g released about 10 dB over their uh, with an attack of a quarter of a millisecond after silence, d's with energy up to 16,000 cycles a second; since pass MU a d, g or j releases into its uh, weighed as the ear hears it (A-weighted, since round one's screen check found the first fix's d and j still 8 to 9 dB over their uh in the click's band): its part between 2,500 and 6,000 cycles a second, where a phone's click lives, no more than about 3 dB over the uh (its plain power against the uh as heard), the whole release 0 to 10 dB over it, with almost nothing over 7,000 cycles and no gap before the uh (juh's hush begins under its release); and the endings ig, og and at release at least 6 dB under their vowel, as heard too, where pass MT's popped a few decibels over it. The whistle: nine tenths of a second on one breath that builds, swells and is spent (2 to 9 dB of swing, loudest between a fifth and half a second in), swooping up to a held note near 2,750 cycles a second and sliding down as the breath runs out. The crowd: 44 people clapping in a small hall for two and a half seconds, each clap one of the eight hand shapes in Jylhä and Erkut's table (DAFx 2008, Table 1, based on Peltola, Erkut, Cook and Välimäki's synthesis of hand clapping, 2007), with the hall's echo and two whistles from the crowd, breaking out over its first third of a second rather than on a cue (round one's screen check); its brightness (its energy centred near 1,800 cycles, where pass MT's centred near 2,600), its share over 4,000 cycles, its peakiness, its claps a second and its swells are set against forty recordings of clapping from the ESC-50 set (Piczak 2015), used only to measure and never shipped. A sound whose end a phone never reports holds a reading its own length and 400 ms more (soundGuardMs), not as long as the longest sound. The voice check page's rows 39 to 44, 65 and 66 play them for Mikey. They stay stand-ins, made sounds however closely they measure: Q82 asked whether ElevenLabs' sound effects replace the whistle and the crowd, and since pass MV (Mikey's answer, "Q82: A") they will, once the recordings are made (after the standards review, Q61; for pre-K 3 to grade 1, since pass NJ at Mikey's word, after grade 1's review and earmarks, section 10's order, these two sounds among build 1's; changed in place, its pass NI words in docs/RULES-AUDIT-HISTORY.md): a few takes of each are made with ElevenLabs' sound-effects tool (one fan's two-finger whistle on a single breath, about a second; about forty people clapping and cheering in a school gym, about two and a half seconds), Mikey picks one of each by ear, and the app plays them in place of its own, keeping its own wherever a file is missing (EARMARKS A60). Until then nothing changes, and the app's own whistle and crowd play. Since pass NI V's vvv (pre-K 4's Q, V and Y; Claude's making, until ElevenLabs records it) is measured too, against speech science's reading of /v/ (weak, spread-out noise over strong voicing): at both paces the voice's low buzz is most of it (at least 60 percent under 500 cycles a second), a breath is there (at least 2 percent over 1,500), that breath is spread out and flat as fff's is (no 1,000 cycles of it from 2,000 to 8,000 hold over a quarter of it, read only where a breath is there), and it is quieter in the highs than zzz's hiss (its share over 4,000 under half of zzz's at the same pace), each bound its own message, each tripped alone at both paces by a copy of logic.mjs whose v is made with one thing changed (its buzz at 700 cycles a second, no breath, zzz's hiss for its breath, its breath nearly twice as loud), since round one's code read found one message holding five bounds and no plant tripping one alone; the fifth, a ceiling on the breath that nothing passing the other four could reach, was dropped. The voice check page's row 91 plays vvv, and since that read rows 85 to 90 the five blends and x, which had no row, and its self-test fails a sound the app makes with no row (soundsWithoutRows, a page without vvv's planted).

| **Checks learned in pass MU:** a sound nobody can hear where it is made is measured against recordings of the real thing, never only against its own last build (pass MT's crowd passed every measure it had and still sounded like static to Mikey; beside forty real recordings its energy centred near 2,600 cycles a second, where theirs centres near 1,700), and each fault a listener names becomes a measure and a plant from his words (a click: a release far louder and brighter than the vowel after it, after a silence); a light's guess that fits the words it was tuned on is run over every new word before the words ship (Q81's mail carrier and police officer, with tiny and meow said fast, left eight to thirteen of 691 sayings late until the guess counted letters); a patch that matches a sentence matches it once in the whole file, since a lesson's sentence can stand word for word in another lesson (Colors' "This color is red, like a strawberry." is in pre-K 3's Red and blue too, and the patch's count stopped it); a page whose device-voice rows say nothing in Mikey's phone preview while the app's own sounds play is the preview's voice, not the page (rows 45 to 50 and 67 to 72, as in pass MO), so the delivery message tells him to open the page in his phone's browser; and a long browser run is started detached with a log of its own, never piped through a command that waits for its end under a time limit (the screenshots were stopped at the limit with nothing logged); and from round one's three checks: a sound is weighed as the ear hears it, not by its plain level, which a vowel's low energy rules (the first fix's duh still stood about 9 dB over its uh in the click's band as heard, where a plain 3 to 8 dB bound passed it), and a crowd's start is listened for as well as its middle (its people's first claps bunched into a cue, then a second clap together); a citation is read again whenever a lesson's game changes what the child does (two said a child "is not asked to say" words the game now asks for); a table of numbers is credited to the paper it is printed in (the hand shapes are Jylhä and Erkut's, DAFx 2008, after Peltola and others, 2007); a guard that waits for an end counts from the start it waits on (play's promise), not from the call; a near miss is checked to trip its own condition alone, since one caught by another bound proves nothing about its own (the g plant was caught only by its peak); and a change to a shared guess is followed to every reader of it (the letter-counting guess changed the child's turn on pass MT's words too); and a near miss that plants on something live (an open question) plants its own when nothing is, so the check holds in every state (the docs sync test's two near misses on the question lists would have failed a pass that left no question open) | INDEPENDENT-CHECKS (the screen and sound, lesson and code briefs), NEW-CHAT (How a pass is delivered), this file | test (rules: the whistle's and the crowd's measures, the stops' and endings' releases, the guard, with plants; scripts: the light over every line on every say-along, and no say-along citation saying a child is not asked to say its words; docs sync: the question lists' near misses with no question open) + brief |
| **Checks learned in pass MM:** a pre-K bank is read on every seed it makes (doubling to 76,800) and again with each wrong choice as its key, so a derivation that would agree with any answer is caught (31 banks had none); a reason is read as the voice says it, a held sound with its carrier word (heardForm); a question's printed words are read against its choices, since a printed capital is a shape a pre-reader can match (printedQuestion); after a pre-reader's wrong tap the whole screen is read for any sign of the answer (its button had turned green before the retry); every dot of a tracing pad is looked at, and a long lesson's row of dots at 320 pixels; a browser test that swaps the page's voice puts the page's own back; a comparison reads every bank as far as the rules test does; a pre-K quote is read against the standard's published text, kept in docs/standards; a long story's paintings take its course's subject's style; an answer on its own line after narration is the narrator's, so a child who answers is named in the words; a sound a story asks a child to hear is a plain one (train and track begin with the blend tr); a game that asks for a name prints none, and closing it stops its voice; and from round three, a line is read through the speech the voice is handed, a word said slowly and all (speechPieces); what the screen prints after a wrong tap is read for the letters it shows; a check of a sign is proven on a copy where the sign never comes (the answer's green); a thing a child holds is followed through the whole story (the telephone's cup); and the comparison reads printed words and answer places; and from round four, a wait is checked at every place it starts (a round's start, a new board, the backstop) with a voice that keeps reporting it speaks and a tap in the page the moment the board can take one; a layout check runs in both themes, since the lines where the cue sat on a nearly whole Next were the dark theme's and a check written for the light alone passed the old code; a wrapped row is measured for its centre as well as its count; a tool that prints a field is read for a value it prints (the packet's example drawing); a comparison is built whole (since round five the tool's default); and the checks are run once from a folder with a space in its path; and from round five, a wait's check taps the moment the wait lifts as well as at once, so a wait too short for its ask fails; a threshold is pinned by a case on each side of it (the cue's third); a control is found by its exact name (the cue's label holds "next"); and every wait in a game is read for each way it can start (Hear it again) | INDEPENDENT-CHECKS (the story, picture, screen, lesson, game and code briefs), docs/MISTAKE-CLASSES.md, this file | test (logic, coverage, stories, ledger, click-through, question-screens) + tool (content-snapshot, content-diff) |
| **Which model a task runs on (Mikey, pass NC: "If you are able to delegate any simple tasks to Sonnet 5.5 to be more efficient with our usage, you can.   Only do it if it maintains your accuracy, we don't want to make mistakes by doing this.  You can also delegate an agent to Fable 5.1 if it improves quality of work even if efficiency isn't better.  Maybe like the over aching vision agent or agent responsible for writing creatively since Fable is good at that."; pass NF: "You may know this already but I would like to switch from Fable 5.1 to Fable 5.2.  We should always use the newest versions of each model.  Please continue" and "It should also act as the advisor moving forward and documentation should update to reflect this."; and pass NG: "If there is a better structure, when it comes to incorporating multiple models to our process and assigning agents, you can make those changes.  We just want to use each model for it's strengths (where it makes the most sense) with the idea of efficiency, accuracy and prestige in mind.  If Fable 5.2 is better than Opus at a task, for example, we can make sure the corresponding agent uses Fable instead of Opus." and "I think Fable has better research capabilities so it should also be used for the self-improvement research each week and for restructuring our flow if things can be improved."):** the model map, since pass NG (Claude's design within his words, from Anthropic's model guide and the Fable 5.2 benchmarks, read November 11, 2026, and the weekly flow review): every agent and check runs on the newest version of its model (Fable 5.2, Opus 5.5 and Sonnet 5.5 when pass NG wrote this). The lead, which builds the lessons, banks, code and records and spends most of a pass's tokens, runs on Opus, Anthropic's own default for most work, and raises its effort on a hard step before anything moves up a model, the order Anthropic's guide gives. Fable writes the stories and the Wonder questions; runs the weekly research sweep, the weekly flow review, evidence briefs and any research a pass needs, where it leads Opus on Anthropic's agentic-search benchmark (WANDR 82.1 against 72.3); and reads the lessons, the code, the alignment and the final record, where it leads on Anthropic's coding and reasoning benchmarks. Opus reads the screens, which drive a browser through hundreds of screenshots, the one read on the builder's own model (the lead builds on Opus), the Fable code read covering the screen code, and reads what Fable wrote, the stories and the Wonder questions, so there a different model reads than wrote (said so since round two's final read, pass NG, which found the screen reads on the builder's model where this row had said every read was on the other). Sonnet does chores whose result a script can check (a count, a regeneration, a scan with a fixed list), at low effort with a failure run again at high. Fable is the advisor (advisorModel "fable" in .claude/settings.json; a cloud chat's own with /advisor fable before its opening message, since a conversation keeps the advisor it began with until /clear or /compact), consulted about three times a pass, before the pass's design is fixed, when an error keeps coming back and before delivery, since each consult reads the whole conversation uncached and Anthropic's guide measured an Opus executor with a Fable 5.1 advisor at 1.7 points over Opus alone for about 2.1 times the cost; an agent doing a chore is told not to consult it, since an agent inherits the advisor; and where a session's advisor is not Fable (pass NG's chat began with Opus 5.5) a briefed Fable agent does the advisor's checks. Fable's share of each pass is read from the session's logs (tools/usage-by-model.mjs, each reply once, at API prices, since a plan does not publish how it counts) and given in DECISIONS: about 40 percent of a week was the aim, with no new Fable work past 45, and since pass NN, at Mikey's word ("You don't really need to keep Fable under 90, just under 100%."), Fable's work is planned so its own bar stays just under 100 percent, each pass's Fable work sized to end before the bar does, so a long pass never runs past Fable's limit midway (the sizing Claude's plan). Where a session keeps no log (passes NK to NP), Mikey's bars at a pass's start and end, each apart, are the measure (pass NM read Fable's own bar as half the week, so that 90 percent of it stood for the 45 percent, its reading, not his word, set aside by his words in pass NN). <!-- RULES-AUDIT rows as pass NQ left them, before pass NR corrected them under Q85, word for word: begin -->
- **My Classroom:** Add someone new comes first, then Who needs help once there are two students. The Active Students cards run youngest band first and then by name, and a press and hold drags one into another place. Inactive Students can be shown again or deleted. Below them the school turns Quick checks on or off and can Walk through as a student at any of the four levels, where every module is open, any question can be skipped and nothing is recorded (a Walkthrough Mode tab says so on every screen). Four cards open Wonder Questions, Reading Lists, Science Experiments and Practical Life Skills, and the page ends on Backup classroom, the Story Log and contact. A What's new pop-up lists each update's changes once.
- **Practice:** a round is five questions through grade 5, eight from grade 6 and ten from grade 9 (`moduleRules`), plus memory checks. A question is a choice, a tap in order, a typed number, a mark, shape, number or letter traced on a pad, or a writing checklist, a Writing lesson's round being one piece of writing. A pre-reader's question is spoken, its setup line above it where it has one, a tapped word says itself once, and a picture never does. A heard question prints none of the words it asks about while the voice can speak. Follow the directions puts a gold number on each picture tapped, takes it back on a second tap, wobbles a picture tapped out of turn and rings the next one after two missed orders. A tracing pad shows one line's green starting dot at a time, and from kindergarten up a line traced backward is cleared with "Start at the green dot and follow the arrow." Section 6 holds the banks' rules and what a question may print.
- **Wonder questions:** a reflection comes after every two different lessons worked, pass or fail, and after a hard round the failure and feelings questions come first (`nextWonder`, `WONDER_EVERY`, `MISS_STREAK`). No question is met more than twice. A reader types or picks an answer that is never stored, then reads the four voices and a closing card. A pre-reader hears the question, hears each choice named as it lights, taps one, and then hears all four voices on cards of their own with the person's portrait, and a closing card under a rotating title (`wonderPreReaderCards`). An answer that names a color shows its square. There are <!-- n:wonder -->414<!-- /n --> questions, <!-- n:wonderEarly -->144<!-- /n --> of them early, the early pool kept ahead of its lessons. The twenty early ones added since pass NF ask about failure (a letter written backward, a puzzle piece that will not fit, a wrong guess, a kite that will not fly, a baby learning to walk, a wrong name, two pages turned at once), feelings (the giggles, a party that is too much, winning while a friend loses, feeling bored, happy tears, the same story again), the world (the moon that seems to follow, tickling yourself, wrinkly fingers, a face upside down in a spoon) and hard days (the day after a big day, a sad part of a story, a new way home). Sections 7 and 8 hold the rules.
- **What pre-K teaches:** pre-K 3 has two courses, Very First Steps and Listen and Point, and pre-K 4 has First Steps and First Sounds and Letters. Together they teach colors, shapes, sizes, matching, counting, feelings and getting along. They teach learning words (Learning words, More learning words), following directions, a voice to fit the place and telling in sentences. For the ear they teach counting a sentence's words, beats, rhymes, first parts and single sounds, then the sound of every letter, and twenty-two big letters and fifteen small ones by name (section 5). For print they teach drawing and writing to tell, spaces, end marks and a name's letters in order, every question a tap, with nothing a child writes asked for or kept. For stories they teach questions about a story read aloud, pictures and print, predictions, retelling, acting out and how a book is held and read, three of these lessons built at Mikey's answer to Q108, "Three lessons, about 15" (pass NM). Each pre-K lesson names the lesson its ideas build on, or none (his "Q84 - B"), and only an educator's hand unlock or erase can lock one that was open (Q87). Every hiss in the phoneme and letters lessons is one breath (his "Q88 - A" and "Q89 - A"), and three letters lessons show a second word where a letter or a sound had one (EARMARKS A104). Section 5's rows named read hold each lesson, section 6 its questions, and section 7 what a lesson requires and what a split lesson changes.
- **The educator's report:** notes kept with the student, restored with a backup and searched from Who needs help, a Summary in sentences (what is mastered in each course, confidence, the reflections answered, play and reading), a Weekly note to edit and print, the courses assigned now with a way back to the recommended set (ticking the human sexuality elective first asks the educator to confirm a parent's written consent, Texas Education Code §28.004), the other courses folded by grade under Core or Electives, progress by course with Unlock it anyway, writing to check, the Transcript, the Story log, the Standards map, Certificates, the raw data, a reset and Print. Who needs help orders the class by need and prints every student's weekly note on one sheet, and an Action Item marks a student who is stuck or has writing to check (sections 7, 7a and 8).
